#10296. The Design Workflow Is Broken. Here Is How Vitalii Khomenko Thinks It Should Be Fixed

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

With 250+ client engagements across both agency and embedded in-house roles, Vitalii Khomenko has seen product teams from the inside in a way few designers have. Here he makes the case for a new kind of design workflow built for the AI era, and explains why the window to adapt is narrower than most founders think.

For the last decade, every startup conversation about speed eventually became a conversation about engineering. Hiring more developers, cutting scope, choosing a simpler stack, it was almost always a dev-side problem. Vitalii Khomenko, Principal Designer at Orizon Design and founder of Fluente LLC, argues that's no longer true. And he has a specific view on what teams should do instead.

What makes Khomenko's perspective worth taking seriously isn't just seniority. It's the specific shape of his experience. Over a career spanning 250+ client engagements, he has worked both as an external specialist brought in to deliver and as an embedded designer who became the single connective thread between product, brand, and execution. That dual vantage point is rare. Most designers live on one side of that line. Khomenko has operated extensively on both, which gives him an unusually clear read on where friction actually originates inside product teams, and where it doesn't.

His work spans early-stage startups finding their first visual language and established brands managing complex, multi-disciplinary design systems. Across that range, certain patterns repeat. And the pattern that started becoming impossible to ignore this year pointed him toward a conclusion that is reshaping how he thinks a modern design workflow should actually be structured.

The bottleneck didn't disappear. It moved. And the workflow needs to move with it.

When shipping code was slow, the existing design workflow made sense. Multiple rounds of revision, user testing, iteration, all of that fit naturally into the window while engineering caught up. That window is now gone, and most design workflows were never updated to reflect it.

"Founders are asking design teams to move at the same speed as an AI-assisted dev pipeline, and design doesn't compress the same way code does," Khomenko says. The result is a workflow that was built around a constraint that no longer exists, applied to a pipeline that has fundamentally changed around it.

The forced choice between polish and speed that product teams are now navigating isn't new as a concept. What's new is how stark it has become, and how directly it traces back to a workflow problem rather than a people problem. "Because development is cheap and fast now, there's constant pressure to ship the next thing immediately," he explains. "But the products that actually retain users are still the ones where the details were considered carefully. Fewer revision rounds isn't a process improvement if it means shipping avoidable friction."

Khomenko has watched this play out across enough teams, at enough stages, to be specific about where the existing workflow breaks down. The issue isn't design judgment. It's where that judgment sits in the process, and what format it's expected to produce at the end.

To understand Khomenko's proposed fix, it helps to understand exactly where the current workflow stalls. For most product teams, the end of the design process looks like this: a designer produces polished, dev-ready screens inside Figma, and hands that file to engineering. Engineering then interprets those screens, translates the visual decisions into code, and builds from there.

That translation step, Figma file to working code, is where the pipeline now breaks down. AI-assisted engineering has made the building part dramatically faster. But the translation step hasn't changed. An engineer still has to read a Figma file, interpret spacing, extract assets, make judgment calls about component structure, and write the implementation from scratch. That process takes the same amount of time it always did.

"The manual workflow inside Figma simply isn't keeping up with AI-boosted engineering," Khomenko says. "The design handoff is quietly becoming the thing everyone is waiting on."

The result is a pipeline where everything around the handoff has accelerated, and the handoff itself has become the single point of drag. Figma, in other words, was built for a world where engineering was the slow part. It wasn't designed to feed a pipeline where engineering moves at AI speed.

This is the core of what Khomenko is proposing, and it's a more significant structural shift than it might first appear.

In his workflow, Figma doesn't disappear. It moves. Rather than serving as both the exploration environment and the final delivery artifact, Figma becomes the visual exploration layer exclusively: the place where brand identity is established, layout logic is worked through, and design direction is validated. It remains the right tool for figuring out what something should look and feel like.

But the final delivery, the artifact that actually goes to engineering, is code. Specifically, components built into prepared libraries that engineering can work from and scale directly, without any translation step in between.

The difference in implementation speed is the point. When a designer delivers a coded component library rather than a Figma file, engineering doesn't need to interpret anything. The decisions are already expressed in the language they're building in. What previously required an engineer to read, interpret, and re-create from a visual reference now arrives ready to use. In a pipeline running on AI-assisted development, that difference compounds fast.

"Figma is still the right place to explore what something should look and feel like," Khomenko says. "But handing over a Figma file and asking engineering to implement it is now the slow path. Delivering in code is the fast path. That's the shift."

Executing this workflow requires a different kind of designer, and Khomenko is direct about that. The UI/UX designer becoming a Design Engineer is, in his assessment, not a distant possibility. It is a matter of time, and the teams treating it as a future consideration rather than a present reality are the ones most likely to find themselves stuck at the handoff.

But Khomenko is equally clear about what this shift doesn't mean. Delivering in code is one part of the role, not the whole of it. The full design cycle isn't going anywhere. Applying branding to imagery, producing animations, building marketing materials, these remain essential creative tasks that require a broad and varied toolkit well beyond code. "The designer of the near future isn't just generating code," he says. "They're moving across the full cycle, brand, motion, marketing, product, with design engineering as an important and growing part of that stack, not a replacement for it."

What changes is that code-ready delivery becomes a core expectation within that stack. The designer who can move fluidly from visual exploration in Figma to component delivery in code, while also carrying the brand thinking, motion, and marketing creativity that differentiates products at the surface level users actually experience, is the profile that the new workflow demands.

Khomenko is candid that no single version of this workflow has fully standardized yet. Every team is figuring it out differently. The tools are evolving, the handoff expectations are shifting, and the processes teams are building around all of it vary considerably. There is no off-the-shelf playbook.

But this is precisely where his 250+ engagements become more than a credential. They're a dataset. Having watched enough teams, at enough stages, through enough cycles of tooling change, Khomenko can see through the noise of individual variation and identify what's directionally true even when the specifics are still in flux. "Each team's workflow is really custom at this stage," he says. "But some general patterns of how this is going to look in the near future are already visible."

Those patterns consistently point in the same direction: design judgment moving earlier, final delivery moving into code, and the designer's role expanding in technical range while staying broad in creative scope. The exact tools and sequences will keep shifting. The underlying logic of the workflow Khomenko is describing is already taking shape in the teams paying closest attention.

For founders and product leads, the workflow Khomenko is describing has direct implications for how teams are staffed, budgeted, and structured.

Front-load design judgment. The biggest leverage in the new workflow isn't faster execution, it's better problem definition before generation starts. Bring senior design thinking in earlier, when it can shape what gets built rather than polish what's already done.

Separate exploration from delivery. Figma and the visual exploration phase serve a distinct purpose from the final delivery layer. Treat them as separate stages with separate expectations, rather than collapsing them into a single step expected to produce everything.

Shift the delivery expectation to code. Whether that means hiring for Design Engineer range, developing it internally, or restructuring handoffs, the end of the design pipeline needs to arrive in a form engineering can work from directly. The translation step is where time goes to die, and it's now the most avoidable part of the process.

Protect the full creative stack. Design engineering matters more than it ever has. It is not, however, the whole job. Teams that reduce design to code generation will lose the brand thinking, motion, and marketing creativity that differentiates products at the surface level users actually experience.

Stay flexible. The workflow is still forming. Teams that build rigidity into their process now will find themselves redoing that work within a year. The teams getting this right are the ones building enough adaptability into their structures to absorb what's still coming.

"The Figma handoff made sense when engineering was the slow part," Khomenko says. "Engineering isn't the slow part anymore. Delivering in code isn't the future of design workflow. For the teams paying attention, it's already the present."

I write about AI and software development from the angle that usually gets skipped: what a spec sheet or benchmark actually implies once you try to build with it. Find me on LinkedIn.

#10295. The price of your Android XR glasses can quickly skyrocket with add-ons

Google’s first collection of Intelligent Eyewear has arrived and, oh boy, the prices are not cheap. While this first batch starts at $395, you’ll be paying a fair bit more for a pair of Android XR glasses if you need to add anything on. Here’s a quick overview.

As we detailed earlier today, Android XR glasses from Warby Parker start at $395, with Gentle Monster’s collection starting at $449. And you’ll get a stellar pair for those prices, but add-ons will cost you.

For Warby Parker, we’ll focus on the “Reid” model, the rectangular lens shape. Like the rest of the catalog, these start at $395 with non-prescription lenses, either clear or tinted. It costs at least $100 to add prescription lenses, with other add-ons such as transition lenses or polarized lenses adding even further to the cost.

Here’s a breakdown of the potential add-ons for Warby Parker’s Android XR glasses:

Notably, there’s a further upcharge for adding prescription lenses to sunglasses versus standard clear glasses.

Just to get a pair of standard, non-prescription glasses with the addition of transition lenses, you’re looking at $520, and it can add up quickly in other configurations. One configuration we were able to build with progressive, polarized lenses with the “thinner lenses” and anti-reflective upgrades came out to $1,080.

Gentle Monster has a higher starting price for its Android XR glasses, but add-ons seem to be a bit more affordable based on what we’re seeing.

For this one, we’re looking at the GGS001 01 style which starts at $449.

Notably, Gentle Monster walks customers through potential insurance benefits even if they’re not buying prescription glasses.

#10290. Nothing’s screen-less new gadget really looks like yet another AI recorder

Nothing has been teasing its first dedicated AI product and, as we expected, it really looks like an AI recorder.

Last week, Nothing CEO Carl Pei announced that the company would be launching its first “AI-native” product that doesn’t have a screen and “couldn’t have been an app.” Now, Pei is back with a brief teaser for the product, captioned “Context doesn’t live in a chat box.”

Nothing is keeping the details of what this product is a secret for now, but it’s becoming painfully obvious that this is an AI recorder.

The green and red lights paired with what looks like a record button paint a very clear picture. As we pointed out initially, Pei’s first post heavily implied this was some type of audio device. He said:

Meanwhile, the context that matters most never reaches the model at all. Think about the last time someone said something worth keeping: an idea over coffee, a throwaway comment that unblocks your whole week. What did you do? “Hold on, let me write that down.” Out comes the phone, and by the time you look up, the moment has moved on without you. Or you don’t bother, and it’s gone by the end of the day.

There are many AI recorders already on the market, admittedly with varying levels of quality, so we’ll have to wait and see how Nothing’s differentiates itself.

#10289. Nvidia announces Rubin HPC GPU with 'record number of CUDA cores' at Supercomputing 2026

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Rubin HPC puts "over 50,000" CUDA cores on a chip, implies four-die layout

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Supercomputing 2026 conference is under way, and despite continuing turmoil elsewhere in the company, Nvidia has lots to reveal at the show. Foremost among its announcements today, Nvidia took the wraps off a new member of the Rubin GPU family called Rubin HPC, a chip that the company says is meant to accelerate agentic workflows and simulation for scientists, engineers, and analysts working with large data sets.

To accelerate those most demanding workloads, Nvidia says Rubin HPC has the highest number of CUDA cores on any of its GPUs - "more than 50,000" - which might imply that this chip uses a configuration similar to the four-die Rubin Ultra GPU, which links those dies together using NVLink-C2C.

Today's Rubin GPU has 224 SMs and 28,672 CUDA cores, so scaling compute resources to the degree Rubin HPC does would necessarily require more dies on a package. Nvidia didn't reveal more details of the chip's physical layout alongside the announcement. (The representative image shared by the company wouldn't appear to take this into account.)

To keep data moving between Rubin HPC GPUs, Nvidia will use its NVLink 7 interconnect. The company didn't share any new details of the bandwidth or physical layer of this interconnect within the rack, but past disclosures about the tech have indicated that it retains the 3.6 TB/s of per-GPU bandwidth that NVLink 6 delivers while enlarging the size of its scale-up domain.

Nvidia didn't share any details of the Rubin HPC GPU's HBM capacity or bandwidth, and it also didn't share whether this part includes a rebalancing of compute resources towards native FP64, as AMD and Intel are touting at the conference with their own GPUs this week.

Nvidia instead touts the suitability of this chip for scientists and engineers using agentic workflows, for which the company expects faster simulation performance and therefore faster opportunities for discovery in those fields thanks to better agent performance in those domains.

In fields like climate research, genomic analysis, and financial simulation, Nvidia says the Rubin HPC GPU's "balanced design," with both higher compute resources and (presumably) faster data movement between nodes, will help feed the chip with the massive data sets typical of those workloads, minimizing idle compute resources and achieving better utilization.

Researchers, engineers, and analysts marshaling agent swarms to explore the problem spaces in their domains will also be able to take advantage of Nvidia's CUDA-X domain-specific libraries to boost performance on Rubin HPC, tightening the reasoning loop from hypothesis to simulation to refinement and hastening the pursuit of the most promising results.

Nvidia says Rubin HPC will arrive sometime in 2028.

As the Senior Analyst, Graphics at Tom's Hardware, Jeff Kampman covers everything that has to do with graphics cards, gaming performance, and more. From integrated graphics processors to discrete graphics cards to the hyperscale installations powering our AI future, if it's got a GPU in it, Jeff is on it.

#10287. 7 Best Accounts Receivable (AR) Software Platforms for Automating Invoice-to-Cash

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The best accounts receivable software for your company is the layer that closes the stage where your cash leaks. If buyers settle from an emailed link and outreach starts late, a reminders tool covers most of it. If customers prefer to pay online by card or ACH, add a payments network with a customer portal. If clients require invoice uploads to Coupa or Ariba and clear multiple invoices with one blank-memo wire, you need an AR automation layer on top of the ERP. Every platform listed here serves B2B businesses invoicing other companies, excluding consumer payment apps and collection agencies.

Our source material is each vendor's public product, integration, security and pricing pages, plus G2 reviews, as they stood in November 2026.

About three in five US suppliers let business clients pay within 30 days, per Atradius's 2026 US payment practices survey (September 2026). Most overdue invoices settle within a month past due, so funds arrive roughly two months post-billing. The same survey notes US enterprises often lose 1% to 2% of B2B invoices to bad debt. Software that shortens this window performs four main functions:

What invoice-to-cash software does not handle is core billing operations. Billing schedules, revenue recognition, and GL accounting remain within the ERP. Contract-first platforms initiate workflows from signed agreements and map customer billing prior to collection efforts; invoice-first applications pick up after ERP invoice generation.

Most finance stacks have four layers: the ERP that creates the invoice and holds the books, a payments network, a reminders tool, and an AR automation layer working the steps in between.

The ERP covers the last row, and a reminders tool covers timing. The rows in between depend on reading what a customer or a portal sent, which is AR automation work. That share keeps growing. Paper checks fell to 26% of B2B payments in 2025, per the AFP 2025 Digital Payments Survey as reported by Nacha, and FedNow settled about 2.5 times as many payments in Q3 2026 as a year earlier, per Federal Reserve FedNow statistics. Heading into 2027, more money arrives over electronic rails while its remittance detail lands somewhere else.

Layer: AI-native AR automation, for Series A to Fortune 500 finance teams

Published pricing: Pricing scales with business volume and isn't a percentage of collections

Leak it closes: Unapplied cash first, then stalled replies and portal rejections.

Layer: AR automation with built-in payments, sold as Invoiced by Flywire

Leak it closes: Cash application, where CashMatch AI handles wires that pay several invoices at once and payments that arrive without remittance.

Leak it closes: Invoice delivery into buyer portals, through 260+ AP portal integrations run from one interface.

Layer: AR automation layer with AI agents, on the ERP

Leak it closes: Customer replies that carry a promise-to-pay date or a dispute.

Layer: Payments network with customer portal

Leak it closes: Payment acceptance, through a customer portal for paying and disputing invoices online.

Layer: Reminders tool with AI reply drafts, on Xero and QuickBooks

Published pricing: Essentials $69 a month; Pro $99 to $799 a month; Custom from $999 a month

Leak it closes: Replies that sit unanswered at small and midsize companies.

Published pricing: US $259, $779 or $1,169 a month by annual revenue band; annual billing saves 10%

Leak it closes: Follow-up timing, with reminders sent from your own email address on a schedule that escalates.

On Xero or QuickBooks with customers paying from the emailed link, the leak tends to be timing, and a reminders tool covers it. Once W-9 requests and payment questions fill your team's week, the replies need a different layer.

Portal rejections and lump-sum wires put the leak in the table's middle rows, where an AR automation layer on the ERP fits. In a demo, we'd ask each vendor to show how fast its software flagged a rejected portal invoice, then to apply a multi-invoice wire with a blank memo live.

If your team still keys card numbers or chases ACH details, start with the payments layer. A payments network with a customer portal gives buyers stored methods and autopay. Unless the vendor says otherwise, it won't read replies or file into buyer portals, so plan an AR layer for those stages.

Whichever of the best accounts receivable software platforms you pick, it sits on top of the ERP. Four steps keep the books intact:

#10281. Fast, reliable A2UI, with swappable inference formats

We've heard your concerns about the speed of A2UI and generative UI experiences: people wait while the model generates each screen. This release gives you levers to make that faster, so you can choose the trade-off that fits each screen.

With this release, we’re happy to introduce swappable inference formats and share examples on leveraging decision models with A2UI, which help building faster and more reliable generative UI experiences. Using our new A2UI Express as an inference format you can reduce latency by up to 3x, and up-to 80x using decision models like Jev to make layout decisions. Express requires up to 3x fewer tokens to generate the same kinds of UI compared to Direct JSON. We'll dive into the details in this blog post.

A2UI is an agent-optimized protocol for generative UI, where a client advertises a catalog of trusted components as a contract, an agent sends A2UI messages describing a bespoke user interface composed from that catalog, and the client's renderer draws the UI (see Introducing A2UI).

Initially agents generated A2UI JSON transport messages directly, every curly brace and nested element. It turns out that generating JSON trees constrained by a complex JSON schema is slow and error-prone for LLMs. This coupled A2UI to LLMs, which output text, and made it difficult to integrate faster System One decision models.

Now agents can generate A2UI Express, an intermediate format optimized for inference speed and accuracy, and rely on deterministic code in A2UI's Agent SDKs to translate it into the JSON-based transport protocol. Agent builders are also free to swap in any other DSL and its associated compiler for any special use-cases, and our A2UI Agent SDK(s) make it compatible with transports and renders.

The format the model generates is what we call inference_format. You set it with one argument when you create the generator. The SDK generates the prompt instructions for that format and compiles the model's reply into standard A2UI messages. This post covers how the swap works, why we developed A2UI Express as the new default inference format, and how a decision model can build screens from layouts you define in advance.

An inference format has two parts, and the SDK generates both from your catalog's JSON schema. A prompt section teaches the model the format's syntax, and a compiler compiles the model's reply into validated A2UI messages. Because both come from the schema, a format can't describe a component or property that your catalog lacks.

You choose the format with one argument when you create the generator:

A2UI Express is available in the Python agent SDK (a2ui-agent-sdk 0.9.0 on PyPI), in the TypeScript agent SDK (@a2ui/agent on npm), and in the Dart SDK (a2ui_agent 0.0.1-wip004 on pub.dev). In TypeScript, you pass new ExpressFormatFactory() as the generator's third argument. The Python and TypeScript SDKs can both stream Direct JSON chunk by chunk, and they compile Express from the complete reply.

Express writes one statement per line, in a style close to Python. Each line assigns a component to a variable, and the variable name becomes the component ID. Arguments are positional: the compiler maps them to property names in the order your catalog schema declares them, and _ skips one. $/title binds a value to the data model, and Event("accept") declares a server action. This is the A2UI gallery's notification card in Express:

Swappable Inference formats including A2UI Express also work with macros in the Python agent SDK, which help when a screen shows data your systems already hold. The model writes a call with only an ID, such as OrderStatus("ord_42"), and your macro fetches the order and fills in the values, so every value on screen comes straight from your data. The macros README shows the setup.

Customer feedback uncovered latency and accuracy issues with direct JSON generation. To fix this, we started devising a new inference format. We experimented with four options:

We ran all four formats through the same 51-task eval on gemini-3.5-flash and compared them for thinking modes: on and off.

A2UI Express follows python like syntax and was inspired in part by OpenUI Lang, the language of the open-source OpenUI project, which also writes each component as a name = Component(...) statement with positional arguments. OpenUI Lang adds its own built-in functions and state, and the two languages aren’t yet interchangeable. The OpenUI team has proposed working toward a shared syntax in #3214. Comment if you're interested.

Based on our tests; we observed that Express inference format works just as well even with thinking off; which can give you the fastest inference times when working with full expressivity of A2UI.

Some screens have a known shape, such as a settings page, a checkout or a confirmation. For those, the model doesn't need to design a layout. It needs to choose among layouts you have already defined, and a different kind of model is built for that. For this, you would not need the full expressivity provided by A2UI Express.

Jev, from TypeSafe, is what is called a System One model, built to make fast, structured decisions that code can use directly. Jev doesn't generate text or JSON. You send it a state and a set of typed questions, and it answers each one with calibrated probabilities. A question can ask whether a statement is true (Noul), which option from a list fits (Choice), or how something scores on a rubric (Score). Jev answers many questions in one request, in roughly 100 to 300 ms.

Apparite(jev2ui), an open-source design tool, shows one way to connect a System One model to A2UI. Jev decides the structure of each screen, code builds the A2UI components from those decisions, and a small model, gemini-3.5-flash-lite, writes only the text. The questions live in a Markdown grammar written ahead of time. A blockquote asks a question, and the bullets are its options, each with criteria. This block from grammar/screen.md decides which control a settings row gets:

On a podcast app's settings screen, Jev answers Skip forward → value, Auto-download → switch and Log out → danger, each at p ≈ 1.00. Apparite builds a whole screen in three steps:

In one run of 11 prompts, the component tree was on screen in 92 to 298 ms and each screen was complete in 0.8 to 1.7 s. The text writer generated 101 to 341 tokens per screen, and both of Apparite's Jev pipelines produced valid A2UI on all 11 prompts. The project treats these numbers as a first look, since the sample is small.

Controlled generation can only produce the layouts its grammar describes. Apparite's grammar covers nine screen archetypes and twelve blocks, so screens without a known shape are a better fit for Express. A2UI Agent SDKs, don’t include a decision model inference format yet; apparite(jev2ui) project is an example on how A2UI messages could be assembled given a batch of independent decisions from a decision model.

A2UI Express is fast and keeps the full expressivity of A2UI, but a decision model making multiple independent decisions is still faster. Choose by how much of the screen you can define ahead of time:

Use Express in your agent. Install the Python SDK with pip install a2ui-agent-sdk or the TypeScript SDK with npm install @a2ui/agent, pass ExpressFormatFactory() when you create the generator, and run the model with thinking off. Your catalog and renderers stay as they are, and the Express specification documents the syntax.

To teach Gemini, or any other model that loads skills, to write Express with only your components, generate skills from your catalog with the Python SDK's SkillGenerator:

Try controlled generation. Clone the example jev2ui repository, and follow the instructions there to get started. A hosted version of Apparite(jev2ui) runs at apparite.cnfg.ai.

Earlier A2UI posts: Introducing A2UI (December 2025), A2UI v0.9 (April 2026) and A2UI + MCP Apps (June 2026).

#10275. Best Ecommerce Platforms for Developers: Six Options and the Layers Each Leaves to You

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Picking an ecommerce platform is really a decision about which layers you want to own: the storefront, the catalog and order logic, the payment flow, and the hosting underneath. Every platform below draws that line in a different place. If you have already settled the front-end question with a comparison like SitePoint's Astro vs Next.js guide, the next question is what sits behind it. Prices are from each vendor's pricing page as of November 2026

Shopify is the fully managed option. Basic costs $29 a month with card rates from 2.9% plus 30 cents, and a 2% fee applies to orders processed through a third-party gateway on that plan. Developers get two routes: Liquid themes, or Hydrogen, Shopify's headless toolkit, where your framework owns routing and rendering and Hydrogen supplies the Storefront API client and cart. Templates exist for React Router on Oxygen and for Next.js on Vercel. Plus starts at $2,300 a month.

Whop is a business platform for taking payments and sending payouts, and it sits under a storefront you have already built rather than replacing it. The Checkout element runs in the browser with no server code and no API key on the page. You pass your own order ID as metadata, Whop copies it onto the payment, and your server fulfills from a signed webhook that follows the Standard Webhooks spec. Domestic cards cost 2.7% plus 30 cents with no monthly fee, buyers see payment methods chosen by their location across 195 countries, and the same balance pays out in over 200 countries, including to affiliates or sellers you onboard. Whop's own guide shows how to integrate a checkout API into a Next.js storefront end to end. The trade-off is that there is no catalog or shipping engine; you collect address fields at checkout and trigger fulfillment from the webhook.

WooCommerce is a free plugin on WordPress you host yourself, so you own updates, security, and performance. Its payment gateway, WooPayments, charges US merchants 2.9% plus 30 cents per card transaction, 1.5% more for international cards, 2% for currency conversion, and $25 per dispute. Everything is PHP hooks and filters, which makes it the natural pick for agencies already living in WordPress and the wrong pick for anyone who does not want to run a server.

BigCommerce hosts the back end and lets you bring any front end. Core is $29 a month billed annually, capped at $30,000 in trailing annual sales, and plans auto-upgrade as volume grows. There are no platform fees when you use an embedded payment provider, and a 2% fee on Core if you use another one. Catalyst, its reference storefront, is built on Next.js, React Server Components, and the Storefront GraphQL API, and deploys to Vercel.

Medusa is an open-source commerce framework in Node.js and TypeScript, with an MIT-licensed core and a commercial enterprise edition. SitePoint covered it when it was still a young Shopify alternative; it now has over 37,000 GitHub stars. Self-hosting is free. Medusa Cloud starts at $29 a month for development, $99 for launch, and $299 for scale, with no percentage of sales, and the company says you can export your data and self-host at any point.

Saleor is a GraphQL-native, API-only commerce platform written in Python and released under the BSD-3 license. Self-hosting is free. Saleor Cloud starts at $1,599 a month for up to $200,000 in monthly sales, with 0.8% on orders above that, and $3,999 a month at the next tier. It is built for teams with a dedicated engineering function and a large catalog, which is exactly who should pay that price.

Own only the storefront, and Shopify or BigCommerce will carry the rest. Own everything, and Medusa or Saleor give you a back end you can read. Already on WordPress, and WooCommerce is the obvious fit. Already have a storefront in Next.js or Astro and only need the money to move, and Whop is the shortest path. Whichever you pick, the checkout is where disputes start, so log what SitePoint's friendly fraud guide for developers recommends from day one.

#10273. AMD grabs over 40% of new TOP500 list for supercomputers, up 14% since last year

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Lux is the first of two machines AMD is developing as part of a $1 billion deal with the DOE.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

With the coming of SC26, also comes a fresh edition of the TOP500 list, which ranks the world's most powerful supercomputers. The newly released list illustrates that AMD powers 40% of the list, up 14% compared to 2025. AMD-powered systems now tally up to 202 on the list, up from 177 last year. New to the list is Lux, a “SI + HPC supercomputer” developed by AMD and the U.S. Department of Energy (DOE), which debuted at position 14.

AMD’s representation in the top 10 is familiar. After debuting in June, China’s LineShine remains in the top slot. El Capitan, powered by AMD chips, is in the second slot, followed by Frontier in third, HPC7 in sixth, and HPC6 in eighth, the same as the most recent TOP500 list.

Lux was announced a little over a year ago in a $1 billion deal between the DOE and AMD to develop two supercomputers. This is the first of the two, and the first time it’s been on the TOP500. The system was built by HPE and developed by AMD and DOE as part of the U.S. government’s Genesis Mission. The machine is housed in Oak Ridge National Laboratory’s Oak Ridge Leadership Computing Facility (OLCF), alongside machines like Frontier.

Lux relies on over 4,000 Instinct MI355X GPUs separated into over 500 nodes. For HPC workloads using FP64, the machine is capable of 317 PetaFLOPS. For AI training, the machine peaks at 20 ExaFLOPS of FP8, and for inference, 40 ExaFLOPS of FP4. With 288 GB of HBM3E per GPU, the entire system has access to 1.1 PB.

The machine uses AMD EPYC host CPUs — we weren’t able to find details on the specific generation— and AMD’s Pensando networking, with nodes built inside HPE’s ProLiant Compute XD685.

LingKun, LX2 304C 1.55GHz, LingQi, Kylin OS

HPE Cray EX255a, AMD 4th Gen EPYC 24C 1.8GHz, AMD Instinct MI300A, Slingshot-11, TOSS

HPE Cray EX235a, AMD Optimized 3rd Generation EPYC 64C 2GHz, AMD Instinct MI250X, Slingshot-11, HPE Cray OS

HPE Cray EX - Intel Exascale Compute Blade, Xeon CPU Max 9470 52C 2.4GHz, Intel Data Center GPU Max, Slingshot-11

BullSequana XH3000, GH Superchip 72C 3GHz, NVIDIA GH200 Superchip, Quad-Rail NVIDIA InfiniBand NDR200, RedHat Enterprise Linux

HPE Cray EX255a, AMD 4th Gen EPYC 24C 1.8GHz, AMD Instinct MI300A, Slingshot-11, RHEL 9

Microsoft NDv5, Xeon Platinum 8480C 48C 2GHz, NVIDIA H100, NVIDIA Infiniband NDR

HPE Cray EX235a, AMD Optimized 3rd Generation EPYC 64C 2GHz, AMD Instinct MI250X, Slingshot-11, RHEL 8.9

Supercomputer Fugaku, A64FX 48C 2.2GHz, Tofu interconnect D

HPE Cray EX254n, NVIDIA Grace 72C 3.1GHz, NVIDIA GH200 Superchip, Slingshot-11, HPE Cray OS

The machine has 3.5 million node-hours (one node running for one hour) per year, half of which are reserved for Genesis Mission research. Genesis Mission was established through an executive order in November 2025 with the stated goal of creating a “dedicated, coordinated national effort to unleash a new age of AI‑accelerated innovation and discovery that can solve the most challenging problems of this century.”

More specifically, the order lays out training scientific foundation models on Federal datasets, and creating “AI agents to test new hypotheses, automate research workflows, and accelerate scientific breakthroughs.” Lux is the first dedicated supercomputer for the Genesis Mission, but it joins existing machines that have been at least partially dedicated to research, including the Intel-powered Aurora at Argonne National Laboratory, which arrived fourth in the most recent TOP500.

Lux is built primarily for AI research, not as a successor to Frontier. Discovery is on track to debut as a next-gen flagship exascale supercomputer in 2028, also developed jointly by the DOE, AMD, and HPE.

#10271. I’d rather use Android XR glasses over Meta Ray-Bans for the app alone

In trying out Google’s Intelligent Eyewear Android XR glasses for the first time, one of my main takeaways is that it’s the app, not necessarily the glasses themselves that would push me into using them over the mainstay Meta Ray-Bans.

One of the inherent advantages that Android XR glasses were always going to have over third-party options is native integration. It’s something we see frequently, and perhaps most well-known on smartwatches.

The “Glasses” app on Android/Pixel, iOS, and Samsung Galaxy devices is dead simple. While we can’t show you the full app just yet as the one we were able to try was not completely final, it’s a very simple experience.

The app opens to an overview of your glasses to show their connection and battery status, with settings below for Gemini, apps, the camera, and more. You’ll also be able to see things such as what the various lights on the glasses mean, tutorials, and more. There’s no bloat and no messing around – it’s a utility to just make adjustments or control the glasses as needed.

You’ll probably be using this app mostly to view the photos and videos captured on Android XR glasses. Google has this set up to auto-import photos in nearly real-time. It only takes about 4 seconds to import and the process starts immediately for photos. Videos, on the other hand, take longer and may need to be manually imported (for the sake of battery in part, Google mentioned).

Across the three main platforms, there are some differences. The iPhone app doesn’t import photos quite as fast, and Samsung has its own app to fit in with One UI’s design language for Galaxy users, but the core premise is the same across all three.

While we’ll have to see how this works in real life, I was blown away by how simple it all was, though that might be because the bar is below the floor.

Meta’s Ray-Bans (and newer Meta Glasses) dominate the smart glasses conversation, and I think that the app is one of the biggest problems. It’s certainly one of the biggest reasons I’ve barely touched my Wayfarers in recent months. That’s because, in early 2025, Meta replaced its dedicated “View” app for its glasses with a Meta AI app, which is just invasive and distasteful in my book. Meta is no stranger to shoving AI down the throat of its existing users. Another well-known move was converting Facebook and Instagram search into AI search boxes with creepy levels of context and invasive actions – it makes Google’s AI Overviews look tame by comparison. But I’d argue the most aggressive was this move with its glasses. Something as simple as tweaking a setting or importing a photo now forces you to dodge an AI chatbot.

Truly, Meta’s app is the worst, and I applaud Google for building a very simple utility for Android XR glasses instead.

#10268. This Quest 3 golf club actually improved my scores, but it comes at a cost

The AMVR GA2 is a fresh take on a Quest 3 golf club accessory, and I was pleasantly surprised with what it did for my scores.

In the past decade of covering VR devices and accessories, I’ve used a lot of golf clubs. Short clubs. Long clubs. Adjustable length clubs. Clubs with realistic weight. Even a club with a magnet inside that makes a convincing “thwack!” sound every time you swing it. But the AMVR GA2’s inclusion of a proper club head at the bottom does something fascinating for its physics, and I immediately noticed the difference at the end of the first course I played with this Quest 3 golf club.

Most VR golf club accessories, including the first-gen AMVR GA1, stick the controller at the bottom of the shaft where the head usually goes. This makes sense since you don’t hit a real ball with a VR golf club accessory; it’s all virtual. But, ironically, putting a proper golf club head on the end (and the controller in the middle of the shaft) apparently does make a difference. This one works with both the Meta Quest 3 and Quest 3S, but not the older Meta Quest 2 or Quest Pro.

I’m not going to delve into the physics of why this works, but it’s likely that shifting the fulcrum point improves it. I didn’t expect this at all. When AMVR told me about their new club, I accepted because the company typically makes top-notch accessories. I really didn’t need another club in my extensive Quest 3 golf club collection. But I’m glad I did, because I’ve done much better than usual in my weekly Walkabout Mini Golf rounds with friends since I got it.

The mechanism for securing the controller onto the club is identical to previous AMVR controllers; only the location of the mount is different. Slide the controller (with wrist strap attached) into the holster. Click the rubber stopper in so the controller doesn’t move, then loop the wrist strap around the controller as a backup in case you didn’t secure the rubber stopper properly. It’s quite simple.

All major VR golf games allow custom spacing for Quest 3 golf clubs, so it doesn’t take more than 30 seconds to get comfortable with the club in your game of choice.

The only problem I’ve found with this club is its price. Most Quest 3 golf club accessories cost between $30 and $50, depending on the capabilities. If you’re serious about your scores in Golf+, Walkabout Mini Golf, or another favorite of yours, this might just be the ultimate enthusiast Quest 3 golf club accessory.

Proton Pass does that work for you. When you sign up for a website, its password generator can create a strong password that’s completely unique. Save it in your vault, and Proton Pass can fill in your credentials when you return.

#10263. What is Model Routing in AI?

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

"Do you know the closest ATM?" and "I was charged twice, the refund never arrived, and now my card is blocked" are both customer messages. Most AI apps send both to the same large model and pay large-model prices for each one.

Model routing fixes that. A router looks at each request and sends it to the cheapest model that can handle it, and keeps the expensive model for the requests that need it.

In this guide we'll cover what model routing is, how routers decide, and what it saves. Then we'll build a small router in about 60 lines of JavaScript and run it over 200 customer messages. At one setting it matched a large model's accuracy for about a third of the cost. At another it came within a point and a half for 89% less.

Model routing in AI is the practice of sending each request to the model best suited to it, instead of sending every request to one model. A router sits between your app and a pool of models, reads each request, and picks a model based on what you care about: cost, speed, or quality.

You'll also see it called AI model routing, LLM routing, or LLM model routing. They all mean the same thing. The pool might be a small and a large model from one provider, or models from several providers behind one API.

The idea rests on one observation: most requests are easy. A small model answers them as well as a large one, in a fraction of the time and for a fraction of the price. The router's whole job is finding the hard ones.

The diagram below shows a typical AI model routing architecture.

Model routing works in three steps: the router reads the request, decides which model should answer it, and forwards the request to that model. The middle step is where routers differ. Routing tools decide which model to use in one of four ways.

Rule-based routing uses rules you write by hand. Requests from free-plan users go to a small model. Anything tagged as code goes to a coding model. Prompts over a certain length go to a model with a bigger context window. It's simple, predictable, and free to run, but the rules only know what you thought of in advance.

Intelligent model routing uses a model to make the decision. A small, fast classifier reads each request and predicts which model in the pool will answer it well. You'll also see it called smart or dynamic model routing.

Two more terms come up here. Task-aware routing picks a model by the kind of job, like summarising, coding, or answering a support question. Context-aware routing also looks at what surrounds the request, like the conversation so far, the user, or the tools available.

The big platforms work this way. Microsoft describes its Foundry model router as "a trained language model" that picks a model for each prompt in real time. Google says Gemini Notebook uses its new Decisions API as a real-time router, serving everyday queries with up to 40% lower latency. I've tested the Google Decisions API against Jev on the same messages we'll use below.

Cascade routing tries the cheapest model first and only moves up when the answer isn't good enough. The usual test is confidence: if the cheap model is sure, keep its answer. If it isn't, send the request to a stronger model. It's also called confidence-based routing, and it's what we'll build below.

A cascade needs no separate classifier, because the first model's own confidence does the routing. The trade-off is that escalated requests pay for two models and wait for both.

Fallback routing is about staying up, not saving money. If a model times out, hits a rate limit, or its provider has an outage, the router sends the request to the next model on a list. Most production routers combine fallbacks with one of the three methods above.

The main benefits of model routing in AI systems are lower cost, faster responses, and fewer outages, without giving up quality on the hard requests.

We'll build a cascade router for a job every support tool has: sorting customer messages. Each message gets one of ten labels, like atm support or request refund.

The test set is 200 messages from Banking77, a public set of banking support messages: ten labels, 20 messages each. They're the same 200 messages from my zero shot classification test, which is where the single-model numbers below come from.

Put both keys in a .env file and run the examples with node --env-file=.env. It's the same setup as in my guide to getting a YouTube API key.

Create router.mjs. The first function sends the message to Jev with one multiple-choice question, and returns the label Jev picked along with its confidence:

Confidence runs from 0 to 1. When nearly all the probability sits on one label, it's close to 1. When Jev is torn between two labels, it drops.

The second function asks Claude Opus 5.5 the same question. The output_config block limits the answer to one of our labels, so there's no free text to parse. SitePoint's guide to LLM structured output with JSON Schema explains how that works.

The router itself is about a dozen lines:

If Jev is at least 80% confident, its answer goes straight back. If not, Claude answers instead. And if Claude fails, the router falls back to Jev's answer, because a shaky answer beats an error page.

Try it on an easy message and a harder one:

Jev was sure about the lost card. On the ATM question it leaned towards card acceptance with a confidence of 0.64, so the router asked Claude, which got it right.

Because cheap, strong, and threshold are options, the same function routes between any two models.

Model routing reduces AI costs by keeping easy requests on a cheap model and paying for the expensive one only when it's needed. Here's what that looked like on our 200 messages, starting with each model on its own:

Opus gets six more messages right out of 200, and costs more than 120 times as much.

Now the router, at four confidence thresholds:

Speed improved too. At the 0.8 threshold, the messages Jev kept came back in a median of 227 ms. The ones that escalated took about 2.1 seconds, because they waited for both models.

A note on method: I ran every message through both models once and saved the answers, then replayed the router over them at each threshold. That's how you compare thresholds without paying for the same test four times.

Then I ran the router live to check. At 0.95 it scored the same 95.0% and sent 61 messages to Opus. At 0.8 it scored 93.5% and sent 20, a point under the replay. Two answers moved: one of Jev's confidence scores landed at 0.82 instead of 0.78, just over the line, and Opus changed its answer on one message. Scores near the threshold shift a little between runs, so leave yourself a margin.

The choice of cheap model matters less than you'd think. With Google's Gemini Decision 1 in first place instead of Jev, a 0.7 threshold reached the same 95% for US$0.28 per 1,000 messages.

Model routing can cost a little quality, and the threshold decides how much. In our test the gap to Opus ranged from nothing at 0.95 to two points at 0.7.

The loss comes from one place: answers the cheap model got wrong while feeling sure. At the 0.8 threshold, Jev kept five wrong answers because its confidence was above the line. Opus got four of those five wrong as well, so only one was a real loss: "Do all ATMs take this card?", which Jev filed under card acceptance with a confidence of 0.91.

Escalating isn't risk-free either. On one message, Jev had the right label with a confidence of 0.44, and Opus overruled it with the wrong one.

To optimize costs using AI model routing, measure first, route second, and keep measuring. These six steps cover LLM model routing cost optimization for any pair of models:

The return is easy to estimate once you know the split. If nine in ten requests stay on a model that costs 1% as much, the bill drops by about 89%, which is what we measured.

Several inference providers have multi-model routing built in, and most AI gateway platforms support model routing too, so you don't have to write your own. As of November 2026, these are the top model routing solutions for AI apps. Each name links to the tool's model routing docs:

Hosted model routing platforms for large language models decide for you, which is the fastest way to start. A hand-built router like ours takes more work, but you choose the signal and you can see why every request went where it did.

An LLM router is the component that does model routing for large language models. It can be a function in your own code, like the one in this guide, a hosted model such as Microsoft Foundry's model router, or a feature of an AI gateway.

Smart model routing is another name for intelligent model routing: a model, not a hand-written rule, decides where each request goes. Dynamic model routing means the same thing.

The ROI of AI model routing depends on how much of your traffic a cheaper model can handle. In our test, a 0.8 threshold kept 89.5% of messages on the cheap model and cut the cost per 1,000 messages from US$1.96 to US$0.22, for about a point of accuracy. The build was about 60 lines of code.

OpenRouter's auto model routing works through its Auto Router. You set the model to openrouter/auto, and a decision model picks a model and a reasoning effort for each request. You pay the standard rate of whichever model it selects, with no extra fee.

OpenClaw model routing is a primary model plus an ordered list of fallbacks. In the OpenClaw model routing configuration you set agents.defaults.model.primary and agents.defaults.model.fallbacks, and an agent can override the fallback list. When a model fails, OpenClaw first rotates auth profiles on the same provider, then moves to the next model on the list, as its model failover docs describe. That makes it fallback routing, not cost-based routing.

An AI gateway simplifies model routing by putting every model behind one API, one set of keys, and one place for logs. Your app calls the gateway, and the routing rules live in config instead of code, so changing a model doesn't need a deploy.

Mid-stream failover is when a router switches models after a streamed response has already started, because the first model failed partway through. It's the hardest kind of failover, because the user has already seen part of an answer. Many routers only fail over before the first token arrives and return an error after that, so check what your gateway does.

#10258. Researchers ‘recognize that AI will never replace HPC,’ says AMD executive — high-precision workloads will ‘exist for a very long time’ even as AI usage expands

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

While AI continues to reshape industries across science and mathematics, traditional supercomputing has seemingly taken a back seat. However, AMD's Scott Tease told Tom's Hardware Premium that most HPC sites “recognize that AI will never replace HPC,” and suggests that building a heterogeneous compute stack is the best path forward to tackle bleeding-edge research, despite Nvidia pushing performance on lower-precision data types higher for AI workloads at the cost of native performance for the important double-precision FP64 format that’s critical for HPC workloads.

A heterogeneous compute stack brings together quantum, HPC, and AI under one roof, a fleet that AMD is helping pilot at Oak Ridge National Laboratory alongside IBM. Tease is AMD’s corporate vice president of HPC and sovereign AI, who joined the company earlier this year after more than a decade at Lenovo (and, before that, another 13 years at IBM). We spoke with Tease ahead of SC26 to get a read on the state of HPC, how it intersects with AI, and what’s actually driving the research community in a time when computer chips are driving headlines about international espionage plots.

FP64 is important for HPC workloads because of its precision. FP64 is a hot-button issue, largely due to how Nvidia’s GPUs have progressed over the past two generations. Or rather, how they haven't progressed. Nvidia’s latest Rubin GPU has 33 TFLOPs of FP64 compute, which is lower than the four-year-old H100’s 34 TFLOPs of FP64. For context, AMD’s year-old Instinct MI355X peaks at 78.6 TFLOPs of FP64 compute, while its upcoming HPC-angled MI430X peaks at 288 TFLOPs.

Nvidia has leaned on emulation to drive FP64 performance higher, a tactic that AMD is now pushing back on, but regardless, it’s easy to see why the HPC world could feel left behind in this new AI era. AI is defined by fuzzy, low-precision data formats, which is diametrically opposed to the accuracy of high-precision FP64. And, over the last generations of hardware from the big green giant that is Nvidia, FP64 performance has seemingly stalled in favor of driving low-precision data formats for AI workloads.

“Maybe a few years ago, people in HPC might have felt a little threatened by AI because it was taking some of the limelight,” Tease told Tom’s Hardware Premium. “But I think now they’ve come to realize that AI is an essential part of scientific discovery today. It’s just, how are you going to deploy it?”

That central question recontextualizes the apparent conflict between AI compute and HPC compute. Tease pointed to researchers spawning dozens of agents to explore viable paths for a given intent, while still leaving the complex simulations for a traditional FP64-driven HPC fleet. “...to try to do that with old approaches where you have islands of CPUs or islands of GPUs or something like that, and they're very different. They're not interconnected. It's just not going to be able to keep up,” Tease said.

One of the challenges right now is actually connecting those systems, which is an area AMD is exploring with its recent collaboration with IBM and Oak Ridge National Laboratory to deploy AMD’s “classical” computing stack in the Umbra HPC system alongside IBM’s 129-qubit Quantum Nighthawk quantum computer. The systems will connect to the Oak Ridge Leadership Computing Facility, allowing researchers to “explore how integrated quantum, AI, and scientific computing can accelerate scientific discoveries and national security.”

If the HPC crowd felt slighted by AI, you can imagine how quantum researchers probably feel.

Regardless, Tease says the future of scientific computing looks like a unified fleet that brings together AI, HPC, and quantum. “There [are] going to be a lot of problems where quantum will not be the be-all, end-all. There are some problems like human health, chemistry, where quantum’s going to allow us to do things we really can’t do with classical computing today. So having those things coexist, having them in a single workload, is where we want to get to.”

Scheduling workloads for HPC is a solved game, with open-source schedulers like Slurm driving much of the TOP500 to properly schedule workloads on supercomputers. Tease described something that goes deeper to connect these systems. “What we want to be able to do is not only do those kinds of ‘where should it go, here or there’ for HPC, we want to figure out when AI could be applied to it, when [GPU acceleration is] appropriate… and also when is it best to go in a quantum environment?”

The Oak Ridge Leadership Computing Facility is a test bed for figuring out how to hook these systems together properly. The press release explicitly states that it’s a “shared environment to evaluate the architectures, software, and workflows that future hybrid quantum systems will require.” The system is a co-design initiative with IBM, bringing together quantum and classical computing into one system.

With the system, researchers are exploring how to accelerate quantum error correction with classical systems, a critical step in quantum computing to weed out environmental influences. The goal is ultimately to accelerate quantum systems in the Quantum Genesis Q competition, an initiative launched by the U.S. Department of Energy that will provide up to $215 million in funding for quantum research.

By late 2028, the competition calls for quantum computers “with at least 100 logical qubits that are capable of performing hundreds of millions [of] fault-tolerant operations.” IBM describes the error correction research that will be performed at Oak Ridge as a “key component” in driving toward that goal. Separately, AMD announced last week an investment in quantum outfit PsiQuantum to provide CPUs, GPUs, and FPGAs to drive the company’s first fault-tolerant quantum system.

Jake Roach is the Senior CPU Analyst at Tom’s Hardware, writing reviews, news, and features about the latest consumer and workstation processors.

#10256. Building Conversational Commerce with Multi-Agent Swarms on GCP: A Working Prototype

Imagine ordering your weekly groceries as naturally as texting a friend:"Hey, we're out of milk and eggs. Can you also add some snacks for Friday movie night?"

In our previous post, Building Conversational Commerce with Multi-Agent Swarms on GCP, we shared the architectural blueprint for conversational commerce—proposing a 3-layer decoupled design (Ingress, Memory, and Cognition) to solve messaging timeouts, state tracking, and response reliability.

While blueprints establish the architecture on paper, production readiness demands working software. In this post, we take that blueprint and bring it to life with a functional prototype called HappyDelivery.

Using LangGraph, Gemini, FastAPI, and Google Cloud, we'll walk through the code, the state machine, deterministic financial tools, and an automated verification suite that demonstrates how this multi-agent swarm operates in practice.

To build a reliable commerce swarm, we implemented three decoupled layers that handle the journey of every customer text message:

Let's examine how each layer functions in code with its own dedicated architecture.

A frequent question when designing conversational commerce is: how does a message sent by a customer on their smartphone actually land in Layer 1, and can this architecture support messaging platforms beyond WhatsApp?

End users do not communicate directly with your Google Cloud servers. Instead, customer messages traverse an operator ecosystem before landing on your webhook:

Every major consumer messaging platform operates on the exact same fundamental contract: an asynchronous HTTPS POST webhook delivery with a strict HTTP acknowledgement timeout:

While the payload schemas and cryptographic headers differ slightly between platforms, Layer 1 abstracts away all client differences:

This ensures the rest of your system—Session Store (Layer 2) and Agent Swarm (Layer 3)—operates on clean, normalized data without caring whether the shopper sent their message from an iPhone, an Android RCS chat, or a WhatsApp thread.

Messaging platforms are completely stateless: every text message arrives as an isolated webhook event. To provide a coherent shopping experience, the system must instantly reconstruct the customer's full context without having to re-read or re-parse days of chat logs on every turn.

We use Cloud Memorystore (Redis) keyed by the customer's verified phone number (session:{phone_number}) to store active state:

A common question in conversational commerce is whether state lookups will introduce latency. Here is what the baseline infrastructure supports:

Key Takeaway: In practice, the Session & Cart Layer will never be the bottleneck—it easily outpaces upstream webhook inference and downstream LLM inference.

Rather than relying on a single monolithic prompt to handle every task, we separate reasoning into a swarm of specialized agents:

Now that we have seen the high-level design of the three layers, let's look at the actual Python prototype code that brings each layer to life.

The goal of this service is simple: verify authenticity across any inbound channel, normalize the payload into a canonical schema, and get off the critical path in < 150ms.

Here is our implementation showing how multiple messaging channels (WhatsApp, RCS, Apple Messages, SMS) are handled by pluggable adapters:

By immediately returning an HTTP 200 OK and handing the payload off to Pub/Sub, we completely decouple message intake from downstream AI reasoning time across all messaging ecosystems.

We benchmarked the live Cloud Run HTTPS webhook intake across warm invocations:

This empirically proves our architectural claim: the Ingress service validates the cryptographic signature and immediately commits the event to Pub/Sub, freeing the customer chat client without waiting on agentic reasoning.

We also verified security enforcement against tampered payloads:

The worker pods pulling from Pub/Sub need to instantly look up who the customer is and what is in their current cart.

Here is the session store implementation using Cloud Memorystore (Redis):

Each record contains the customer ID (cust_9821), saved address, dietary restrictions, and current cart items. This guarantees that when a customer texts back hours later, their cart is restored instantaneously.

Every session lookup executes in 0.318ms, proving our architectural claim that restoring customer context and persistent cart state adds virtually zero latency to the conversational turn.

With Ingress and Redis handling incoming traffic and memory, all the decision-making lives in the LangGraph multi-agent graph.

Every turn passes through a typed data structure holding the customer's profile, active cart, and the supervisor's routing decision:

Next, we define the nodes and conditional edges that allow the Supervisor to dynamically route tasks to the right specialist:

A core tenet of our blueprint is: delegate arithmetic to deterministic tools. Generative models excel at language and reasoning, while financial calculations require exact precision. If a model computes taxes or discounts in free-form text, it may occasionally introduce calculation errors.

Instead, we bind deterministic tools to our specialist nodes. For example, the Inventory and Transaction agents use a Python calculation tool rather than computing prices in-prompt:

Worker containers pull messages asynchronously from Pub/Sub and stream LangGraph node transitions in real time.

Now that we have all three components—Ingress (Layer 1), Redis Memory (Layer 2), and the LangGraph Swarm (Layer 3)—how do they actually run together as a single unified system?

Here is the complete end-to-end lifecycle of a customer's message:

The worker loop that coordinates all three layers is concise, clean, and completely decouples the user-facing webhook from back-office AI reasoning:

With the entire pipeline wired together, let's look at what happens when we run an actual customer order through it.

To verify the prototype, we ran an automated simulation of an end-to-end customer order. Here is how the swarm behaves across three natural conversational turns:

Behind the Scenes: The InventoryAgent mutates the in-memory cart (removing Sprite and adding Coke Zero), executes calculate_cart_totals, and confirms delivery details without losing context.

We validated all core behaviors—webhook HMAC validation, catalog indexing, deterministic math, and multi-turn state transitions—with automated unit and integration tests:

A critical question when evaluating multi-agent architectures is token consumption and operational inference cost. In a naive implementation where massive prompts and entire conversation histories are repeatedly re-sent on every step, LLM API bills can quickly eat into e-commerce retail margins.

Our prototype is engineered specifically to prevent token waste through strict agent specialization, deterministic tools, and externalized Redis state:

Here is the exact token telemetry measured across the live 3-turn HappyDelivery session:

Bringing the blueprint into working code revealed several practical realities:

Building this prototype proved that the decoupled architecture outlined in our blueprint isn't just theoretical—it is practical, reliable, and ready for production on Google Cloud.

By combining LangGraph for multi-agent workflows, Gemini for natural language reasoning, and GCP for fast ingress and persistent memory, we can build shopping experiences that feel as simple as chatting with a friend while maintaining the enterprise rigor required for commerce.

#10252. Android Auto 18.2 update starts rolling out with more light mode teases

Google has started rolling out the Android Auto 18.2 update, but don’t expect much to change on your car’s display.

Heading first to beta users, Android Auto 18.2 has started rolling out. This comes a little over a week after Android 18.1 hit the stable release track.

As Android Auto users should be very used to at this point, the update itself doesn’t bring any new user-facing changes that we can see. That’s not to say nothing is changing. There’s an endless list of bugs Google can fix. Just a few recent examples include Gemini bugs, foldable headaches, strange UI issues, connection problems, double-skipping songs, and more. Bug fixes are the primary point of these smaller Android Auto app updates.

Notably, this update also doesn’t fully cut off users on older Android versions yet. Google has confirmed Android 11 will be the new minimum requirement, but Android Auto 18.2 still supports as far back as Android 9.

About APK Insight: In this “APK Insight” post, we’ve decompiled the latest version of an application that Google uploaded to the Play Store. When we decompile these files (called APKs, in the case of Android apps), we’re able to see various lines of code within that hint at possible future features. Keep in mind that Google may or may not ever ship these features, and our interpretation of what they are may be imperfect. We’ll try to enable those that are closer to being finished, however, to show you how they’ll look in case that they do ship. With that in mind, read on.

Our teardown of Android Auto 18.2 didn’t turn up much of interest.

The biggest noteworthy change is that Google has updated over 100 assets within the Android Auto app in preparation of light mode support. These are largely around various button designs in the experience, such as:

Light mode for Android Auto is something we’ve been waiting ages to see go live, and every recent update has been building out the feature more and more. As for when it will be released, that’s anyone’s guess.

#10250. The Xiaomi 18 Pro’s rear displays support Google apps, Spotify, and custom GenAI apps

The Xiaomi 18 Pro series’ rear displays might actually be useful, after all.

Only a few companies have debuted phones with rear displays, and, quite frankly, most of them have been fairly useless. Xiaomi is hoping to turn all that around with the Xiaomi 18 Pro and Xiaomi 18 Pro Max’s rear display, which the company says supports Google Maps, YouTube Music, Spotify, and lots of additional apps via a nifty GenAI integration.

The news comes via GSMArena and builds on the AMA videos Xu Fei, VP & CMO of the Xiaomi Group, has been posting on X. If you haven’t seen it yet, the camera island on the back of the Xiaomi 18 Pro series looks similar to the cover screen on a flip-style foldable like the Galaxy Z Flip 8 or Motorola Razr series. It’s a full-color touchscreen and has impressively high resolution.

YouTube Music and Spotify will feature prominent play/pause and skip buttons, plus the artwork and name of the song playing. Google Maps will give you a navigation progress indicator, plus the next upcoming direction.

In addition to official integration, Xiaomi is letting users create their own rear display apps using a built-in GenAI app creation tool. Just describe what you want, and the onboard AI will create the app for you. It looks quite handy and should help solve the “useless” problem that prior rear displays have faced. The Xiaomi 18 Pro series will debut globally.
