#10249. How to Migrate an Elementor Site to the WordPress Block Editor

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Elementor made your site easy to build. It may also be making it slow. Here's how to move a site onto the Block Editor without breaking URLs, losing content or dropping out of Google.

Page builders solved a real problem. Before the Block Editor matured, they were the easiest way to give clients drag-and-drop layouts. Many of those sites are still running today, and a lot of them are slow on phones.

The data backs that up. According to the HTTP Archive Core Web Vitals Technology Report for June 2026, 36.8% of Elementor sites pass Core Web Vitals on mobile. Sites built with the Block Editor pass 53.0% of the time. That's correlation, not proof, since hosting and setup matter too. But in my experience, it matches what happens when you remove the builder from a slow site.

My reason for migrating sites off Elementor is simple: **page builders make websites heavy. **Having said that, Elementor has improved a lot. Its performance settings, Flexbox Containers, and asset-loading options cut much of the bloat, and a carefully tuned Elementor site can pass Core Web Vitals. But tuning only goes so far. You’re still shipping the builder’s framework, wrapper markup and scripts, and every plugin or Elementor update is a chance for the speed to slip again.

For small business sites that don’t need drag-and-drop editing, rebuilding the same design on the WordPress Block Editor (Gutenberg) removes that weight at the source, without changing what the client sees.

This guide follows the order I use. It assumes you're comfortable with WordPress admin, WP-CLI and a staging site. If you need to set those up first, SitePoint's guide to the ultimate WordPress development environment covers the tooling.

Not every Elementor site needs to move. Migration makes sense when:

It's harder to justify when the site leans on Elementor Pro features you'd need to replace, such as complex popups, dynamic loops or WooCommerce templates. Those can all be rebuilt, but the job gets bigger fast.

You can't prove a migration worked without "before" numbers. Capture these on the live site first.

Crawl the site with a tool such as Screaming Frog, and export every indexable URL with its title, meta description, H1 and canonical tag. You can also pull published pages and posts with WP-CLI:

Run Google PageSpeed Insights, which uses Lighthouse for its lab scores, on the homepage and two or three key templates. Save the mobile lab scores and, if the site has enough traffic, the field data from real users.

Export the last three months of pages and queries from Google Search Console. That's what you'll compare against after launch.

Elementor stores each page's layout as JSON in the elementordata post meta field, and marks builder pages with elementoredit_mode. This command lists every page that uses the builder:

Then list everything else Elementor controls, which is easy to miss:

On the small business sites I've migrated, the inventory usually turns up more than the owner expects. The pages are the easy part. The surprises are saved templates nobody remembers creating, a popup still running on every page, add-on widgets from plugins that were installed once and forgotten, and contact forms that live inside Elementor Pro rather than a form plugin. Finding those before the rebuild saves a broken launch.

Work on a staging copy. Install a block theme, such as Twenty Twenty-Five or a lightweight starter theme, and move the Elementor global styles into theme.json. That way colors, fonts and spacing are defined once, instead of in every widget.

Copy the hex values and font names from Elementor's Site Settings so the new site matches the old one. Rebuild the header and footer as template parts in the Site Editor. If you'd rather write a theme yourself, SitePoint's series on building a WordPress theme from scratch covers the basics.

Most Elementor sites repeat the same few sections: a hero, a services grid, testimonials and a call to action. Build each one once as a block pattern, then reuse it on every page. Patterns are plain block markup, so you don't need React to write them, whatever you may have heard about React and WordPress development.

In a block theme, any PHP file in the /patterns folder with a header comment is registered automatically:

For page content, there's a useful shortcut. Elementor saves a plain HTML copy of each page into post_content. Open a page in the Block Editor, and the old content usually appears in a Classic block. Choose Convert to blocks, then delete the leftovers and rebuild the layout with your patterns. It keeps the copy and images, so you only redo the design.

The difference shows up in the page source. Here's typical front-end markup for a single heading on an Elementor page, using the older section and column layout:

Newer Elementor versions with Flexbox containers and the optimized markup settings trim some of those wrappers, but most older sites still carry all of them. Multiply that by every element on every page, and the weight adds up.

To speed up this step, I built a free Elementor to Gutenberg converter. Paste in a page's elementordata JSON from Step 2, and it returns block markup you can paste into the Block Editor's code editor. It handles headings, text, images, buttons, spacers, dividers and column layouts, and lists anything it can't convert, such as forms and popups, for you to rebuild by hand. It runs entirely in your browser, so the page data never leaves your computer.

The code is rarely what holds a migration up. Content is. Once clients see their site being rebuilt, many decide it's time to rewrite their pages too, and the project ends up waiting on copywriters and approvals. My fix is to separate the two: migrate the existing content as it is, launch, then update the copy afterwards. That keeps the speed and SEO gains on schedule, and it means any ranking change can be traced to either the migration or the new copy, not both at once.

This is the step that decides whether rankings hold.

To spot-check a redirect or a status code from the command line:

When staging matches the live site, deploy it. Then:

Leave the old elementordata meta in place for a few weeks, in case you need to roll back. Once you're confident, take a backup and remove it to shrink the database.

Run PageSpeed Insights again straight away for lab scores. Field data takes longer: the Chrome UX Report uses a rolling 28-day window, so give it a month before judging real-user results. Compare Search Console clicks for the same period before and after launch.

In my migrations, the biggest change is usually page weight and loading time, because the builder's CSS, JavaScript and wrapper markup are gone. What doesn't always improve is server response time. If the site is on slow shared hosting, the first byte still arrives late, and that needs a hosting fix, not a rebuild. Search traffic should hold steady if the URLs and metadata were protected. If it drops, the Step 5 crawl comparison is the first place to look.

Moving off Elementor isn't a one-click job, but it isn't a full redesign either. Record a baseline, inventory what the builder controls, rebuild with patterns, and protect every URL. Most of the speed gain comes from removing code the site never needed. For what to tackle after that, see these practical web performance techniques. Most of the risk comes from rushing the SEO checks. Done carefully, the client gets the same site, a faster one, and an editor that comes with WordPress itself.

***Author Bio: ***Fahad Raza is a WordPress developer and SEO consultant based in Sydney. He has built websites since 2005, worked in SEO for more than 18 years, and spent eight and a half years on the IKEA Australia and New Zealand website.

As we spotted in September, the Gemini app is adding Google Wallet integration in the US that makes possible “responses informed by your spending habits.”

In the Gemini app, @Google Wallet allows you to:

The interactive visualizations (seen above) are quite cool. This integration leverages third-party service Plaid to “securely link your financial accounts from your credit cards and banks” to Google Wallet.

You decide if and when you want to opt in to this experience, and you can turn the connection on or off at any time. Learn more here.

New steps have been taken to ensure that countries and companies that are not on whitelists that would allow them to import advanced AI hardware cannot use cloud-based workarounds. The U.S. Commerce Department’s Bureau of Industry and Security (BIS) has today unveiled an interim final rule, or IFR, in collaboration with the Department of Treasury and the National Security Council, aimed at backing up existing limitations on the export of hardware built by companies like Nvidia to non-whitelisted companies based in countries such as China. "The rule is designed to ensure that existing restrictions on access to sensitive American technology cannot be circumvented through remote computing arrangements" using data centers in third-party countries, a statement explains.

Nvidia, for example, is only allowed to export its high-performance AI chips to companies that are on a whitelist. But amid ongoing claims of black market GPU sales and a recent crackdown on overseas AI GPU rentals from China, it has become clear that these restrictions perhaps aren't as robust as they were intended to be. Nvidia Blackwell Ultra B300 GPUs were even recently discovered in a data center used by China Telecom.

But while blocking the sale of physical goods is one thing, the cloud-based workaround is another entirely. Nvidia itself told data center operators earlier this month that they must limit remote access to their services — 51% of their business must come from non-Chinese companies. But, again, it's unclear how effective that approach has been in ensuring access to powerful AI capabilities doesn't fall into the wrong hands, even if they're accessed remotely rather than in person.

The new BIS rule means that overseas data centers will be required to do more to limit access to their services where U.S-based technology is being used. Restrictions will also be placed on data centers that supply remote access to companies whose headquarters are deemed to be in higher-risk countries, such as China, Russia, and Iran. A license will also be required for anyone trying to export advanced chips to companies with headquarters in Indonesia, Malaysia, Singapore, Thailand, or Brazil. The new requirements will officially go into effect in 90 days.

This latest rule comes against a backdrop of ongoing uncertainty over America's AI crown jewel, Nvidia. Jensen Huang, the company's CEO, resigned after confirming an affair, but is now potentially in line for a quick-fire comeback, despite the fact that the woman involved in the affair is an alleged Chinese spy.

#10238. Smaller apps and faster GPU inference with LiteRT in Google Play services

LiteRT in Google Play services is Android's on-device inference runtime for custom models, handling more than a trillion inferences a day across billions of devices. Today, it gets its biggest upgrade yet: GPU inference that runs about 1.4x faster than the legacy GPU delegate, powered by ML Drift and available through the new CompiledModel API, LiteRT’s modern successor to the legacy Interpreter API.

Until now, taking advantage of this faster GPU stack required bundling LiteRT in your app. In our testing, apps that switched to LiteRT in Google Play services are about 8 MB smaller, making them faster to download and lighter on users' devices, because the runtime, including the GPU accelerator, ships once with Play services rather than inside every app. More than 100K Android apps bundle their own copy of LiteRT or TensorFlow Lite today, so the savings add up quickly as apps switch.

If your Android app runs custom ML models on CPU or GPU, we encourage you to switch to LiteRT in Google Play services. You can see it in action in the Google AI Edge Gallery app, where the Scrapbook feature's image segmentation runs on LiteRT in Google Play services.

Developers that adopt LiteRT in Google Play Services enjoy immediate benefits:

LiteRT is the evolution of TensorFlow Lite. It keeps the same .tflite model format you rely on and adds the CompiledModel API, which makes acceleration across fragmented hardware reliable:

Whether you're starting fresh or switching from bundled LiteRT, using LiteRT in Google Play services takes a few lines of code:

If you're already using the legacy Interpreter API in Google Play services, your Interpreter code keeps working on CPU when you switch to play-services-litert. To run on the GPU, move to the CompiledModel API.

Let your coding agent do the work. In Agent Mode, the new LiteRT skill for Android Studio automates the move to LiteRT in Google Play services and the CompiledModel API. Ask it to “migrate my TFLite code to CompiledModel,” or type @litert-runtime, and it switches your app to play-services-litert, rewrites your Interpreter calls, and sets up GPU acceleration with CPU fallback. It can also scaffold a new LiteRT app. Treat it as a strong first pass: review the diff, build, and compare outputs on a real device. Get the skill on GitHub.

Upgrading from legacy APIs? The legacy GPU delegate and custom delegate APIs are deprecated in favor of CompiledModel GPU acceleration and the Compiler Plugin and Dispatch APIs, and NNAPI will be removed starting in 2027. Older SDKs now receive critical security fixes only. Apps you've already shipped keep working, but plan your move to the CompiledModel API now: code that uses deprecated APIs won't compile against the latest SDK, and staying on an older SDK can cause conflicts as your other dependencies move forward. See the release notes for details.

Looking ahead, LiteRT in Google Play services is our foundation for running your own task-specific models on Android, with CPU and GPU acceleration.

#10236. Automating Contract Signing in Node.js: Webhooks, Verification, and Storing Signed PDFs

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Sending a document for signature is the easy part of an e-signature integration. One POST request and the signer gets an email. The work that actually breaks in production happens afterwards: knowing when the document was signed, trusting the notification that told you so, handling the same notification twice, and getting the final PDF into storage before the download link expires.

This tutorial builds that whole loop in Node.js with Express. An event in your app (a deal is approved, a client is onboarded, a task moves to "ready to sign") sends a contract from a template. When the signer finishes, a webhook arrives, your server verifies it, and the signed PDF plus its audit trail get saved without anyone touching them.

The code uses Firma.dev, the e-signature API I work on, because I needed a real API to make the examples runnable. The patterns are not specific to it, though. Signature verification, idempotent handlers, short-lived download URLs, and reconciliation jobs show up in almost every webhook-driven integration, and the same structure applies whether you're receiving events from a payment processor, a CI system, or another signing provider.

There's a fifth part that tutorials usually skip: a reconciliation job that catches anything the webhooks missed. We'll build that too, because webhooks are a notification mechanism, not a delivery guarantee.

Before sending anything, wrap the HTTP calls in one place. This is where authentication lives, and it's also where you handle 429 Too Many Requests once instead of in every caller. Most APIs, this one included, return a Retry-After header on a 429, so the client should honour it rather than guessing a backoff.

The 503 branch matters later. When a document has just been completed, the final PDF and its completion certificate take a moment to generate, and the download endpoint says so with a 503 and a Retry-After instead of handing you a half-built file.

The trigger depends on your product. It might be a route handler, a queue consumer, or a CRM automation calling into your service. What matters is that the function doing the sending is small and takes plain data:

Two design choices are worth calling out.

Use a single create-and-send call when you can. Many signing APIs split this into "create a draft" and "send the draft." That's useful when a human needs to review the document first, but for automation it creates a window where your process can crash between the two calls and leave an orphaned draft. A combined endpoint makes the operation atomic from your side.

Store the provider's ID on your own record immediately. Webhook payloads reference the signing request, not your deal or customer. Without that mapping you'll end up searching by email address, which falls apart as soon as one client signs two documents.

Here's the first place integrations quietly go wrong. Webhook signatures are computed over the exact bytes the sender transmitted. If Express parses the body as JSON first, you lose those bytes, and re-serialising the object won't reproduce them (key order, whitespace, and number formatting can all change). Signature checks then fail intermittently, which is the worst kind of failure.

The fix is to use express.raw() on the webhook route only, so the handler receives a Buffer:

Most webhook providers sign payloads with HMAC-SHA256 using a shared secret. The details vary, so read your provider's docs for the exact header format, but the scheme here is common: the header looks like t=1731456000,v1=5f2b..., and the signed string is the timestamp, a dot, and the raw body. Stripe uses a very similar format, and the Standard Webhooks spec describes the same idea as a vendor-neutral convention.

Each line here defends against something specific.

The OWASP guidance on cryptographic storage covers the other half of this: the secret belongs in an environment variable or secrets manager, never in the repository.

Every serious webhook system delivers at least once, not exactly once. If your endpoint is slow, returns a 5xx, or the network drops the response, the sender retries. Firma.dev, for example, makes up to three attempts per event (immediately, then after at least five minutes, then after at least an hour) and treats anything other than a 2xx as a failure, including redirects. Other providers retry more aggressively. Either way, your handler will eventually see the same event twice.

The defence is to record each event's unique ID and skip ones you've seen. In the server above that's alreadyProcessed and markProcessed. For a demo, a Set works:

In production, use your database and let a unique constraint do the work, so two concurrent deliveries can't both pass the check:

Also notice the order in the route: respond with 200, then do the work. Many providers time out deliveries after a few seconds (five, in this case). Downloading a PDF and uploading it to object storage can easily take longer than that, and a timeout counts as a failure, which triggers a retry, which triggers duplicate work. Acknowledging fast and processing asynchronously avoids that loop.

setImmediate is fine for a tutorial, but it has a real weakness: if the process dies after sending the 200 and before finishing the work, the event is acknowledged and lost. In production, push the event onto a durable queue (BullMQ, SQS, a jobs table) before responding. Step 7 covers the safety net for whatever still slips through.

Now the actual business logic. You'll usually see several event types (sent, viewed, a signer signed, declined, expired, completed). Only one of them means "the document is final":

Three things in here are easy to get wrong.

Don't treat "a signer signed" as "the document is done." With multiple signers, each one triggers its own event, and a per-signer "all complete" flag can be true on more than one of them. Key your storage logic off the single completion event.

Don't persist the download URL. Pre-signed URLs expire (this one carries an expires_at). Saving the link to your database and serving it to users later produces broken links a few minutes or hours later. Download the bytes and store them in storage you control. Swapping the fs.writeFile calls for an S3 PutObject or a Google Drive upload is a drop-in change.

Store the audit trail, not just the PDF. If a signature is ever disputed, the PDF shows what was signed, and the audit trail shows who signed it, when, and from where (timestamps, IP addresses, the sequence of views and signatures). That evidence is what makes an electronic signature defensible, which brings us to the legal side.

Retries are finite. Endpoints go down during deploys. Some providers auto-disable a webhook after enough consecutive failures, and events aren't guaranteed to arrive in order. Any one of these can leave a completed document sitting unsaved.

A small scheduled job closes that gap. Keep a list of signing requests your app considers "pending," and periodically ask the API for their real status:

Since storeSignedDocument overwrites by ID, running it twice for the same document is harmless. That's the property you want from every step in a webhook pipeline: doing it again shouldn't change the result.

None of this is legal advice, but a few facts shape how you build.

In the US, the ESIGN Act and the Uniform Electronic Transactions Act (adopted by 49 states) give electronic signatures the same legal standing as handwritten ones for most commercial documents. ESIGN also has requirements around consumer consent and record retention, which is part of why you store the final PDF yourself rather than relying on a third-party link.

In the EU, eIDAS defines three levels: simple (SES), advanced (AdES), and qualified (QES). SES and AdES cover most business contracts. QES requires a qualified trust service provider and certificate-based identity, and some documents (certain property transfers and court filings, depending on the country) require it or exclude e-signatures entirely. Check which level your document types need before you pick a provider, since not every API offers every level.

Across jurisdictions, the evidence that holds up is the same: who signed, how they were identified, when it happened, and proof the document hasn't changed since. That's the audit trail and the completion certificate, which is why Step 6 stores both.

If you're evaluating providers for a workflow like this, the questions that matter at the integration level are mostly not on the pricing page:

The send call is one line. The reliability comes from everything around it: keeping the raw body for verification, checking timestamps and comparing in constant time, acknowledging fast and deduplicating by event ID, downloading files before their links expire, and running a reconciliation job for the events that never arrive. Get those right and contract signing becomes something your app does in the background, with no one chasing signatures or copying PDFs between folders.

For the Firma.dev-specific details used here (event names, header format, rate limits), see the webhooks guide.

#10232. China retains fastest supercomputer in the world crown as AI data centers soak up new hardware

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

As hyperscalers want more AI, traditional supercomputers take a back seat.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

China's LineShine remains the world's highest-performing supercomputer, according to the 68th edition of the Top500 list published at SC26 in Chicago on Monday. For the first time in decades, the supercomputer ranking saw exceptionally few additions despite unprecedented demand for high-performance computing hardware, as the vast amounts of compute resources deployed these days are destined for artificial intelligence (AI) data centers rather than traditional supercomputers.

China's LineShine, installed at the National Supercomputer Center in Shenzhen, offers sustained FP64 compute performance of 2.198 FP64 ExaFLOPS in the High Performance Linpack (HPL) benchmark at 42.22 MW. The machine relies on the homegrown LingKun platform with 304-core Armx9-based LX2 processors clocked at 1.55 GHz (13,789,440 cores in total) and the proprietary LingQi interconnect. Unlike the other four exascale-class supercomputers, LineShine does not use any compute accelerators and exclusively relies on CPUs, which is largely why it only gains 3.6X performance when moving from FP64 to mixed-precision data, which is lower than systems that use low-precision accelerators.

America's most powerful El Capitan supercomputer at the Lawrence Livermore National Laboratory relies on AMD's Instinct MI300A accelerated processing units (APUs), and the HPE Cray EX255a platform delivers 1.809 FP64 ExaFLOPS at 29.69 MW, which makes it significantly more power-efficient than its rival from China. The Frontier supercomputer at the Oak Ridge National Laboratory, based on AMD's 3rd Generation EPYC processors and Instinct MI250X compute GPUs, achieves 1.353 FP64 ExaFLOPS at 24.61 MW. Meanwhile, Aurora at the Argonne National Laboratory, which uses Intel's Xeon CPU Max 9470 processors and Data Center GPU Max accelerators, offers 1.012 FP64 ExaFLOPS at 38.70 MW.

All three American exascale supercomputers use HPE’s Cray EX platforms and Slingshot-11 interconnect.

Germany’s Jupiter Booster at the Jülich Supercomputing Center, which employs Nvidia’s GH200 Grace Hopper Superchips, the BullSequana XH3000 platform, and Nvidia’s quad-rail InfiniBand NDR200 interconnect, delivers 1.0 FP64 ExaFLOPS at 15.79 MW and remains the most energy-efficient exascale supercomputer on the list.

Remarkably, neither the 10 highest-performing supercomputers nor the 10 most energy-efficient machines have changed their positions since June.

Only 27 new supercomputers (5.4% of all machines on the list) made it to the November edition of the Top 500, the lowest number since the project was founded in 1993. The most powerful newcomer is South Korea's Hanggang at the KISTI Supercomputing Center, which occupies the 12th position with 342.6 FP64 PFLOPS. The machine uses Nvidia's GH200 Grace Hopper Superchips and HPE's Cray EX254n platform. Hanggang also ranks eighth in the HPCG benchmark with 4.29 HPCG PFLOPS.

The aggregate HPL performance of the world's 500 highest-performing supercomputers increased by a modest 4.4% over the past six months to 19.57 FP64 ExaFLOPS. Nonetheless, it now takes a machine with 2.857 PFLOPS of sustained FP64 performance to qualify for the TOP500, up 7.3% from 2.662 PFLOPS in June. The performance of the systems ranked 50th and 100th rose from 49.85 PFLOPS to 55.81 PFLOPS (12%) and from 21.85 PFLOPS to 22.74 PFLOPS, respectively. Also, there are now 27 machines that deliver at least 100 PFLOPS of FP64 performance, up from 25 in June, whereas the number of supercomputers equipped with compute accelerators increased from 275 to 291 (58.2%).

The historically low number of new entrants to the Top 500 is particularly noteworthy given the unprecedented scale of investments in advanced computing infrastructure these days. According to estimates by Epoch AI cited by the Top 500 authors, the amount of AI compute capacity added globally every quarter has increased by 2.5X per year. Essentially, the industry's priorities have shifted from construction of machines capable of solving exceptionally complex scientific problems to deployment of vast amounts of compute capacity for commercial AI workloads.

In fact, the world's installed AI compute capacity already exceeds the equivalent of 15 million Nvidia H100 GPUs, according to Epoch AI. Furthermore, as of the fourth quarter of 2025, five major American hyperscalers accounted for an estimated 71% of the world's AI compute resources, much of which was deployed in massive clusters. While demand for advanced computing hardware is at an all-time high, it appears that the vast majority of new hardware never makes it to the world's most known supercomputer ranking.

#10225. Preventing API Contract Drift in TypeScript Applications

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A frontend application can compile successfully and still fail when it communicates with the backend.

Imagine a backend developer changes an API response from:

The backend works correctly. The frontend also builds without errors. However, a component expecting user.name now receives undefined.

This is an example of API contract drift: the frontend and backend no longer agree on the structure or behavior of an API.

The problem becomes more difficult when several developers or teams work on different parts of an application. A change made by one team may affect another team's assumptions without producing an obvious error during development.

TypeScript helps catch many programming mistakes, but its types do not automatically validate data received over HTTP. A type declaration can describe what the application expects without proving that the server actually returns it.

In this tutorial, we'll look at a practical way to make API contracts more reliable.

Consider a typical frontend API function:

The function promises to return a User, so TypeScript allows the application to access properties such as name and email.

The return value of response.json() is not automatically checked against the User interface. The interface only provides compile-time information; it does not validate the actual JSON received from the server.

the frontend may still treat the response as a User even though the data has a different structure.

This is particularly important when working with:

Independently deployed frontend and backend applications.

APIs maintained by different engineering teams.

The solution is to validate data at the boundary where it enters the application.

Zod is a TypeScript-first schema validation library. It allows developers to describe expected data structures and validate actual values against those definitions.

Now create a schema for the user response:

First, UserSchema describes the structure that the application expects at runtime.

Second, z.infer generates the corresponding TypeScript type from the schema.

This means developers do not need to maintain a separate interface and validation definition for the same data structure.

The response is initially treated as unknown, which prevents the application from assuming that untrusted data already has the expected structure.

UserSchema.parse() validates the actual response.

If the backend returns an unexpected property type or misses a required field, Zod throws a validation error instead of allowing invalid data to move silently through the application.

You can also use safeParse() when you prefer to handle validation errors without exceptions:

This approach is useful when API responses are external or when a frontend must provide more controlled error handling.

One common mistake is placing API validation logic directly inside React components.

For a small application, this may seem convenient. As the project grows, however, it becomes difficult to reuse validation rules and identify where data transformations happen.

A better approach is to separate API schemas, service functions, and UI components.

The responsibilities are straightforward:

schemas/user.ts defines the expected user data.

api/client.ts handles common HTTP configuration.

api/users.ts contains user-related API requests.

hooks/useUser.ts manages data fetching for React.

components/UserProfile.tsx displays the validated data.

This structure makes it easier to change an API implementation without rewriting the UI.

It also makes testing more manageable because validation logic can be tested independently of React rendering.

For more ideas on organizing application code, see SitePoint's guide to modern JavaScript modules.

Runtime validation protects an application when data arrives. However, teams also need a shared description of what an API is supposed to accept and return.

OpenAPI provides a standard format for describing HTTP APIs, including:

For example, an endpoint can be described using an OpenAPI document:

This document describes what the endpoint should return.

Both frontend and backend developers can refer to the same contract instead of relying on separate assumptions or outdated documentation.

Swagger Editor helps developers write and validate OpenAPI documents. It also provides a convenient way to inspect API definitions.

Swagger UI turns an OpenAPI document into interactive API documentation, allowing developers to explore endpoints and test requests.

Redoc provides another way to generate readable API documentation from an OpenAPI specification.

These tools are particularly useful when APIs are maintained by multiple developers or consumed by several applications.

Maintaining API types manually across frontend and backend repositories creates opportunities for inconsistencies.

For example, a backend developer might add a new optional property while the frontend continues using an older interface.

One way to reduce this duplication is to generate TypeScript definitions and API clients from an OpenAPI specification.

Orval is a useful tool for this workflow.

It can generate TypeScript clients and types from an OpenAPI document, helping frontend developers work with consistent endpoint definitions.

A typical configuration might look like this:

The exact configuration depends on the Orval version and the structure of the OpenAPI document.

Once configured, the client can be regenerated when the API specification changes.

This reduces the amount of repetitive code developers need to maintain manually.

However, generated types should not be confused with runtime validation. They improve consistency during development, but an application may still need to validate responses received from external systems or services that cannot be fully trusted.

Frontend developers often need to build or test features before the corresponding backend endpoint is ready.

Waiting for a complete backend implementation can slow down development, particularly when multiple teams work independently.

Mock Service Worker (MSW) helps solve this problem by intercepting network requests and returning controlled responses.

You can define a mock handler for the user endpoint:

The frontend can now make normal HTTP requests while MSW supplies predictable responses during development or testing.

You can also simulate an invalid response:

This makes it possible to check whether the application's validation and error-handling logic behave as expected.

MSW is particularly valuable because it allows frontend tests to run without requiring a live backend environment.

Runtime validation and generated clients improve reliability, but they do not automatically guarantee that independently developed services remain compatible.

For systems with multiple services, contract testing can provide another layer of protection.

Pact is a contract-testing tool that allows consumers and providers to verify their expectations about API interactions.

Consider a frontend application that expects the user endpoint to return:

The consumer defines the expected interaction.

The backend provider can then run verification against that contract to check whether its actual response satisfies the consumer's expectations.

This helps identify breaking changes before they reach production.

These tools solve different problems. A project does not necessarily need all of them, but larger applications can benefit from combining the approaches that match their architecture.

A contract is only useful if developers consistently check it when making changes.

A practical approach is to run validation and tests automatically whenever someone opens a pull request.

For example, a GitHub Actions workflow might look like this:

The api:validate script should be configured to validate the project's OpenAPI document or run the relevant contract checks.

The test command should execute the application's automated tests.

For a production project, you may also want to include:

The objective is to detect incompatible changes before they are merged.

A failed pipeline gives developers an opportunity to investigate the change while the context is still fresh.

API contracts become harder to maintain when several teams contribute to the same system.

For example, a company might have separate teams responsible for:

If nobody owns an API contract, small changes can create unexpected problems for other teams.

Assigning clear ownership helps establish who reviews changes, maintains documentation, and coordinates breaking updates.

This becomes particularly important when an organization expands its engineering capacity across locations or adds several specialized teams. Companies planning that kind of growth may also need to consider enterprise IT recruitment as part of their wider engineering workforce strategy.

Regardless of how a team is assembled, its development process should establish clear responsibilities for API design, versioning, testing, and release coordination.

A few practical conventions can make a significant difference:

Assign an owner to every important API or service.

Document breaking changes before implementation.

Require contract-related tests in pull requests.

Keep generated clients synchronized with the API specification.

Communicate deprecations before removing existing fields.

Monitor production errors after deploying API changes.

These practices help prevent a growing engineering organization from becoming a collection of disconnected services.

API contract drift is not simply a TypeScript problem. It is a coordination problem that becomes visible when applications and services develop different expectations about the same data.

TypeScript provides useful compile-time checks, but it cannot guarantee that an HTTP response matches a declared interface.

A more reliable approach combines several techniques:

Use OpenAPI to document API expectations.

Generate TypeScript clients with tools such as Orval.

Use MSW to test frontend behavior without a live backend.

Use Pact when consumer-provider contract verification is appropriate.

Run validation and tests automatically in CI/CD.

You do not need to introduce every tool at once. Start by validating external API responses, document important endpoints, and gradually automate contract checks as the application grows.

The result is a codebase where developers can make changes with greater confidence and where frontend and backend teams have a shared understanding of how their systems communicate.

#10224. Pixel Tag is a brilliant Band-Aid on a Find Hub network Google can’t easily fix

The Google Pixel Tag is here and, at least so far, it’s really good. But fresh hardware and some new features can’t fix the core problem. Google’s Find Hub network has been restricted from the very start, and it’s not something Google can easily fix.

9to5Google Weekender by Ben Schoon is a part of our free newsletter that also includes ‘Inbox,’ a twice-weekly roundup of key stories from around the web by Daniel Bader. Sign up here to get Weekender delivered to your inbox every Sunday along with ‘Inbox’ on Tuesday and Thursday. You can find past issues here.

The good news on Pixel Tag: this is a well-built tracker that, unlike others, is the clear product of the hardware having direct access to the software. Little touches like being able to adjust ringer volume on the fly combine nicely with UWB – the latter something we rarely see on third-party devices largely due to Apple – all things that elevate the Find Hub tracker to what we called the new “gold standard.”

The bad news: this is still a Find Hub tracker.

Google’s Find Hub network has improved dramatically over the two years since its launch. Where Google originally had to admit that tracking performance was lacking (a nice way of putting it, frankly), improvements started arriving in 2025. We noticed that the network seemed to be working much better a few months after its wide rollout, and Google later confirmed speed improvements.

But, ultimately, the Find Hub network is still reeling from how it debuted.

Again, it’s gotten better since then, but it’s still not as good as it should be. Samsung’s network, limited solely to Galaxy phones, shouldn’t outperform Google’s. Find Hub trackers should be just as good as AirTags and the third-party trackers that work with Apple’s network. They’re just not, and I truly do believe it all boils down to this initial choice. One of the reasons this is getting better is that Google has been pushing users to the “all areas” setting during the setup of a new device – I’ve noticed this on some recent devices I set up, but it’s not been completely consistent just yet. That’s a good step forward but, even if it is the new default, that still requires millions of users to upgrade their devices, and we’ve never been in a better time to not upgrade your phone.

All of that is to say, the Pixel Tag isn’t fixing this problem. Surely, it’ll give Google some needed fire to fix the problem, but new hardware isn’t the solution. That’s something I was discussing with 9to5Google readers on our review. UWB is a tentpole feature of the Pixel Tag, but it doesn’t change the network problems, it just improves precision finding. We needed a first-party tracker like this regardless of the state of the network, something that really takes advantage of the platform and pushes it forward, but it’s just a quick fix, a Band-Aid.

What do you think? Have you found the Find Hub network reliable enough, or is there still room for improvement? Sound off in the comments below!

Google this week launched yet another new Gemini model, Hydrogen, which is a refresh of its Flash tier model and a “major standalone upgrade” on top of Gemini 3.8 Flash. Hydrogen is already powering AI features in Google Search.

Perhaps the biggest story in tech this week, Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang is out following not just an affair with a suspected Chinese spy, but two additional employee affairs beyond that were exposed.

#10222. Hill-Climbing Agent Quality with Agents CLI Evaluation

A live walkthrough of the Agents CLI eval loop, from a first run to regression tracking, model-backend experiments, and automated alerting

Building an agent that works on a demo is a different problem from building an agent that stays working across model upgrades, tool changes, and new conversation shapes. The first takes a weekend; the second takes a loop. A team writes test cases, runs them, grades them, reads what failed, changes the agent, and runs the cases again. If the score goes up, the change was good. If it goes down, revert. Over weeks of this, the agent inches toward the ceiling of what the model, tools, and prompt can do together. The quality-engineering name for this is hill-climbing.

The Agents CLI eval namespace was built around this loop. The commands are small and framework-agnostic: eval generate runs the agent against a dataset of cases, eval grade scores the generated traces, eval compare diffs two run results side by side, eval analyze clusters failures into themes, and eval optimize runs an automated hill-climbing pass on the agent's prompt.

Below: baseline → regression → model swap → scheduling and alerting.

The scaffold used throughout is the LangChain template that ships with Agents CLI v1.8.0. The setup is a single command:

The scaffold ships with an eval dataset at tests/eval/datasets/basic-dataset.json, a config at tests/eval/eval_config.yaml, and a custom LLM-as-judge metric at tests/eval/response_quality.py. The config declares the metric to run:

The metric grades each response on a 1-to-5 scale for accuracy, relevance, and clarity, using gemini-3.8-flash as the judge. The default dataset has a single trivial case, which is not enough for a hill-climb. The first thing we do is expand it to four cases that probe different behaviors:

Four cases: a greeting, a weather question the mock tool handles with the hardcoded foggy response, a weather question for a city the tool defaults on, and an off-topic question that the agent has to decide how to handle. This is a small dataset by design; the hill-climbing loop pays off when the dataset is small enough to run on every change.

With the dataset in place, we run eval generate to drive the agent against each case, then eval grade to score the resulting traces:

Mean 4.0 out of 5 across four cases, with a standard deviation of 1.41. That stdev tells us immediately that the cases are not scoring uniformly, which is a healthier starting point than a flat 5.0 across the board. A flat 5.0 either means the agent is perfect (unlikely) or the dataset is too easy to show variation. 4.0 with real spread is a hill to climb.

To demonstrate the loop, we introduce a realistic regression. A developer working across several agent projects accidentally pastes the wrong system prompt into our weather agent: a chef persona meant for a different codebase. In app/agent.py:

The change compiles, the agent still runs, and nothing in the pre-eval pipeline (lint, unit tests, type checks) catches it. Re-running the same eval generate plus eval grade sequence produces:

Mean 2.75, down from 4.0. The judge agrees with our intuition that a chef persona on a weather agent is a worse product than a general-purpose helpful assistant. The stdev also tightened from 1.41 to 0.96, consistent with the chef prompt pushing most cases toward the same mediocre range.

A 1.25-point drop on a 1-to-5 scale is substantial, and the kind of regression that can slip through code review when the diff looks small ("one string literal changed"). The loop surfaced the change at all, with a specific number attached to it, before the agent reached production.

Mean scores are a start; the per-case diff is where the hill-climb happens. eval compare reads two result files and produces a structured JSON diff:

The output lists which keys changed and which stayed the same:

The differences object has the full per-case breakdown with the baseline and candidate values side by side. For a hill-climbing workflow, we typically pipe the output through jq or a small Python script to extract the signal; the raw JSON is the complete evidence.

Walking the two result files and lining up the scores per case shows where the regression landed:

Three cases dropped, one held. The most important of the three is sf_weather: the chef persona pulled the model's attention away from the registered get_weather tool, so the agent stopped calling it and started making up weather values. Unit tests can't catch this: the code runs, the tool is still registered, but the model's behavior around it has shifted. Only an end-to-end eval that scores the response in context surfaces this.

The mean drop by itself (−1.25) is a clear signal, but the per-case breakdown is what tells the developer where to look. A one-line system-prompt change propagated through three different cases in three different ways: greeting lost a point on tone, sf_weather lost two on tool usage, off_topic lost two on scope creep.

A common hill-climbing question is whether a cheaper or faster model would do the job acceptably. For the LangChain template, the model is defined in app/agent.py:

We swap gemini-3.8-flash for gemini-2.5-flash (restoring the helpful system prompt first), re-run eval generate plus eval grade, and compare the two result files. The mean drops from 4.00 to 2.75, the same magnitude as the chef-prompt regression, but the per-case breakdown tells a completely different story:

The cheaper model matched 3.8-flash exactly on the greeting and rome_weather cases and lost a point on sf_weather (slightly less polished phrasing). The whole visible drop traces to off_topic, where the cheaper model refused with a hedge about the event "not having happened yet" (a training-cutoff artifact), and the judge marked it harshly.

The lesson is that the summary number is misleading. Look only at the mean and you'd call gemini-2.5-flash as bad as the chef prompt. Look at the per-case breakdown and you'd call it a reasonable swap for most of the agent's workload, with one case (handling out-of-scope questions about recent events) exposing a real limitation. If the production distribution of user questions matches the first three cases more than the fourth, the swap is worth considering. If off_topic-style questions are common, it is not.

Two caveats on this experiment. First, model swaps interact with the prompt: a prompt tuned for a stronger model often leaks capability when ported to a weaker one, so the right comparison is "weaker model with a prompt tuned for it" versus "stronger model with its own tuned prompt." Second, the Vertex AI Eval Service calls a judge model to grade the traces, and the judge is held constant across the comparison. Changing the judge is a different experiment and should be done separately.

Agents CLI does not ship a scheduler, but the eval commands are designed to drop into standard infrastructure. The pattern we can use here is a cron job or Cloud Scheduler trigger that runs eval generate plus eval grade on a cadence and writes the results to a persistent location:

A nightly run against a stable regression dataset catches drift from model updates, data changes in the agent's tools, and dependencies that shift under the agent's feet. For a production agent, the scheduled run also captures the model vendor's quiet upgrades, which can move the agent's behavior without any change on our side. The dataset for the scheduled run is typically larger than the one used in the inner loop (dozens to hundreds of cases rather than a few), since it only runs once a day.

The scheduled run produces a result file every day, but a file nobody looks at is not an alert. The pattern here can be a wrapper script that compares each new run's mean against a baseline (the previous run, or a committed known-good) and exits non-zero when the drop exceeds a threshold. The non-zero exit is what triggers the alert from the surrounding scheduler.

Running it against our regression demo (baseline 4.0, candidate 2.75 from the chef-prompt run):

And against an identical pair (self-compare):

The non-zero exit code is what a cron job, Cloud Scheduler, or GitHub Actions step picks up to send the alert.

For ADK projects, eval optimize runs an automated hill-climbing pass on the agent's prompt using the GEPA framework (not currently supported for the LangChain extension). The command takes a dataset and a target metric, proposes prompt variants, grades each one, keeps the variants that improve the metric, and iterates. The output is a set of candidate prompts ranked by their eval score, which a human then reviews and promotes to the agent definition.

Two things to understand about eval optimize. First, it is scoped to prompt optimization, not to arbitrary agent changes: the agent's tools, structure, and model stay fixed. Second, GEPA sometimes finds genuine improvements and sometimes finds prompt variants that score higher on the specific metric by overfitting to its wording. The reviewer is the control. For projects where the loop applies, the discipline is to run eval optimize on the regression dataset (where improvements are measurable) and to review the candidate prompts carefully before promotion.

A few patterns and anti-patterns from running this loop ourselves and watching teams do the same.

Lead with a small, representative dataset, not a comprehensive one. The temptation when starting an eval is to try to cover every edge case the agent will ever see. The result is a dataset that takes an hour to run and that nobody runs on a change. Four cases that run in under a minute, as above, exercise the main behaviors and get run on every change. A regression dataset of three hundred cases that runs for an hour becomes a release gate at best and shelfware at worst.

Snapshot result files, not scores. The score is a summary; the result file is the evidence. When a regression surfaces three weeks after a prompt change, having the full result JSON from before and after the change (not just the mean score) is what lets eval compare show the specific case that moved. The per-case breakdown in Step 4 above would not have been possible from scores alone.

Separate the dataset from its expectations. The dataset is the inputs. The eval config is the expectations. They evolve on different cadences: the dataset grows as new real-world cases surface in production, and the config evolves as the team's definition of "good" matures. Keeping them in separate files, both committed to version control, makes it clear which half changed when a score moves.

Treat summary metrics with suspicion. The model-swap experiment in Step 5 is the clearest example. The mean dropped by half a point, but the per-case breakdown showed the drop was concentrated in one case while the others held or improved. A team that only looked at the mean would have made the wrong call.

More evals do not automatically lead to more fixes. A recent controlled study we ran found that coding agents using agents-cli run four times as many evals as coding agents without it, but change the agent after an eval about as often as the baseline. The behavior gain comes from what the skills put into the build at the start, not from the inner iteration. Evals are necessary for quality, but the quality comes from acting on what the evals show.

Scaffold an Agents CLI project and run the first eval loop in a handful of commands:

The full eval namespace is documented at google.github.io/agents-cli/cli. The source code, including the first-party eval primitives, is at github.com/google/agents-cli.

Technology has changed rapidly over the last few decades. While we've got hardware that's so much more powerful than what came 10 or 20 years ago, there's still a lot of enjoyment to be had in the classics. That's why there's a huge market for retro hardware, from game controllers designed to look like classic gamepads from old consoles, to full re-releases of old machines, with plenty of fun (and well-priced) accessories in between.

If you're buying for a retro aficionado, you're going to want to pick up something that's fun to use and has a serious nostalgic edge. There's a whole host of gear out there for those who prefer an '80s or '90s aesthetic, rather than the products we live with today.

#10217. Google Maps rolling out travel itineraries & list search for Ask Maps

Ahead of the holiday season, Google Maps is launching a handy travel itineraries feature alongside a slew of other new capabilities for Ask Maps.

Ask Maps can now build travel itineraries that feature numbered map pins for each stop. It supports everything from shopping excursions to multi-day road trips with several stops. When used for the latter, Google Maps will generate a day-by-day schedule with tips and the numbered pins that aid visualization. Plans can be shared with others and exported to Google Docs on desktop.

Once on the trip, Ask Maps will show directions and real-time details, including live traffic conditions, whether a place is open, and “even what the vibe is at your next stop.” You can conversationally have Google add more stops to the itinerary.

Meanwhile, Ask Maps can now tap into your saved lists to find bookmarked places and take action. This lets you ask: “Which spots saved in my lists have availability for five people in the next 30 minutes?”

Before you start directions, you’ll see familiar “Know Before You Go” tips that surface information about your route, like parking locations, car charging options, weather, potential delays, and tolls.

New agentic capabilities let Ask Maps find online beauty, pet care, auto, and home repair appointments, as well as call local businesses on your behalf. For example, “Find me a 4 p.m. nail appointment near me” returns a list of open slots.

Finally, Ask Maps is expanding to 30 more countries across Europe, including Spain, Germany, and the UK.

These updates are rolling out starting today on Google Maps for Android and iOS. Travel itineraries and list search are available in all 200+ Ask Maps countries. Know Before You Go in directions and appointment booking are coming first to the US.

#10215. Save serious money with RedPocket Mobile’s $5 Black Friday plans

You’re not going to believe this, but there’s one part of your life that’s not actually getting more expensive. If you switch to RedPocket Mobile during its Black Friday sale, you can ditch your costly carrier and get a plan that starts at $5 per month. It’s not too good to be true: RedPocket has been around for 20 years, and unlike most MVNOs, it’s independently owned and operated. That gives it the ability to offer you real network choice when you sign up for a new plan (we’ll explain more later), and keeps your costs low. Really low.

But you’re reading this on 9to5Google, so how does it impact you as an Android user? That’s a great question.

RedPocket is offering $150 rebates on Android devices like the Pixel 11, Galaxy S26, Galaxy Z Flip 7, Moto G Play, and more. (You can also get $100 rebates for iPhones if you’re into that sort of thing.)

These offers run from November 9 through the 30th at redpocket.com, so don’t wait too long to save big.

It’s OK if you haven’t heard of RedPocket Mobile, because it has been quietly working for 20 years to improve the way people use their phones. Founded in 2006, the company works with all three major US carriers — AT&T, T-Mobile, and Verizon — and because it doesn’t own any of its towers, it can pass along significant savings to its customers. Here’s what the company says on its website:

RedPocket was built for price-conscious consumers who want real value and freedom from rigid, high-cost contracts. As a no-contract carrier, we’ve stayed focused on making reliable, transparent, and flexible phone service for everyone. Our plans are designed to fit real lifestyles, whether you’re a heavy data user, a frequent traveler, or just looking to cut your monthly bill.

Most MVNOs are owned by the Big Three, which means you don’t really have a choice of network when you sign up. But RedPocket works with all three major carriers and lets you choose which one works best for your location and usage patterns.

RedPocket offers a tool called CoverageGenius that helps you decide which network is right for you. While you can’t switch between networks once your line is set up, it’s a great way to figure out where to start.

I teased that $5/month cost at the beginning of the article, but here’s the real breakdown: you can get a full year of service for $60 if you’re a light data user. RedPocket’s Annual Essentials and Annual Plus plans get you unlimited calling and texting in the US, along with calling and data roaming in dozens of countries around the world. The main difference is how much data you get: 3GB for Essentials and 20GB for Plus.

Annual Premium ups the ante to unlimited data, plus calling and data roaming in even more countries.

Across all three plans, you’re saving between 33% and 50% off the regular price when you sign up between November 9th and 30th.

I even made you a table to make it easier to understand: 👇

One thing to note about all of these plans: the annual prices are paid upfront, not billed monthly. Promotional pricing applies to the first year, and after that, annual plans renew at $110 for Essentials, $220 for Plus, and $330 for Premium.

RedPocket understands that it may be hard to commit to a full year of service even if it’s super cheap. That’s why it offers monthly plans, too, that can serve as a trial for any of the networks. It costs just $10 for the first month to try any of the three plans, with Monthly Plus rising to $20/month after that and Premium $30/month thereafter (Essentials stays at $10/month).

If you need a new phone to go along with your new plan, RedPocket is a great place to get it.

During the Black Friday deal period, you can get a $150 rebate on the following phones:

Legal stuff: Rebates are paid out 90 days after service begins on a new line activation with an eligible plan. Full rebate terms at redpocket.com/rebate. Devices are available while supplies last.

Usually with sponsored content, I have to spend the whole time convincing you that the product is worth buying. But RedPocket’s Black Friday deals do all that work on their own.

Not only do you get amazing discounts for the first 12 months of service by prepaying for a full year, but RedPocket’s “Lock-in Low” promise means that you get to stay as close to that promotional price as possible once the first year is over.

There’s no contract and no port-in requirement. You can keep your number, keep your phone, and activate with an eSIM on compatible devices. During the event, there’s even a Refer-a-Friend credit that gets you $25 for every person you bring to the service.

Explore RedPocket’s plans or compare the annual offers. This is one of the best Black Friday decisions you’re going to make this month, and maybe even ever.

#10214. 10 Best AI App Builders I've Used in 2026 (and What Each One Gets Wrong)

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI App Builders are exploding in popularity, and for good reason. It’s never been easier to build real, functional apps and internal tools using app builders. To test them all, I built the same app on 10 of the most popular AI app builders and tested each across usability, cost, correctness, and ease of use. Most of them can turn a paragraph of description into something that looks like software within a single session. The differences show up around the tenth prompt, when you learn what each edit costs and who has to fix the code once something breaks. I took every price from the vendor's own pricing page on October 29, 2026.

TLDR: Of the best AI app builders I've used, Softr is the one I'd hand to a team that needs auth, data, user groups or integrations. The other nine I tested include code generators such as Lovable and Bolt and visual platforms such as Bubble and Glide, and each one carries a cost or upkeep trade-off worth seeing before you commit.

I looked at the same seven things in every tool, from the first prompt onward:

Softr gets used a lot by operations and business teams as their AI app builder for secure client portals, internal tools and other business apps, none of which require code. Write a short brief in your own words, and the AI Co-Builder turns it into a database plus the core app that sits on it: tables, pages and layouts, the data connections behind them, plus user groups and permissions, so real users can sign in from day one. Workflows come as the next step, either by prompting the Co-Builder again or by adding them in the visual editor.

The Free plan is $0 for unlimited apps, with room for 5 app users, 3 builders, 5,000 records and 5 AI credits each month. Basic runs $19 per month, Pro $99 and Business $329, all on annual billing. Enterprise pricing is custom and includes SSO and SCIM for organizations managing access at scale. The full plan table sits on Softr's pricing page.

Lovable builds full-stack web apps through conversation, writing React code while it takes care of hosting, SSL and the backend. It's designed to move a founder or product team from a prompt to a shareable prototype, and the preview updates as the agent works.

Base44 calls itself a no-code AI platform: you explain the app, and it generates the structure, design and logic while running the backend itself. Authentication, a database and analytics come on every plan, Free included.

Base44 is free for 25 message credits a month. Starter costs $16 per month and Builder $40 per month, both on annual billing, with Pro at $80 and Elite at $160.

Bolt is a coding agent that writes full-stack projects in the browser and runs them on Bolt Cloud, which supplies hosting, databases and integrations. It suits people who want to watch the code take shape and edit it themselves.

Bolt Free is $0 with 1M tokens a month and Bolt branding. Pro costs $25 per month with 10M tokens, and Teams runs $30 a month for each member on monthly billing.

Replit pairs its Agent with a cloud workspace, so a description turns into a running app whose sign-in, database, hosting and monitoring come wired up. The homepage pitches it to people who don't code, though the result underneath is a code project.

Core costs $20 per month ($18 with annual billing) and Pro costs $100 per month ($90 with annual billing). Enterprise is custom and adds SSO, SCIM and audit logs.

Glide starts internal business apps from whatever you hand it, whether a spreadsheet, a written prompt or a PDF, and its newer platform, GlideOS, reads a sheet's structure and relationships to draft screens and workflows. Its apps grew up on phones, and that heritage still shapes the product.

Glide Free is $0 with limited credits. Basic costs $25 per month, Plus $50 per month and Pro $125 per month; annual billing isn't offered yet, according to Glide.

Bubble Free gives you 50K workload units and 500 AI credits each month. Web plans run $32 per month for Starter, $119 for Growth and $349 for Team, all on annual billing.

Retool is an internal tools engine aimed at engineering teams. You describe or assemble an app that connects straight to production databases, APIs and LLMs, with auth and access controls in place at deployment.

v0 generates full-stack Next.js web apps from a prompt and deploys them to Vercel, with Design Mode for visual tweaks and GitHub sync for the code. It's designed around a developer's workflow.

v0's Free plan gives new users $5 of one-time credits and caps messages at 7 per day. Plus runs $30 a month per user, and Business $100 a month per user.

Figma Make turns prompts and Figma design context into code-backed prototypes and web apps you can publish at a URL. It can also scaffold a Payload backend with a database, APIs, auth and an admin panel, or connect to Supabase for sign-in and stored data.

Figma's Starter plan is free with up to 500 AI credits a month. Figma lists a Professional Full seat at $16 per month with 3,000 AI credits, and Organization Full seats cost $55 per month on annual billing.

Collins Dictionary named "vibe coding" its Word of the Year for 2025, and the phrase now covers any workflow where you describe software in plain language and an AI produces it. AI app builders package that workflow with hosting, data and sign-in, so a non-developer can get from idea to something clickable without installing anything.

The questions buyers ask have turned plainer since then: what each edit costs, who keeps the app running, and whether a client can sign in and see their own records alone. A generated front end makes a good demo, and a team using it every day needs permissions and a predictable bill for the next hundred edits.

Softr is now my default when recommending AI app builders. It handles teams, auth, and real company data better than any other product. The AI Co-Builder drafts tables, pages and user groups, workflows follow as the next step, and the app keeps running on maintained platform components with no codebase to patch. App-user pricing and an editor that works without AI credits keep later changes from turning into a running meter.

Three situations call for a different tool. Glide makes a better mobile-first choice when the app spends its days on phones in the field. Bubble gives more front-end design freedom when a project calls for bespoke layouts, interactive animation or heavy brand styling. If your engineers want version control and audit logs and can handle a technical setup, Retool covers that need.

Agents are moving inside the apps these tools produce. Gartner expects task-specific AI agents inside 40% of enterprise applications by the close of 2026, against under 5% in 2025, so a 2027 app builder will need to host agents that respect the same permissions as human users.

Building is also leaving the builder's own window. The Model Context Protocol now sits under the Linux Foundation's Agentic AI Foundation and reports more than 97 million SDK downloads a month, with client support in ChatGPT and Claude, among others. Softr's changelog dates its official Claude and ChatGPT connector to October 6, 2026, and I expect more teams to start an app from the chat assistant they already use.

The caution comes from Gartner too: it predicts organizations will scrap upward of 40% of agentic AI projects before 2028, citing escalating costs, unclear business value and inadequate risk controls. Going into 2027, I'd judge any builder on whether it keeps agent costs and access rules visible to the team running the app.

Neville Naidoo is a technology journalist covering AI, startups, and the digital economy, with a focus on how emerging technologies are constantly evolving and shaping our everyday life.

#10210. Someone rebuilt old Android versions in your browser, and it’s an instant nostalgia hit [Gallery]

The “Android Time Machine” is a fun project that puts Android versions into your browser, giving you a quick and easy chance to play around with older versions for a quick hit of nostalgia.

Built on the back of AOSP builds for classic Nexus devices, “Android Time Machine” lets you quickly access older Android versions. Specifically, Android 2.1 Eclair through Android 7.1.2 Nougat.

The simulator runs within the shell of a matching Nexus smartphone on a desktop browser, or to fit your actual smartphone if you run it on Android.

Versions and simulated smartphones include:

The best part here is how it works on your actual smartphone. When launched in fullscreen, it scales to match your phone’s screen – meaning it fits even devices like the Galaxy Z Fold 8’s smaller outer display (it just shows the desktop version when open).

Since this is running through the browser and not an actual Android build, you won’t get a perfect replication. Performance is a little hit or miss, especially with certain animations, but it works really well overall.

The browser even simulates results from each era (oh, to go back to when Google Search wasn’t an absolute mess). Similarly, Google apps and the camera are filled with placeholders since the web app can’t access the actual camera or connect to the internet to sign into a Google account.

Seeing things like the boot animations and live wallpapers from classic Nexus phones, as well as peeking back at lost apps like Google+ and Play Music, is just such a fun little piece of nostalgia.

“Android Time Machine” is free, and the source code is available on GitHub.

#10206. Pixel Journal update rolls out Recorder integration, file export [U]

Following the last update in August, Google is rolling out a sizable update to Pixel Journal that adds Recorder audio entries and file exports.

Update 11/16: Updates for Pixel Journal (version 2026.10.27.x) and Pixel Recorder (4.2.20261001.x) are widely rolling out. After installing (be sure to open the apps and Force stop), you’ll see “Record new audio” and “Add existing recording” in the Add item menu.

The first option directly launches the Recorder app into a new session, while the second brings up a list of previous recordings. The Journal app will show an inline audio player and transcript, which can be removed.

Curiously, these updates weren’t mentioned as part of the November 2026 Pixel Drop.

Journal November 2026 updates:• Recorder integration: Record new audio or add existing recordings from Pixel Recorder, complete with playable clips and transcripts• Journal export: Export your entries and media to view offline anytime

Original 11/10: From the “Add item” plus menu in entries, you’ll see “Record new audio” join the existing Take photo/video, Add photo, Health Connect activity, and location & map options.

At the moment, this audio integration is not live as Pixel Recorder also requires an update that we’re not seeing in the Play Store yet.

Being able to add a “voice entry with audio” is a pretty common feature for journaling apps. A transcription capability looks to be available for Google’s implementation.

Meanwhile, in Pixel Journal settings, there’s a new “Export your journal” option. This creates a ZIP file of all your entries, including images. Text is exported as HTML files.

Version 2026.10.27.x of Pixel Journal is still rolling out via Google Play today.

#10201. Samsung Galaxy’s next major update keeps leaking – here’s what to expect in One UI 9.5

Samsung is preparing its next major update for Galaxy phones, One UI 9.5, and new details have been coming to light. Here’s an overview of a lot of One UI 9.5 updates that have leaked so far.

One UI 9.5 will still be based on Android 17, but it’s set to be packed with various changes based on recent leaks. In particular, it seems like Samsung has a ton of new AI features in store.

“Galaxy Claw” is supposedly Samsung’s new “personal agent,” able to leverage apps on your device including your notifications, Contacts, Gmail, Messages, and more. Specifically, Android Authority reports it will be able to use Calendar, Contacts, Gallery, Gmail, Messages, Notifications, Perplexity, Phone, and Voice Recorder, with the agent able to set up scheduled tasks. It does sound like this will be a paid feature, though, with Samsung’s new $4.99/month AI Premium plan. There would also be a new dashboard for showing AI usage.

There’s also a new Memory feature in Bixby that would let the assistant reference past details from conversations or commands.

Daily Brief, sharing its name with Google’s canceled Pixel feature, would be able to summarize notifications from various apps, grouping cards into “Finance and payments,” “Bookings and appointments,” and “Work email,” though that list seems incomplete.
