#10200. Top 6 AI Detector Tools in 2026: Features, Accuracy Claims and When to Trust Them

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI writing tools are now part of everyday work for students, marketers, developers and editors, and a parallel category of tools has appeared to answer one question: was this text written by a person or a model? Teachers, publishers, hiring teams and content managers use these detectors to screen submissions, but the technology is probabilistic, its accuracy claims vary, and the cost of a wrong call can be high.

This guide compares six AI detector tools, CudekAI, GPTZero, Copyleaks, Originality.ai, Turnitin and Sapling, on what their own pages state. It then explains how detectors work, summarizes the research on their limits, and ends with a short script you can use to test any detector on your own samples. The tools are listed in no ranking order. Feature details, limits and prices change often, so confirm current information on each vendor’s site before you commit.

• A detector gives a probability, not proof. Every vendor in this list warns that results can be wrong, and several advise against using a score alone.

• Limits differ widely. Character limits, supported languages, models detected and free allowances vary between tools and even between pages of the same tool.

• Bias and short text are known weak spots. Research has found detectors misclassify some non-native English writing, and most vendors say accuracy improves with longer text.

• Vendor accuracy claims are not comparable. Figures come from different datasets, thresholds and models, so test on your own material.

• Use detection as one signal. Combine it with drafts, version history, interviews or other evidence before acting on a result.

CudekAI’s AI detector returns an AI percentage and a human percentage for pasted text, with paragraph, sentence and word-level analysis for mixed documents. According to its page, it also accepts PDF and Word files with per-page scores and downloadable reports, and offers separate code and image detectors. The page lists models it says it detects, including ChatGPT, Gemini, Claude, Llama, DeepSeek and Grok, and says it supports more than 100 languages.

Strengths: sentence-level highlighting, file uploads with reports, and extra detectors for code and images in the same product.

Limitations and gaps: the page shows different character limits in different places, including 1,000, 5,000 and 15,000, so check the limit that applies to your plan. It says to start free but does not state a free allowance, and it lists detection as costing one credit. A headline accuracy figure of 99 percent appears in its page metadata, while the body refers to independent benchmarking without naming sources, and its FAQ says accuracy may vary for mixed or paraphrased text and that false positives can occur. API access is listed under an enterprise plan.

Fits: writers and teams who want a quick check with sentence-level detail and file reports, and who will verify results rather than rely on a single percentage.

GPTZero gives an overall score and highlights flagged sentences, and adds a Writing Replay feature that records the writing process, including typing patterns and paste events. It also lists a plagiarism checker and integrations with Google Docs, Google Classroom, Canvas, Zapier and a Chrome extension, plus an API.

Strengths: process-based evidence through Writing Replay, strong education integrations and published benchmark figures. On the RAID benchmark, the page reports 95.7 percent of AI text detected and 1 percent of human text wrongly flagged.

Limitations and gaps: it claims full support for five languages (English, German, Portuguese, French and Spanish) and says other languages lack stated accuracy levels. The company’s own page says no detector is 100 percent accurate, that results should not be the final verdict, and that accuracy is higher on longer English text. The free scan box has a 10,000-character limit, and plan prices are not listed on the page.

Fits: educators and editors who want writing-process evidence as well as a score.

Copyleaks describes an AI detector covering more than 30 languages and models including ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini, DeepSeek and Llama. Its AI Logic feature explains why text was flagged, and it says it can detect AI-generated code. It offers a browser extension, a Google Docs add-on, learning-management-system integrations and an enterprise API.

Strengths: explanation of flagged text, code detection, broad integrations and published per-language accuracy figures, which range from 93.08 percent for Portuguese to 99.20 percent for English on AI text, according to the page.

Limitations and gaps: the figures are the vendor’s own. It claims a 0.03 percent false-positive rate and cites a 2023 study, so treat the number as a claim to verify on your data. Free scans cover up to 25,000 characters, but paid plan prices sit on a separate pricing page.

Fits: organizations that need multilingual coverage, code detection or integrations.

Originality.ai combines AI detection with a plagiarism checker and a fact and hallucination checker. It lists sentence-level highlights, a mixed AI and human category in its newer detection models, a Chrome extension with a writing replay feature and an AI detection API.

Strengths: several checks in one product, an API with documentation and a clear statement of limits. The page acknowledges false positives, says very short text under a 100-word minimum and AI rewriting tools can affect results, and advises against using the score alone.

Limitations and gaps: it is credit-based, free scans are capped at 100 credits per account and credits expire two years after purchase. Its accuracy claims cite third-party studies and internal benchmarks that you should review yourself.

Fits: publishers, agencies and SEO teams that want detection, plagiarism and fact checks in one workflow.

Turnitin is built for educators and institutions. Its AI writing detection estimates how much of a submission is human-written versus likely AI-generated, including likely AI-paraphrased content, and works inside learning-management systems alongside similarity checking.

Strengths: deep institutional integration and unusually explicit framing of results as probabilities. For documents above its 300-word minimum, the page reports false-positive rates of 0.014 for English language learners and 0.013 for native English writers and concludes there is no statistically significant bias. It also links guidance on handling suspected false positives.

Limitations and gaps: it is aimed at institutions rather than individual freelancers, the page does not give an overall false-positive figure, and it describes detection as just one piece of a broader challenge. Mention of language support is limited, with a linked post about Arabic.

Fits: schools and universities that already use Turnitin and want detection within the same workflow.

Sapling offers an AI content detector with a free check of the first 2,000 characters, and Pro and Enterprise accounts can check up to 100,000 characters. It provides a free API key and documentation, and it publishes unusually candid guidance on limits.

Strengths: transparent caveats and a developer-friendly API. The page says false positives and negatives may occur, that accuracy varies with dataset, text length and threshold, and advises measuring results on your own examples. It also warns that some tools claiming to be undetectable falsely say they passed its detector.

Limitations and gaps: accuracy improves after about 50 words, non-English text is scored by the English model for unsupported languages and is described as less reliable, and prices are not listed on the page. A reported 60 percent drop in false positives comes from internal evaluations that the page does not publish.

Fits: developers and teams that want to integrate detection and test it on their own data.

The table summarizes what each page states. It is not a benchmark, and “not stated” means the page did not say, not that the feature is absent.

• False positives. Human writing can be flagged, especially when it is short, formulaic or highly predictable.

• Bias risk. A research paper titled GPT detectors are biased against non-native English writers found that the detectors it tested consistently misclassified non-native English writing as AI-generated, and warned against using such detectors in educational settings. Turnitin’s page reports the opposite for its own tool, which is a reason to test on your population.

• Evasion. Paraphrasing or editing AI text can lower scores. The same research found that simple prompting strategies could both reduce bias and get text past detectors.

• Mixed authorship. A document that is partly AI-assisted can get a low overall score while individual sentences score high, as Sapling’s page points out.

• Moving targets. New models change the text distribution, so a tool trained on older models can lose accuracy.

• Shifting benchmarks. Vendors report different datasets and thresholds, so numbers are not comparable.

OpenAI’s own experience is a useful reminder. Its AI text classifier correctly flagged 26 percent of AI-written text on a challenge set while mislabeling 9 percent of human text, and the page states that the classifier was withdrawn on July 20, 2023 due to its low rate of accuracy. Detection has improved since, but the underlying difficulty has not gone away.

Most detectors are classifiers trained on large collections of human-written and machine-generated text. They look for statistical patterns, such as how predictable the word choices are or how uniform the sentence structure is, and output a probability. Some systems add features such as sentence-level scoring, writing-process data, or checks for hidden provenance signals.

• Statistical signals. Text that a language model finds highly predictable tends to look machine-like, which is why formulaic human writing can be misflagged.

• Trained classifiers. Detectors learn from labeled examples and update as new models appear.

• Writing-process evidence. Tools such as Writing Replay record how a document was typed or pasted, which is stronger evidence than a style score.

• Provenance. Standards such as C2PA Content Credentials aim to record the origin and edits of content when the creating tool supports them, which is different from guessing after the fact.

Because vendor figures are not comparable, the most reliable approach is to measure. Build a small labeled set that reflects your real content, including human writing from different groups of authors, and calculate false-positive and false-negative rates by group. The script below wraps any detector behind a single function. The stub is a placeholder, so replace it with a call to the tool you are evaluating and follow that vendor’s API documentation.

is_ai: bool        # ground truth you control

group: str = "all" # e.g. "native", "non-native", "technical"

def evaluate(detect, samples, threshold=0.5):

"""detect(text) -> probability the text is AI-generated (0..1).

Wrap any vendor API or tool export in this function."""

g = stats.setdefault(s.group, {"fp": 0, "human": 0, "fn": 0, "ai": 0})

g["fn"] += not flagged          # AI text that slipped through

g["fp"] += flagged              # human text wrongly flagged

fpr = g["fp"] / g["human"] if g["human"] else float("nan")

fnr = g["fn"] / g["ai"] if g["ai"] else float("nan")

print(f"{group:12} false positive rate {fpr:.1%}  false negative rate {fnr:.1%}")

return 0.9 if "delve" in text.lower() else 0.2

Sample("Let us delve into the topic.", True, "native"),

Sample("I wrote this note by hand.", False, "native"),

Sample("We delve into methods used here.", False, "non-native"),

Sample("The results were reviewed twice.", True, "non-native"),

With only a handful of samples the numbers mean little, so use a larger, representative set and report results per group, because an acceptable overall rate can hide a high rate for one group.

• Never treat a score as proof. Use it to start a conversation, not end one.

• Gather corroborating evidence. Drafts, version history, writing-process data and discussion with the author carry more weight than a percentage.

• Tell people how detection is used. Publish a policy so authors know what to expect and how to appeal.

• Check for bias. Test on writers like those you evaluate, including non-native speakers.

A tool that estimates how likely a piece of text is to have been generated by an AI model, usually as a percentage, often with sentence-level highlighting.

They can be useful, but accuracy depends on the text, length, language and threshold, and every vendor in this guide states that errors can occur. Test on your own samples.

Yes. False positives are acknowledged by vendors and documented in research, particularly for short or formulaic text and some non-native English writing.

Free tiers are fine for a quick check, but they have limits on length and features, and the result should not be the sole basis for a decision.

Sometimes. Paraphrasing and editing can lower scores, and partly AI-generated documents can produce mixed results.

A plagiarism checker looks for matches with existing sources. An AI detector estimates whether text resembles machine-generated writing, which is a different and less certain task.

AI detectors are useful screening tools, but none is a verdict. The six reviewed here differ in language coverage, limits, integrations, explainability and how candidly their pages describe error, and their accuracy figures come from different tests. Compare them on the factors that matter for your content, measure false positives on your own samples, and combine any score with other evidence before acting on it.

#10198. Build Generative UI Apps with A2UI & CopilotKit Agents

Agent-User Interactions are now evolving from text exchanges to rich UIs. Until recently, most conversations looked like this:

User: "Find me a holiday package to Bali in March." Agent: (six packages in prose, each with dates, hotel, flights, price) User: "The five-night one with breakfast." Agent: "Here's the link. You can book it on the operator's site."

Agents could plan the trip, but everything came back as walls of text. They had no standard way to render a selectable list of packages, a map of the destinations, or a date picker to carry you through the rest of the booking, inside the conversation itself.

Generative UI is the layer that lets agents build interfaces at runtime, shaped by what each request needs - giving users a rich, interactive experience. Implementing that takes two pieces: a way to describe the UI and an agentic stack that connects your agent to your application and renders that UI.

Generative UI covers a spectrum of approaches, from using predefined higher order components, to enabling your AI agent to have the flexibility to decide how to build the UI.

At the controlled end, you build each component ahead of time, and the agent picks which one to render and with what data. It's brand-safe but it does not allow for deep customization and personalization based on the user’s intent. At the open end, the agent returns a raw UI program written in HTML, Javascript and/or Dart that renders inside a sandboxed iframe. That covers anything you did not plan for, at the cost of a surface that drifts from your design system and executes code a model wrote.

A2UI works through the entire spectrum. It participates in controlled generation with decision models like Typesafe’s Jev and also open-ended code generation that may output A2UI JSON messages. The declarative mode, where agents assemble pre-declared components and functions, is the most popular. A2UI is the framework-agnostic standard for declaring UI intent, so local or remote agents can generate your UI from your existing component catalog using a common language.

Frontend developers can now ship the building blocks once, and the agent composes them for each request.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

The Basic Catalog ships 18 general-purpose primitives across layout, display, input, and containers. The agent can compose entirely different interfaces from the same 18 primitives, depending on what the request needs.

The Basic Catalog is deliberately sparse, so most production apps define their own custom catalog. A custom component can be anything your frontend already renders, including a live map the agent fills with coordinates while your renderer draws the route.

CopilotKit is built on AG-UI (Agent-User Interaction Protocol), which supports A2UI payload and provides Agent SDKs across many agentic backends.

Your agent emits A2UI operations. AG-UI carries them to the client as typed events. When the user takes any action, the values are sent back along the same stream.

A2UI works with any AG-UI compatible agent. Frameworks like Google ADK, LangChain, Mastra and Strands are all AG-UI compatible, so the setup is identical whichever one your agent runs on.

Setup comes down to two things: connecting your agent to CopilotKit, and defining which components the agent is allowed to use.

Using Google ADK as the example, the ag_ui_adk package wraps any ADK agent and serves it as an AG-UI endpoint on FastAPI:

On the app side, you register that endpoint with the CopilotKit runtime at /api/copilotkit. It's a few lines of standard setup, covered in the Google ADK quickstart, and it needs nothing A2UI-specific.

A2UI itself turns on from the client, where your components already are. Define what the agent is allowed to draw, map each definition to a renderer, and pass the catalog to the CopilotKit provider.

The description goes into the agent's prompt, so the model knows when to reach for FlightCard instead of a generic card. The Zod schema validates the props it sends back.

The A2UI configuration can be set up ahead of time on the server, or it can be created dynamically, shipped from the client to the server with the request. The ADK code above used dynamic configuration which is why it used generate_a2ui without defining it. The docs cover both approaches.

That’s the CopilotKit path. You can also consume the event stream directly with @ag-ui/client and hand the A2UI operations to any compliant renderer: @a2ui/react, or the Flutter, Lit, or Angular equivalent.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Most agent-generated UI is one-shot. The agent draws something, you read it, and it goes stale the moment anything changes.

A2UI surfaces are bi-directional and the components are reactive, which makes multi-step flows possible. Any action the user takes on the interface (picking dates, choosing a flight, entering passenger details) travels back along the same stream, and the agent re-emits the surface from there using those values.

Here is a high-level architecture example of an end-to-end booking flow, with an AG-UI agent on Cloud Run streaming A2UI surfaces to a CopilotKit frontend.

Two things make the round trip work: inputs bound to a data path, and a button carrying an action that names which paths to send back.

Validation runs on the client, so a bad email shows an error inline without a model call. Nothing reaches the agent until the button fires.

Then the client sends the bound values, and the agent re-emits against the same surfaceId. That is how the whole flow lives on one surface: flight search, results, passenger details, and confirmation.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Every AG-UI integration inherets these benefits, so you don't have to build them yourself:

Create a complete A2UI + CopilotKit application. This creates a starter A2UI application with Copilotkit Agent integration.

The CopilotKit Dojo has runnable A2UI demos: fixed server-side schema, dynamic schema against a custom catalog, and an error-recovery example where an invalid first render heals into a valid one.

Explore how to build Generative UI Agents with CopilotKit, or dive into A2UI documentation to learn how to integrate A2UI with any framework of your choice.

#10192. How Much Latency Does a Self-Hosted LLM Gateway Add? Langfuse vs LiteLLM vs AcruxCore

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A self-hosted LLM gateway sits between your app and the model provider, so every call pays for one extra hop. We measured that cost for three open-source gateways: the Langfuse AI Gateway, the LiteLLM proxy and AcruxCore. Each one ran on the same machine, in front of the same OpenAI model. LiteLLM added 61 to 67 ms to every non-streaming call. AcruxCore added 15 to 24 ms. Langfuse's number could not be told apart from zero.

The bigger lesson was about the measurement itself. Before we controlled for it, one OpenAI network address moved our results by more than any gateway did.

Each number is the median time a gateway added to a direct OpenAI call, with its 95% interval in brackets. A gateway can log each call, and logging takes time, so we ran the test three times with three logging levels.

The direct call took 781 to 831 ms at the median, so even LiteLLM's cost is under 9% of a short call. Read the tables this way:

The obvious test is to time 100 calls through the gateway, time 100 direct calls, and compare the two medians. That test gives the wrong answer, for two reasons.

The first reason is noise. OpenAI's own response time moved by hundreds of milliseconds between calls in our runs. The direct call's p95 was 1,047 to 1,233 ms against a median near 800 ms. A gateway that adds 20 ms is hidden inside that spread unless the test removes it.

The second reason is the network path to OpenAI. It does not stay the same between a direct client and a gateway, and that difference can be larger than the gateway itself.

api.openai.com resolves to more than one address. From our network, DNS returned two Cloudflare addresses, and one was much farther away than the other. We measured both with a script that pins one address at a time:

On a connection that is already open, both addresses answer in the same time. On a new connection, the far address adds about 250 ms, because the TCP and TLS handshakes take that much longer.

A gateway keeps its connection to OpenAI open between calls. It pays the setup once and then stays on whichever address it reached first. A client that opens a new connection for each call pays the setup again on every call. How much it pays depends on which address DNS returns that time.

Node's built-in fetch closes an idle connection after about 4 seconds. So a test client that calls OpenAI every few seconds reconnects on almost every call. In our first, uncontrolled run, the Langfuse gateway came out 43 ms faster than calling OpenAI directly. A gateway cannot be faster than the call it forwards. The direct client paid for new connections, and the gateway did not.

The fix has two parts. First, pin api.openai.com to one address on every path. In Docker, that is an extra_hosts entry on each gateway container:

Second, let the direct client keep its connection open, as a gateway does. With undici, the HTTP client behind Node's fetch, that is a custom Agent:

We then listed each container's open connections to confirm that every path used the same address. The edge-check script is in the repository linked at the end, and it takes about a minute to run from your own network.

This advice also applies outside benchmarks. If your app calls OpenAI directly from Node, keep the connection open between calls. Otherwise some of your calls pay the handshake, and on a far address that costs about 250 ms.

Each test sent 150 rounds after 5 warm-up rounds. One round calls OpenAI directly and through each gateway, once without streaming and once with streaming. The order rotates every round, so no path always goes first or last.

For each round, the script takes the gateway's time minus the direct call's time in that same round. Calls in one round are made within a few seconds of each other, so a slow moment at OpenAI hits all four paths together and cancels out of the difference. The script then reports the median of the 150 differences.

The interval comes from a bootstrap. The script draws 150 differences at random, with replacement, and takes their median. It repeats that 2,000 times, and the middle 95% of those medians is the interval. This is a shortened version of the script's function:

An interval that includes zero means the test could not separate the gateway's cost from zero. That is what the Langfuse rows show. It does not prove that the cost is zero.

Every call used OpenAI gpt-6-luna with reasoning_effort: "none", the same short prompt and a cap of 5 output tokens. A short answer keeps the model's own time small, so more of the measured time belongs to the path. Each streamed call measured the time to its first token. Each non-streamed call measured the time to the full response.

All three gateways did real work on every call, which made the comparison fair:

The three logging levels mapped to these settings:

The machine was an AMD Ryzen 9 5900X with 62 GB of RAM, running Fedora Linux and Docker 29.9. All three stacks ran on it at once, and the test was run on 10 November 2026.

LiteLLM added 61 to 67 ms to the full response, but only 9 to 43 ms to the first streamed token. The first token arrives as soon as the model starts to answer, so time spent before that point shows up in both numbers. The larger cost without streaming suggests that most of LiteLLM's extra time is spent after the model has answered. We did not profile LiteLLM to confirm this.

Logging level made no clear difference for any gateway. Adding LiteLLM's Langfuse callback did not raise its cost. AcruxCore's median rose from 15 ms with logging off to 24 ms with full logging, but its intervals overlap across all three runs. AcruxCore's median is 16 to 24 ms above Langfuse's, and those intervals overlap too, so this test does not rank the two of them.

If 60 ms per call matters to you, as in a chat app that makes several model calls per user message, LiteLLM's cost is real and repeatable. Langfuse and AcruxCore stayed near or within the noise. If your calls take several seconds, any of the three costs less than OpenAI's own variation from one call to the next.

Before you trust a gateway number, your own or anyone else's, check two things. Was each gateway compared with a direct call in the same round? And did the direct client keep its connection open on the same OpenAI address as the gateway? Without both, the result measures the network path and not the gateway.

The scripts, Compose files and raw results are on GitHub: scripts/comparison/langfuse-gateway-vs-litellm-vs-acruxcore.

Talha Anwar is a senior AI and full-stack engineer and writes about building and running AI systems.

#10181. Find Orphan Pages and Buried Content: Build an Internal Link Graph for Your Site

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The pages of a site form a graph. Think of it as many nodes connected to each other via edges. Each of the edges are the links between the pages, and the nodes themselves are the pages. Now if you start to visualize this as a graph, you will quickly see that there are multiple ways to traverse this graph and reach a particular endpoint.

A search crawler usually uses one of these pages as a starting point and crawls through all of the pages (especially when pages are not fully covered in a sitemap).

If a page lies very deep from the home page, it means that it's not an important page. It's an indication to the search engine that this is not something that you yourself consider important, and the search engine has a lesser incentive to rank this page over pages that are linked better within your site.

The key metric here is link depth. That is the number of clicks it takes to reach a particular page from your home page.

Another key metric is broken links. If you have too many broken links, it indicates to the search engine that your site is outdated and not well maintained, and the search engine will then not want to rank your site.

The way to identify both these problems is the same: to actually walk through your site, do a crawl of your site outside in, and try to figure out what the link depth is and if there are broken links on your website.

Seed the crawl with the home page and all URLs mentioned in the sitemap. Technically, the sitemap is not required if you're doing a crawl, but going from the sitemap will allow us to detect orphaned pages as well.

Fetch the pages and follow redirects as required. Record when you're following a redirect: we do not let fetch follow it silently, we record each hop and add its target to the crawl. Record HTTP errors as they occur.

Drop URL fragments and tracking parameters. Keep trailing slashes because sometimes there can be mismatches around expectation of trailing slashes in different URLs of a site.

In order to navigate from the home page, we can use BFS. So we know that the first time we reach a page, that is the page's shortest path.

We can cut off the depth over a certain threshold, say 5, to indicate that pages beyond that are too far or too deep to be reached.

Noindex pages will still be crawled. However, we need to make a record of them being not indexed, to reflect that they will not be present in the search engine.

The desired page depth is as close to the home page as possible. Important pages should be close to the home page. The deeper you go from the home page, it signals to the search engine that the page is less important.

A couple of examples of how a deep page is present:

The suggested fix for this is that we link the pages from as close to the home page as possible:

Wasted links are those internal links that do not do the job they're supposed to do. The different types of wasted links:

We have seen here how to look at our own website through the lens of a search engine. You can use some of the code over here to create your own crawler and identify internal linking issues; the full script is in this gist. Alternatively, we can use a free internal link visualizer to identify these issues and focus only on the fixes to improve our site's internal linking.

#10175. Secure cloud storage is the cheaper, safer alternative to keeping files physical

Physical storage and memory are only getting more expensive, but that doesn’t mean we need to store any less. The alternative solution is a secure cloud storage platform, and there’s really only one good option.

That extends throughout every platform it’s available on, including Android, iOS, macOS, Windows, and Linux. On just about any platform, users get access to the pCloud Drive app for dedicated storage and backup. On Android and iOS, you have an additional option to automatically back up photos to free up space on your phone. pCloud Photos also acts as a smart photo gallery within the app, and sharing between other pCloud users and friends is made incredibly simple.

No matter where you access your pCloud Drive, all your files are there – safe and secure. You can store all kinds of files in pCloud, including documents, photos, videos, and even documents containing passwords.

Of course, you also get the option to take security a step further with pCloud Encryption. Your most sensitive files can be stored with an added layer of client-side, zero-knowledge encryption. The key is all yours, and no one else’s.

Even if you already have a hefty backup solution like an in-home NAS, pCloud is still a good option because it makes that NAS that much more useful. It has an official NAS backup feature with support for WebDAV, Rsync, SFTP, and SCP. pCloud simply provides another safety net for your existing backup.

Unlike a lot of cloud storage services, pCloud doesn’t charge on a monthly or even yearly basis. Just like buying physical storage, paying once for what you need is enough. But what makes it a better option than going the traditional route – beyond the skyrocketing storage prices – is the features and security that come with it.

This just so happens to be the best time to choose secure cloud storage, too. pCloud’s Black Friday promotion runs from November 13 to November 28. Starting with the base 500GB lifetime storage plan, pCloud’s one-time price comes down to $159 – 43% off. The 2 TB plan is currently down to $379, while the massive 10GB lifetime plan is 61% off at $1,099. pCloud is also offering a bundled 3-in-1 option that includes 3TB of storage, a lifetime pCloud Pass subscription, and pCloud Encryption for $579.

Secure cloud storage has major advantages, and a one-time subscription for terabytes of storage is rare these days.

#10165. Running an AI Agent on a Local LLM: What I Measured on One GPU

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Running an AI agent on your own GPU sounds simple: point it at Ollama and stop paying per token. I set up OpenClaw, an open-source AI assistant, with a 9B model in Ollama on one RTX 3090 and connected it to Discord. Then I read the Ollama log for every request.

The agent worked. The numbers were not what I expected, though. A one-line question turned into a prompt of more than 18,000 tokens, the first reply took over a minute, and the small model sometimes guessed instead of using its tools.

This article covers what I measured and what each number means if you plan to run an agent on local hardware.

OpenClaw ran in Docker and reached Ollama on the host at http://host.docker.internal:11434. Discord was the chat interface, and OpenClaw's web panel showed the sessions and context use.

This article is about the results, not the installation. The full step-by-step setup is in a separate guide to running OpenClaw in Docker with Ollama and Discord. That guide covers the Compose file, the Discord bot and how to keep the ports off your network.

All timings below come from the Ollama log on the test machine. Ollama loaded the model with a 32,768-token context.

My first message in Discord was short: "Which model are you running on, and what is 17 * 23?" The bot answered correctly: it ran on ollama/qwen3.5:9b, and 17 × 23 is 391.

The Ollama log showed what that question cost. The model processed 18,415 prompt tokens and generated 33 tokens.

The extra tokens come from the agent itself. With every request, OpenClaw sends:

On the RTX 3090, reading that prompt took 10 seconds at 1,838 tokens per second. A slower GPU, or a CPU, needs much longer to read the same prompt.

Prompt tokens per model call, from the Ollama log. Only the first message had to process the whole prompt.

OpenClaw counts tokens with its own estimate, which was higher than the Ollama count. The gateway log estimated the prompt at 24,608 tokens, against a prompt budget of 24,576 tokens for this model. By OpenClaw's own count, the base prompt alone used up the budget before any conversation history.

The web panel showed a similar figure: 68 to 69 percent of the context window was in use for the Discord sessions after only a few messages.

The first message arrived while the model was not loaded in GPU memory. That reply took 1 min 10 s:

Reply times on the test machine. Loading the model took most of the first reply.

The speed-up comes from prompt caching. The system prompt, tool definitions and workspace files at the start of the prompt stay the same between requests. Ollama kept that part cached and processed only the new tokens. The model wrote at 91 to 96 tokens per second in every test.

Ollama's documentation says models stay in memory for 5 minutes by default before they are unloaded. After that, the next message pays the load time again. You can change this with the OLLAMA_KEEP_ALIVE environment variable on the Ollama server, or with the keep_alive parameter per request. A negative value, such as -1, keeps the model loaded. I did not change this setting in the test.

If you run Ollama in Docker, pass the variable with -e OLLAMA_KEEP_ALIVE=-1 or in the environment: section of your Compose file.

Two things used the GPU besides my chat messages.

A heartbeat. OpenClaw's web panel listed one scheduled automation, heartbeat-main, which runs every 30 minutes. The agent wakes on that schedule, so the model gets called even when nobody chats. With Ollama's 5-minute default, the model is unloaded between heartbeats unless someone chatted recently, so a heartbeat most likely starts with a model load. I did not measure this.

An embedding model. During the first message, OpenClaw also called Ollama's /api/embed endpoint twice to index its memory. It used qwen3-embedding:4b, which was already installed on the Ollama server. I did not set OpenClaw to use that model; it picked it on its own.

The two models took this much GPU memory:

That is 11.4 GB for the models alone, almost half of the RTX 3090's 24 GB.

What this means for you: plan GPU memory for the chat model, the embedding model and the context, not the chat model alone. If the GPU also runs other models, the heartbeat can compete with them.

#10160. Cost per task: Using cheap classifiers to route across Gemini models and thinking levels

Per-token API prices keep falling, yet engineering bills for autonomous coding agents keep climbing. Price per million tokens isn't the metric that actually governs production economics anymore. The dominating metric is now cost per successful task: total spend across every loop iteration, tool call, retry and internal thinking pass divided by the number of tasks that pass verification.

When an agent calls a high-thinking reasoning configuration for every micro-decision in its harness, you're paying deep-reasoning prices for questions a lightweight configuration already answers accurately. To prevent this unnecessary overhead, consider adding a lightweight routing layer that can intercept routine decisions before they ever reach an expensive reasoning model.That is the premise behind wiring TypeSafe's Jev classifier in front of Google Antigravity's model calls. Rather than generating text token by token, Jev—a specialized structured-output classifier designed for high-throughput decision pipelines—evaluates structured classification primitives in a single forward pass: a Choice from a list, a numeric Score or a Noul, which returns a 0 to 1 probability for a binary statement.

For full disclosure, Google has no partnership with TypeSafe, and this article is not an endorsement of TypeSafe or its products. Jev is used here as one example of a lightweight classifier, and the same routing pattern can be built with other classifiers, including Google-native ones. The Getting started with discriminative models codelab, for example, runs the same kind of classifier on DiffusionGemma, Google DeepMind's open-weights model, in your own Google Cloud project.

We ran a 50-question, auto-graded, coding-agent benchmark against live Gemini and Jev APIs to test whether a near-free classifier can route each task to the cheapest model that still gets it right. Here's what we learned:

The naive way to ask Jev to route a coding task is a single broad Choice question: simple or complex. When we tested that first on 16 coding-agent tasks, Jev routed nearly everything to simple, even tasks requiring an LRU cache trace or modular exponentiation. TypeSafe's documentation warns against this exact anti-pattern. If a question weighs multiple factors or requires a meta-judgment, decompose it into an atomic check.

When we replaced the broad Choice question with one atomic Noul question, it fixed the separation immediately. The question was: does answering this require performing more than one sequential calculation, simulation step or logical deduction, rather than a single direct lookup, format check or one-step classification?

On those initial 16 tasks, Jev routed 13 of 16 in agreement with our hand-labeled difficulty on the first run and 14 of 16 on the second pass. Many engineering teams ask whether they even need a classifier API when they can write a zero-cost regex and prompt-length heuristic in Python, matching words like sequence, modulo and race condition or escalating any prompt over 35 words.

On the 16 development questions used to write those exact rules, the local heuristic scored 15 of 16, or 93.8%. To test whether that heuristic survived contact with realistic coding-agent traffic, we froze its code without changing a character and added 34 unseen held-out tasks across seven coding-agent buckets: fact extraction, schema validation, short classification, single-hop code checks, state simulation traces, math or logic chains and subtle language-semantics bugs.

On the 34 held-out questions, the frozen heuristic dropped 23.2 percentage points to 70.6%. Realistic simple tasks such as extracting a port from a Kubernetes YAML manifest, a file path from a unified git diff header or a line number from a Python traceback exceeded 35 words and triggered false escalations. Conversely, concise multi-step code traces such as mutable default arguments across calls, Python dictionary key hashing on {1: "a", True: "b", 1.0: "c"} and C short-circuit evaluation lacked the heuristic's hardcoded puzzle words and were falsely routed to the simple path. Jev classified all 34 unseen tasks with 100.0% agreement, 34 of 34, reaching 48 of 50, or 96.0%, across the full suite.

A common mistake when building a fast-path router is assuming that setting a low maxOutputTokens cap on a reasoning model will make it spend less on easy tasks. Our 50-question data shows why that assumption fails.

When we compared gemini-3.8-flash with maxOutputTokens=300 against gemini-3.8-flash with maxOutputTokens=4096 while leaving thinkingConfig at the API default, both configurations spent almost identical thinking tokens on the 27 tasks Jev labeled SIMPLE:

Note: Figures reflect single-run experimental measurements on a sample test suite; actual latencies, token counts, and billed costs will vary based on prompt length, context caching, and production load.

Capping the model's output at 300 tokens didn't save any money on easy tasks. The cap is only a ceiling, and easy answers never came close to it. On the easy tasks, the capped version actually cost slightly more than the uncapped one. All the cap did was break 7 of the 25 hard tasks. On six of them, the model ran out of room while still reasoning and never gave an answer. On the seventh, it squeezed its reasoning to fit, finished on time and got the answer wrong.

So a Jev router that sends easy tasks to the capped model and hard ones to normal Flash got all 50 right. It still cost slightly more than skipping the router and using normal Flash for everything, and it was slower. If your cheap option doesn't actually make the model think less on easy tasks, no router can save you money. Not even a perfect one that costs nothing.

The obvious way to spend less on thinking is to ask the model to think less. So we ran a second round of tests with two lighter options: Flash-Lite, which doesn't think at all by default, and Gemini 3.8 Flash with its thinking level set to low. We added those results to the same results file, matched question by question.

A note on timing. Most of the router's time per task is spent in Flash-Lite and Flash calls, which we measured in the same run and time window as the plain Flash baseline, so the speed comparison between them is fair. Jev's own decision is a small share of the total and comes from the first round. Speed comparisons against default Flash or Pro mix the two rounds, so read those as approximate.

Note: Figures reflect single-run experimental measurements on a sample test suite; actual latencies, token counts, and billed costs will vary based on prompt length, context caching, and production load.

The 7-bucket breakdown shows where gemini-3.5-flash-lite and the heuristic router missed tasks and how Router Jev: Flash-Lite to 3.8 LOW recovered 100% accuracy:

The strongest setup without a router was Gemini 3.8 Flash on low thinking. It solved all 50 tasks. That's the number a router had to beat.

The Jev router beat it. It sends every task to Flash-Lite first and escalates only what Jev flags as complex. It also solved all 50 tasks, and it can help reduce both cost and response time compared with running low thinking on everything. On prompts this short the cost difference per task is small, so for an interactive agent the shorter wait is the bigger benefit.

Why it works. On its own, Flash-Lite answered every simple task in half a second but missed 6 of the 25 hard ones. Jev flagged all 6 as complex with high confidence and escalated them. That brought its cost close to what a perfect router would achieve.

The keyword heuristic was cheaper, but only because it got things wrong. It sent 5 hard tasks to Flash-Lite, and 2 of them failed, for 48 of 50.

Escalating to a heavier setting didn't pay here. Routing hard tasks to Flash on default thinking was cheaper than running default thinking everywhere. It still cost more than running low thinking on everything, which already solved all 50. A heavier escalation target only makes sense when the low setting starts failing.

Limits: one run per setup, 50 prompts with hand-assigned difficulty and simple automatic grading.

Two things in our results only show up once you look past it.

Routing and caching pull in opposite directions. Our test prompts were short, standalone tasks, and on those the Jev router helped reduce both cost and response time. Real coding agents often look different. They send the same big block of context on every turn, such as a map of the repository and the tool definitions. Agent Platform Context Caching stores that block so you pay 75% to 90% less to resend it. The catch is that a cache belongs to one model. If your agent switches between Flash-Lite and Flash from turn to turn, it either keeps two caches or loses the discount. The practical split: route short, self-contained tasks like log parsing or schema checks, and keep long, context-heavy turns on one model.

Match the model to the workload. Gemini 3.1 Pro is built for deep, multi-step reasoning, and it brings that same depth to every request, including short lookups that don't need it. For a workload made mostly of quick checks and extractions, a Flash configuration is the better fit, and Pro is best saved for the tasks that need its reasoning depth. Model configurations can also apply different safety settings to security-related prompts. In our run, the Pro configuration declined two short code-security review prompts that Flash answered. If your agent performs security reviews, test those prompts on the configuration you plan to use.

Other ways to build the router. Jev is one way to get a fast classifier, but not the only one. The Getting started with discriminative models codelab runs the same Choice, Score and Noul questions on DiffusionGemma, Google DeepMind's open-weights model, hosted on a Compute Engine GPU VM or on Cloud Run in your own Google Cloud project. An open-source server exposes the same API in front of it, so the routing code in this article only needs a different endpoint. Our results come from Jev, so measure accuracy and latency on your own tasks before switching, and keep in mind that a self-hosted model is billed by the GPU-hour rather than by the token.

Taken together, the results give three practical rules.

Start with Flash on low thinking before you build a router. It solved all 50 tasks and cost considerably less than the default setting. Lowering the output token limit is not a substitute, because it doesn't reduce how much the model thinks on easy tasks. Set the thinking level directly, with thinkingLevel: "LOW".

Use Jev to route short, standalone tasks. Send simple tasks to Flash-Lite and hard ones to Flash on low thinking. Ask Jev one simple yes-or-no question per factor. In our test, this helped reduce cost and response time compared with running Flash on low thinking for everything, and came close to what a perfect router would achieve. Keep long, context-heavy turns on a single model so caching keeps working. For high-volume warehouse workloads, Jeff Nelson's BigQuery and Cloud Run cascade pattern applies the same idea.

Measure what your agent actually spends. Send your agent's traces and thinking-token counts to Cloud Logging and BigQuery, so you can see which tasks drive your spend and catch truncation or retries that inflate it. The benchmark code will be published after open-source review.

In summary, the most cost-effective way to run an agent is to match each task to the model that fits it. Start with a fast model on a light setting. You'll often find it already gets the job done. Then add a small, almost free classifier like Jev to send only the hard tasks to a stronger model, and ask simple yes-or-no questions rather than one broad one. Judge every option by what it costs per task done right, not per token.

#10157. Best Ecommerce Platforms for Developers: Six Options and the Layers Each Leaves You

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Picking an ecommerce platform is really a decision about which layers you want to own: the storefront, the catalog and order logic, the payment flow, and the hosting underneath. Every platform below draws that line in a different place. If you have already settled the front-end question with a comparison like SitePoint's Astro vs Next.js guide, the next question is what sits behind it. Prices are from each vendor's pricing page as of November 2026.

Shopify is the fully managed option. Basic costs $29 a month with card rates from 2.9% plus 30 cents, and a 2% fee applies to orders processed through a third-party gateway on that plan. Developers get two routes: Liquid themes, or Hydrogen, Shopify's headless toolkit, where your framework owns routing and rendering and Hydrogen supplies the Storefront API client and cart. Templates exist for React Router on Oxygen and for Next.js on Vercel. Plus starts at $2,300 a month.

Whop is a business platform for taking payments and sending payouts, and it sits under a storefront you have already built rather than replacing it. The Checkout element runs in the browser with no server code and no API key on the page. You pass your own order ID as metadata, Whop copies it onto the payment, and your server fulfills from a signed webhook that follows the Standard Webhooks spec. Domestic cards cost 2.7% plus 30 cents with no monthly fee, buyers see payment methods chosen by their location across 195 countries, and the same balance pays out in over 200 countries, including to affiliates or sellers you onboard. Whop's own guide shows how to integrate a checkout API into a Next.js storefront end to end. The trade-off is that there is no catalog or shipping engine; you collect address fields at checkout and trigger fulfillment from the webhook.

WooCommerce is a free plugin on WordPress you host yourself, so you own updates, security, and performance. Its payment gateway, WooPayments, charges US merchants 2.9% plus 30 cents per card transaction, 1.5% more for international cards, 2% for currency conversion, and $25 per dispute. Everything is PHP hooks and filters, which makes it the natural pick for agencies already living in WordPress and the wrong pick for anyone who does not want to run a server.

BigCommerce hosts the back end and lets you bring any front end. Core is $29 a month billed annually, capped at $30,000 in trailing annual sales, and plans auto-upgrade as volume grows. There are no platform fees when you use an embedded payment provider, and a 2% fee on Core if you use another one. Catalyst, its reference storefront, is built on Next.js, React Server Components, and the Storefront GraphQL API, and deploys to Vercel.

Medusa is an open-source commerce framework in Node.js and TypeScript, with an MIT-licensed core and a commercial enterprise edition. SitePoint covered it when it was still a young Shopify alternative; it now has over 37,000 GitHub stars. Self-hosting is free. Medusa Cloud starts at $29 a month for development, $99 for launch, and $299 for scale, with no percentage of sales, and the company says you can export your data and self-host at any point.

Saleor is a GraphQL-native, API-only commerce platform written in Python and released under the BSD-3 license. Self-hosting is free. Saleor Cloud starts at $1,599 a month for up to $200,000 in monthly sales, with 0.8% on orders above that, and $3,999 a month at the next tier. It is built for teams with a dedicated engineering function and a large catalog, which is exactly who should pay that price.

Own only the storefront, and Shopify or BigCommerce will carry the rest. Own everything, and Medusa or Saleor give you a back end you can read. Already on WordPress, and WooCommerce is the obvious fit. Already have a storefront in Next.js or Astro and only need the money to move, and Whop is the shortest path. Whichever you pick, the checkout is where disputes start, so log what SitePoint's friendly fraud guide for developers recommends from day one.

#10151. Your SSL certificates can now last 200 days at most: The real risk isn’t renewal

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Every SSL certificate comes with a limited lifespan. Following the latest update from the CA/Browser Forum, the maximum lifetime of a certificate has fallen from 398 days to 200, and it will drop to 100 days in March 2027 and 47 days in 2029. Renewal is no longer an annual chore. It’s an ongoing task that only stays manageable with automation.

So what does this mean if you already look after sites with SSL certificates, and will it bring drastic changes? For many setups, very little will change. For others, one missed renewal can mean an outage. This article explains what’s changing and what to do if you’re in the second group.

Today, an SSL certificate is valid for 200 days at most. The limit applies when a certificate is issued, so the ones you already hold keep their original expiration date.

Where you feel it depends on how your certificates renew. Sites on managed hosting platforms or behind a content delivery network (CDN) usually renew automatically already, often every 90 days, so the new limit changes little. The pressure lands on setups where someone buys a certificate once a year and installs it by hand. At 47 days, that becomes about 12 renewals a year.

A certificate is a promise from a certificate authority that a key really belongs to a domain, and that promise gets less reliable the longer it runs. If a private key leaks, or a certificate is issued by mistake, it stays usable until it expires. Cutting the lifetime shrinks that window, so a problem that once lingered for over a year is now fixed in weeks.

Shorter lifetimes also make revocation, the process for canceling a bad certificate, far less critical. Revocation has never worked perfectly, but a certificate that expires within weeks matters much less if a cancellation doesn’t reach every browser.

Another reason is about people. A task done once a year is easy to forget and hard to check from outside. Browser makers have pushed for years to move the industry onto automatic renewal, on the logic that a process that runs every few weeks gets tested constantly. If it breaks, you notice quickly instead of discovering it on the day the certificate expires.

For most people, the first effect is simply more work. A certificate that once needed attention every year will soon need it every few weeks. Plus, each renewal is a small chance for something to go wrong. Multiply that by dozens of sites, mail servers, and devices, and the chance of one slipping through grows quickly.

The safety nets are also thinner. If you relied on the renewal emails, nothing is watching your certificates now. Additionally, with a shorter life, there’s less time between this should have renewed and this has expired.

An expired certificate doesn’t make a site slow or dated. It makes it unreachable for most visitors. Chrome, Firefox, and Safari all show a full-page warning that the connection isn’t private, and most people leave instead of clicking through. If the site uses HSTS, a setting that tells browsers to connect only over secure links, there is often no way to click through at all, and visitors are blocked outright until the certificate is fixed.

The damage doesn’t stop at the home page. Checkouts, contact forms, and logins that depend on the secure connection fail along with it, often without an error message that points at the real cause. Other systems break, too, because apps and integrations that call your site over a secure link refuse to connect to an expired certificate.

Nothing about your site has to change for any of this to happen. A date passes, and a site that worked on Friday is down on Monday. In a 47-day lifetime, that date comes around about every six weeks.

Automatic renewal tools do the heavy lifting well. The failures happen around them:

Look at the five failures above and you’ll see what they share. Each one happens quietly. The renewal job ran, or looked like it did, and nobody checked what the server was actually serving afterwards. Automation can tell you it tried. It can’t tell you the result reached your visitors.

What closes that gap is a check that never relies on your server’s own account of events. Site24x7’s SSL/TLS certificate monitoring does exactly that. It reads the certificate installed on your web server from the cloud, the way a visitor’s browser would, and alerts you before it expires, using thresholds you choose. If a renewal fails quietly, you hear about it from the monitor and not from a customer.

For each certificate, it records the issuing authority, the owner, the issue date, the expiration date, and the days remaining, including on servers that host several certificates behind one address. That last number matters more as lifespans shrink. When a certificate lasts 47 days, the days left is the figure that tells you a renewal is overdue, and you can set your warning against it using the quarter-of-the-lifetime rule covered next.

Once a monitor is watching your certificates, the next question is when it should speak up.

Most expiration alerts are set to a fixed number of days: warn at 30 days left and escalate at seven. That works for a certificate that lasts a year and renews with a month to spare. It breaks when the whole certificate lasts for a limited number of days.

Here’s why: a healthy certificate renews with about a third of its life still left. If your alert appears earlier than that, it goes off on certificates that are doing nothing wrong, and soon everyone ignores it. As lifespans shrink, a fixed 30-day alert will start doing exactly that.

The fix is to set the alerts based on lateness, not on how close the expiry date is. Warn when a quarter of the lifetime is left, since a renewal is overdue by then, and escalate when a tenth is left. Because these are shares of the lifetime, they keep working, however short lifespans get.

The next cut lands on March 15, 2027, so a few checks now will save you from scrambling later:

Shorter lifespans won’t break your site, but a missed renewal will, so fix the quiet failures now while a mistake still costs an afternoon to fix, not a Monday morning outage affecting lots of customers.

If you’d like a second pair of eyes on your certificates, you can start a 30-day free trial of Site24x7’s SSL/TLS certificate monitoring.

#10146. Agent Distraction

What is easy for humans to skim past in code, agents see as a call to action. They do not know better, latching on to that which is littered across the files read during the planning and research phase of the coding journey.

As agents' agency through tools and skills grows the ability for side-quests scales proportionally. Obsolete specs, long resolved bugs, and lagging documentation no longer rest in long forgotten corners of the codebase, but rather are unintentionally ingested as context indirecting and misdirecting the next wave of coders - the agents. This indirection is not free: in the best case it only costs tokens, steps, and time - in the worst case it distracts and grounds the agent on falsehoods leading to incorrect logic and decisions during implementation.

Measuring how often agents fall into these traps across millions of monthly sessions is impossible with manual human review - we need an objective signal derivable from the code itself. Inline TODO(bug/...) comments provide the perfect proxy. TODOs tend to be left behind long after the underlying bug is resolved or abandoned, while the linked bug ticket acts as the canonical record. By cross-referencing every TODO an agent encounters against the TODO's bug status and the user's prompt, we can automatically separate valid work from wasted detours at scale.

By leveraging the distributed traces of agent interactions across thousands of engineers and over hundreds of millions of individual agent steps, we quantify the impact of TODO distractions in the codebase and how agents act on them:

Controlling for developer, codebase complexity, task type, model tier, number of files read, initial prompt length, and the step where the bug reference appeared, using a multi-way fixed-effects regression with developer-clustered standard errors, we see that unprompted bug detours add substantial overhead (all stat sig p<0.0001). Per session:

The Obsolete Detour: In the following trajectory we can observe how this drift plays out. During an investigation into an unrelated bug The agent views SurfaceControlTest.java and sees // TODO(bug/XXX)

This bug, while fixed in 2023, remains lingering in the codebase leading to subsequent investigation distraction across Steps with the agent digging into the bug to learn more.

The Eager Agent: In the trajectory the agent encountered a TODO in the bug it was investigating and decided (unprompted) to "fix" the code

The agent executed multiple Code Search and internal knowledge search queries for the bug ultimately resulting in the agent changing the ModelDownloaderV1 code. Later in the trajectory the human challenged the scope creep.

With the model when admitting it was an incorrect increase in scope:

While in both of these cases they were only trivial to minor detours - they were real distractions costing time, tokens, and human intervention.

As part of addressing this finding at scale we created agents that scrubbed the totality of our codebases, finding bug references in the code that were marked as fixed or obsolete for cleanup. That step alone however was not enough to determine if the TODOs in the codebase were load-bearing or not. The agents had to go further looking at the history of the bug, the file, and changes to the codebase, at times spanning years of history. Only in cases where the agent was able to confirm that the TODO and the intent behind the TODO was obsolete did our agent send a change for review to delete it from the codebase. As a result of this multi-layer approach, our todo cleaner has been able to remove thousands of actually obsolete references over the last 6 months exceeding 95% acceptance from human reviewers.

In recap, as we task agents on more and more ambitious problems, "agent friendly codebases" require more than just the code in the hotpath being healthy. The forgotten corners of the codebase now more than ever carry a significant tax levied against agents.

Tooling like presubmit analyzers, entire corpus scanners, and automated coding agents to remediate dead-todos offer out of the box ready to scale solutions to address the tax enabling a healthier and distraction free codebase for agents to thrive.

Projects that once were on the backburner to cleanup lingering debt but deprioritized due to perceived low value need to be reevaluated with the new agents acting at scale on your codebase.

#10141. Logging AI Prompts and Responses in Java Without Leaking Sensitive Data

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Picture this. A customer writes to your support chatbot: "Hi, I'm Asha, my card ending 4111 was charged twice, call me on 98400 12345." Your Java service wraps that message in a prompt, sends it to a model, and logs the whole thing so you can debug it later. Congratulations, you now have a customer's name, phone number and card details sitting in a plain text log file that half the engineering team can open.

This happens more often than people admit. Logging AI calls is genuinely useful, but prompts and responses are some of the most personal text your application ever touches. Here's how to keep the usefulness and drop the risk.

A regular web request is easy to log: URL, status code, time taken. AI calls are messier.

The output changes from run to run. If a user says "your bot gave me a wrong answer yesterday", the only proof of what happened is what you sent and what came back at that moment. You can't just replay it and expect the same result.

Prompts are also built, not typed. The final text is usually a template, a few retrieved document chunks, some chat history and the user's message stitched together. When something looks off, the bug could be in any of those pieces, and only the assembled prompt shows you which one.

Then there's cost. You pay by the token, so without per-call records you can't say which feature is burning money. And quality drifts over time as models get upgraded and templates get edited. Old logs are your only baseline.

So yes, you need these logs. The real question is what goes into them.

Before writing any masking code, agree on what counts as sensitive. In most business apps that means:

Names, emails, phone numbers and home addresses

Card numbers, bank details and government ID numbers

Health, legal or job-related details people mention in free text

Internal material like contract wording, pricing rules or source code

The first three groups follow patterns, so they're easy to catch. The last two don't, and that's where most teams get caught out. Keep that in mind, because it shapes the design below.

The safest sensitive value is the one that never reaches the log. So split what you record into two buckets.

The first is metadata: a request ID, a hashed user or tenant ID, model name, prompt template name and version, token counts, latency, retry count, error type and estimated cost. None of this is personal, and it answers most day-to-day questions.

The second is content, meaning the actual prompt and response text. You only need this when you're investigating quality, and even then you rarely need all of it. A sensible default is to log metadata for every call and masked content for a small sample, maybe five percent, plus every failed call.

Here's a mistake that's easy to make: letting the log appender clean things up afterwards. By then the raw text may already be in a buffer, an async queue or a crash dump. Mask first, then log.

The cleanest way is one small component that every AI log call has to pass through:

public interface PiiMasker {     String mask(String input); }

Nothing else is allowed to write raw prompts. One chokepoint is much easier to review and test than masking sprinkled across fifty classes.

For structured data like emails, cards and tokens, regular expressions do the job well:

public class RegexPiiMasker implements PiiMasker {     private static final Map RULES = new LinkedHashMap<>();     static {         RULES.put(Pattern.compile("[A-Za-z0-9.%+-]+@[A-Za-z0-9.-]+\.[A-Za-z]{2,}"), "[EMAIL]");         RULES.put(Pattern.compile("\b(?:\d[ -]?){13,19}\b"), "[CARD]");         RULES.put(Pattern.compile("\b(?:\+?\d{1,3}[ -]?)?\d{10}\b"), "[PHONE]");         RULES.put(Pattern.compile("(?i)bearer\s+[A-Za-z0-9.~+/=-]+"), "Bearer [TOKEN]");     }     @Override     public String mask(String input) {         if (input == null || input.isBlank()) return input;         String result = input;         for (var rule : RULES.entrySet()) {             result = rule.getKey().matcher(result).replaceAll(rule.getValue());         }         return result;     } }

A few small choices matter here. The patterns are compiled once, not on every call. The order is fixed, because a card number can otherwise get half eaten by the phone rule. And the replacement labels such as [EMAIL] keep the sentence readable, so whoever debugs it later can still follow what the user was asking.

Regex won't catch a name like "Asha Raman" or an address buried mid-sentence. For those you have a few options, and they work best together.

You can run a named-entity recognition model that tags people, places and organisations, then mask anything above a confidence threshold. You can do exact replacement of values you already know, such as the customer's name from the current session. Or, best of all, you can mask at the field level while building the prompt. If your code knows that customer.getEmail() is going into the template, swap in a placeholder right there instead of trying to spot it afterwards. Knowing beats guessing every time.

Plain redaction can remove too much. If the same email shows up five times in a conversation, five identical [EMAIL] tags hide the fact that it was one person.

The fix is consistent pseudonyms. Turn each real value into a short token using a keyed hash, so the same email always becomes something like EMAIL_7f3a. You can still follow the thread, but nobody can read the original from the log. Use an HMAC with a secret from your secrets manager. A plain unkeyed hash of a phone number can be brute-forced in minutes, because there just aren't that many phone numbers.

One warning: if you also store a lookup table to reverse the tokens, that table is now sensitive data too. Most teams are better off with one-way tokens.

Free-text log lines are hard to search and easy to leak through. Write one JSON record per AI call with clearly separate fields:

log.info("ai_call",     kv("requestId", requestId),     kv("model", model),     kv("promptVersion", "support-reply-v4"),     kv("inputTokens", usage.inputTokens()),     kv("outputTokens", usage.outputTokens()),     kv("latencyMs", elapsed),     kv("promptMasked", masker.mask(prompt)),     kv("responseMasked", masker.mask(response)));

Now dashboards for cost and latency never need to touch the text fields at all. You can also keep the two kinds of data for different lengths of time: metadata for a year, masked content for a couple of weeks. Put the request ID into your logging framework's MDC so every line belonging to one call, including retries and tool calls, carries the same ID.

Streaming trips people up. Tokens arrive in tiny pieces, and a card number can be split across two chunks, which means masking each chunk on its own will miss it. Collect the full response in a buffer, mask it once the stream ends, and log that. If the stream dies halfway, run the partial buffer through the same masker.

Tool calls are the other trap. When the model calls one of your Java methods, the arguments and return values are often the most sensitive thing in the whole exchange: order details, account balances, addresses. Treat them as part of the prompt and response. Log the tool name, duration and outcome by default, and log arguments only after masking.

Masking lowers the risk but never removes it, so surround it with some ordinary discipline:

Give content fields a short retention period. Old prompt archives are liabilities.

Limit who can read AI logs. Debugging a model doesn't mean someone needs to read customer messages.

Encrypt log storage and the pipeline that feeds it.

Log the hashed user ID so you can find and delete one person's records when they ask.

Allow verbose logging in development with fake data, and enforce strict settings in production through configuration, not through people remembering.

A masker that quietly stops working is worse than having none, because everyone assumes the logs are clean. Write tests for it:

@Test void masksEmailAndCardNumber() {     String raw = "Contact me at asha@example.com, card 4111 1111 1111 1111";     String safe = new RegexPiiMasker().mask(raw);     assertFalse(safe.contains("asha@example.com"));     assertFalse(safe.contains("4111"));     assertTrue(safe.contains("[EMAIL]"));     assertTrue(safe.contains("[CARD]")); }

Build a small set of messy, realistic samples: numbers with odd spacing, mixed languages, addresses inside sentences, text pasted from spreadsheets. Run them in your CI pipeline, and add a new case every time a leak turns up. A scheduled job that scans stored logs for patterns that should never appear makes a good safety net too.

A few I'd watch for. Logging raw text at debug level and assuming production never enables it; debug flags get switched on during incidents, so mask at every level. Masking only the user's message while forgetting retrieved documents, system prompts and tool results. Letting SDK exceptions carry the full request body into the logs. Leaving wire-level HTTP logging on in production, which bypasses your masker completely. And treating masking as finished once it ships, when every new prompt template can bring new kinds of data.

Every AI call writes a structured metadata record with a request ID

All prompt and response text goes through a single masker first

Streams are masked after assembly, and tool arguments are masked too

Content logging is sampled, short-lived and access-controlled

Tests cover the masker, with a regression case for every past leak

Client libraries aren't dumping raw payloads into the logs

Good logs turn an AI feature from a black box into something you can actually debug, price and improve. The same logs can also expose the people who trusted you with their details. The way through is fairly plain: record metadata for every call, push all content through one well-tested masker, use pseudonyms when you need to follow a conversation, handle streams and tools carefully, and keep retention short. Set that up before launch, not after the first incident, and you get visibility without regret.
