#10130. How to Build a Unified Lead Tracking Pipeline for Web Forms and Phone Calls

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A visitor clicks a search ad, submits a quote request, and calls your sales team ten minutes later. The form integration creates a CRM contact. The phone platform records a call. By Friday, the marketing report shows two leads, while sales remembers one conversation that turned into a deal.

Both systems captured something useful. The problem is that neither record explains how the two interactions belong together.

A unified tracking pipeline gives forms and calls a common event format, preserves their acquisition context, and connects them to CRM outcomes. It also needs to survive a less tidy reality: repeated callbacks, missing campaign data, delayed updates, and a CRM that occasionally refuses requests.

This tutorial builds the core of that pipeline with JavaScript and PostgreSQL. We will capture campaign parameters, define a shared event contract, and persist events alongside the work needed to deliver them. Provider authentication and CRM requests belong in adapters around this core, using the documentation for the services you choose.

Before writing an endpoint, decide what you are counting. A phone call can produce several status callbacks. A person can submit two forms. An existing customer can contact sales about a new purchase.

Use separate identifiers for these different things:

This separation prevents two common mistakes. Deduplicating by call ID alone can discard later status updates. Counting every callback as a lead inflates demand.

For this implementation, each event is unique within a tenant and source. A tenant is the business whose data you are processing; the source identifies a website or provider account. The same provider event ID can therefore appear in two accounts without causing a collision.

Keep the original interactions even after you connect them to one contact. The form and the call are both useful evidence. Your reporting layer decides whether they represent one opportunity.

Campaign parameters are easiest to capture when the visitor first arrives. Waiting until the form submission can lose them if the visitor has already moved to another page.

Google's campaign URL documentation explains the roles of utm_source, utm_medium, and utm_campaign. The following helper reads those fields and a few optional ones, without copying the entire query string:

const params = new URL(url).searchParams;

"utm_source", "utm_medium", "utm_campaign",

return Object.fromEntries(keys.flatMap(key => {

The browser's URLSearchParams interface handles query parsing and decoding. It saves you from writing string-splitting code that breaks on encoded characters.

Run this helper through your site's existing consent and attribution flow. Where storage is permitted, associate the result with an opaque attribution reference in your backend and retain that reference through the visit. A later form submission sends the reference; the server resolves it within the correct tenant and visitor session. The form must still work when attribution storage is unavailable.

Keep first-touch and current-touch records separately if you need both. Overwriting the first campaign every time someone returns makes the eventual report answer a different question.

Treat campaign labels as untrusted input. Visitors can edit query strings and hidden fields. Validate them on the server, escape them when displayed, and never use them as authorization or as the sole evidence for commission payments. Avoid putting names, email addresses, or other personal information in UTM values.

Missing tags also need careful handling. An empty UTM object only tells you that no accepted UTM values were captured. Classifying a visit as direct, organic, or referral requires additional evidence and an explicit attribution rule.

Forms carry browser context naturally. Phone calls need a bridge between the number dialed and the marketing interaction that prompted the call.

There are two useful levels of precision. A number assigned to one campaign can identify that campaign. Dynamic number insertion can support a more detailed association by displaying tracking numbers according to a visitor or session mapping. That association depends on the provider's number allocation and expiry rules.

Providers differ in how they implement this, and in what they expose beyond it, such as keyword-level reporting or conversation analysis. If you are still choosing one, this comparison of attribution software with call tracking is a useful starting point. At the integration boundary, check which event identifiers, attribution fields, delivery mechanisms, and authentication options your configured product exposes.

Keep that provider-specific work in an adapter. Its job is to turn an authenticated notification into your event contract and resolve any attribution reference supported by the provider's data. The application schema below is an internal contract; each provider adapter translates its documented payload into that contract.

Store the basis of a match with the attribution record. For example, session_match, campaign_number, and unknown make different levels of evidence visible to reporting. If a number has been reassigned, use the mapping valid when the call occurred, not whichever mapping happens to be current when the callback arrives. If the evidence is ambiguous, leave the association unresolved.

Caller ID is a separate issue. A shared office number, a withheld number, or an outdated contact record can make automatic identity matching unreliable. A confirmed CRM contact ID or an authenticated account provides a stronger link. Treat a matching phone number as a candidate for reconciliation rather than proof that two records belong to the same person.

A shared event envelope lets the rest of your application work with a small, predictable vocabulary. Here is an illustrative call event:

"occurred_at": "2026-11-06T10:14:00.000Z",

The event ID identifies this occurrence. The interaction ID groups it with other events for the same call. contact_ref can remain empty until you have enough evidence to connect the interaction to a contact.

Keep contact details in a controlled contact store and campaign details in an attribution store. The references above point to those records; they are not credentials and must not grant access on their own.

The following factory checks the shared envelope. Each adapter still needs its own validation for provider fields and the contents of data.

"form.submitted", "call.started", "call.completed",

export function makeEvent(input, trusted) {

const ids = [trusted.tenantId, trusted.source,

if (ids.some(id => typeof id !== "string" ||

throw new TypeError("Invalid event identifiers");

throw new TypeError("Unsupported event type");

const time = Date.parse(input.occurredAt);

if (typeof input.occurredAt !== "string" ||

new Date(time).toISOString() !== input.occurredAt) {

throw new TypeError("Expected a canonical UTC timestamp");

attribution_ref: input.attributionRef ?? null,

Resolve trusted from server configuration and the authenticated integration. Never let a submitted tenant_id choose whose records a webhook can write. Resolve contact and attribution references within that same tenant.

For a form, issue a submission ID and reuse it when retrying the same submission. Generate a new one when the visitor deliberately starts another inquiry. Preserve the originally accepted timestamp as well. For a phone event, prefer the provider's stable event ID. If the provider documents a sequence number, a call ID plus that sequence can identify an occurrence within a provider account.

Twilio's call resource documentation provides a concrete example: call progress callbacks include a SequenceNumber, and separate HTTP requests can arrive out of order. Preserve that sequence in the adapter. A late “ringing” event should remain in the history without replacing a newer terminal state.

Authenticate before calling this factory. Use the provider's supported validator and the exact request representation it requires. Twilio's webhook security guide describes signature validation, including the importance of the request URL and payload. Other providers may use different mechanisms. Public form endpoints need their own input limits, abuse controls, and appropriate CSRF protection.

Consider a failure between these two operations: the application saves an event, then crashes before scheduling its CRM update. The event exists, but nothing is waiting to deliver it.

A transactional outbox addresses that gap. Save the event and a delivery task in the same database transaction, then let a worker process the task. AWS explains this dual-write problem in its transactional outbox guidance.

Here is a compact schema. received_at records ingestion time, while the envelope retains the event's original occurred_at value.

received_at timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),

PRIMARY KEY (tenant_id, source, event_id)

id bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,

available_at timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),

FOREIGN KEY (tenant_id, source, event_id)

REFERENCES lead_events (tenant_id, source, event_id)

The ingestion function below accepts a pg pool and a normalized event. Install pg in your Node.js project, save the function as persist.mjs, and initialize the pool using your application's database configuration.

export async function persistEvent(pool, event) {

const key = [event.tenant_id, event.source, event.event_id];

const values = [...key, JSON.stringify(event)];

await client.query("BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL READ COMMITTED");

ON CONFLICT (tenant_id, source, event_id) DO NOTHING

WHERE tenant_id = $1 AND source = $2 AND event_id = $3

const error = new Error("Event ID reused with new data");

INSERT INTO crm_outbox (tenant_id, source, event_id)

return { inserted: inserted.rowCount === 1 };

PostgreSQL's INSERT documentation defines the conflict handling used here. The unique key makes duplicate detection hold across application processes. A duplicate with the same normalized payload creates no second task; reuse of the key with different data raises a conflict for investigation.

This depends on immutable event bodies. Do not add a fresh receipt timestamp, random identifier, or current CRM status to the normalized payload on each delivery. Store later enrichment separately or emit a new event. Otherwise an ordinary retry will appear to be a conflicting event.

The function deliberately uses one database client throughout the transaction, as required by the node-postgres transaction documentation. Parameter values are passed separately from SQL, following its parameterized query guidance.

After this function commits, acknowledge the notification using the response expected by the sender. An identical duplicate should also receive a successful acknowledgement. If persistence fails, return the appropriate failure response and rely on the configured retry or reconciliation process. A success response sent before durable storage can turn a temporary database failure into a missing event.

For voice integrations, distinguish an informational status callback from a request asking your application how to handle a live call. They can require different response bodies, as explained in the Twilio webhooks FAQ.

The outbox gives you durable work to process. It does not make a remote CRM operation happen exactly once.

Imagine the CRM saves an update, but the worker crashes before setting processed_at. When the task runs again, the same request may reach the CRM twice. Design for that case explicitly.

Use a stable external identifier for each contact, opportunity, and activity, and check which deduplication guarantees the destination supports. HubSpot's contacts API documentation, for example, describes upserts using unique properties. Support for a contact upsert does not automatically make every associated activity or workflow idempotent. Verify the behavior of each operation you send.

If the destination cannot deduplicate a create request and a timeout leaves its result uncertain, reconcile that operation before repeating it. A local “sent” flag alone cannot prove whether the remote write happened.

The worker should claim due tasks, commit a recoverable lease, and perform network calls outside the database transaction. A lease allows another worker to recover work after a crash. When multiple workers poll the outbox, PostgreSQL's SKIP LOCKED behavior can help claim separate rows, but the lease and its recovery logic still need to be implemented.

Retry transient failures with increasing delays and jitter. Respect the destination's rate-limit instructions. Send persistent validation failures to a review queue with a useful error category, and provide a way to replay them after correction. The schema above is the ingestion foundation; a production worker needs lease fields, failure details, and a retry policy appropriate to its destination.

Also preserve ordering where it changes meaning. If two events update the same contact or opportunity, process them serially or reject stale updates using a stored version. A historical event should not move a won opportunity back to an earlier stage.

Once both channels reach the CRM, connect them to the result the business cares about. A form submission is an inquiry. A completed phone call is a technical outcome. Neither alone establishes that a lead qualified or a purchase happened. If agents record call dispositions, such as whether a call was qualified or transferred to a closer, that field is a richer signal than call completion status alone.

Have the CRM or the system that owns the transaction emit explicit outcome events, such as lead.qualified and deal.won. Include the internal opportunity ID and the original outcome time. Model reversals or cancellations explicitly if they affect revenue reporting.

Then choose the unit behind each metric. For a report about opportunities acquired during a particular month, a qualification rate can use qualified opportunities divided by all opportunities in that acquisition cohort. Counting qualification events received during the month would answer a different question and could include older inquiries.

Deduplicate opportunities before calculating channel totals. A person who submits a form and calls about the same purchase should retain both interactions, while the opportunity receives credit according to your chosen attribution rule. Label that rule in the report so first-touch and last-touch numbers are not compared as though they measure the same thing.

Measure data quality alongside performance. Track unresolved attribution, duplicate deliveries, ingestion failures, and the age of the oldest pending outbox task. Once several sources and destinations feed the same reports, a data observability platform can watch these signals across the whole pipeline instead of relying on checks inside a single worker. An apparently quiet campaign may actually have a broken integration.

Operational logs should contain enough context to investigate failures without becoming another customer database. Use internal references and error categories, and avoid dumping webhook bodies into logs. The OWASP Logging Cheat Sheet provides guidance on excluding or protecting sensitive values.

A successful form submission proves little about retries or missing data. Before connecting production traffic, test the cases that could distort reporting or lose an inquiry.

Run database cases against PostgreSQL with separate connections so the tests exercise actual constraints and transaction behavior. Use the provider's test tools or signed fixtures for webhook authentication. Test CRM retries against a sandbox with the destination's real duplicate-handling rules.

Start rollout with one form and one phone source. Trace a known inquiry from its captured campaign through its interactions to its CRM outcome. Reconcile event totals against the source systems over the same time window, allowing for delivery delays. Expand once that path is explainable and failures are visible.

The same adapter pattern extends to inquiries that start offline, such as leads collected at a trade show and exported as a CSV or XLSX file. Import each row as a form.submitted event with the event as its source, a stable row identifier as event_id, and the original capture time as occurred_at. Because the outbox treats an identical event as a duplicate, re-importing the same export won’t create a second CRM task.

What is a unified lead tracking pipeline?

It is a workflow that converts inquiries from different channels into a shared data model, preserves their acquisition context, and connects them to business outcomes. Forms and calls keep their own interaction records, while contacts and opportunities provide the relationships needed for reporting.

Can I connect a phone call to a website visit?

Sometimes. Dynamic number insertion can provide a session or visitor association when the provider's mapping is available and valid. A campaign-specific number usually supports campaign-level attribution. If the caller uses a saved number, switches devices, or calls after a mapping expires, the available evidence may be less precise.

Handle duplicate delivery and identity resolution separately. Stable event IDs and a database constraint prevent repeated notifications from producing repeated work. Connecting several interactions to one contact or opportunity requires a separate matching policy, with ambiguous cases left unresolved until there is enough evidence.

Should a completed call count as a conversion?

Only if that is the conversion your report explicitly measures. A completed call does not establish that a prospect qualified or bought something. Keep technical call status separate from CRM outcomes and use the latter when reporting qualified demand or revenue.

Do I need a message broker for this architecture?

You can begin with PostgreSQL and an outbox worker if that setup meets your throughput and latency needs. A broker becomes useful when several consumers need independent delivery, or when buffering and scale justify the additional operations. In either case, retries still require idempotent consumers.

What happens when tracking consent is unavailable?

Keep the inquiry flow usable and apply your site's existing consent rules to optional attribution collection and storage. Accept permitted form or call data, leave unavailable attribution empty, and avoid reconstructing a visitor identity through fingerprinting. Reports should expose that missing context instead of inventing a campaign match.

#10127. What’s !important #18: , Syntax ::highlight()ing, named-feature(), and More

Well, that’s a wrap. No, not a flex-wrap, but rather today marks a new day, week, month, season, aaaand new edition of What’s !important (#18), bringing you the best content that CSS developers have produced over the last couple of weeks or so.

Abhishek Jakhar explained why tooltips should be delayed then instant (basically, so that we don’t trigger them accidentally, and then when we don’t want them anymore, so that they disappear immediately). Abhishek showed us how to implement all of that, too. One thing that I’d like to point out is that if the hover-able region is too small, the tooltip can be de-triggered unintentionally, so I’d keep the delay in that case.

Chris Coyier then responded to that with a modern implementation that utilizes interest invokers, which come with two key CSS properties: interest-delay-start and interest-delay-end. Only Chrome supports interest invokers at the moment, but progressive enhancement, right?

Speaking of progressive enhancement, the Geolocation API has always been complicated because of all of the external hardware involved (GPS satellites, Wi-Fi networks, cellular towers, etc.) and how frustrating it can be to change the permission status later. fixes a lot of that but introduces a few new quirks. For example, there are some…styling restrictions.

And since only Chrome supports it, I explained how to use alongside the current Geolocation API, which means that you can use it today, essentially.

Dave Rupert made a small syntax highlighter called MicroLighter using the new Custom Highlight API. He also showed us how to use MicroLighter, and Geoff talked a bit about it too.

Temani Afif demonstrated how to get CSS grid information into CSS custom properties. Specifically, how to get the number of rows and columns, as well as the coordinates (x/y indexes) of each cell. The only condition is that the columns must have equal widths, but with the way that CSS has been evolving lately, that could definitely change.

Temani also showed us how we can use this to determine hover proximity, which is very cool:

Lea Verou says two-state, but Bramus says tri-state — what do you say? Personally, I like the auto-until-overridden two-state approach, as described by Vale.Rocks:

Bramus also introduced the class prefix selector, which, even though it isn’t supported anywhere yet, is very simple and very useful (as you can see below), so I’m expecting a quick turnaround on this one.

This is cleaner and more performant than attribute selectors:

Here’s what Geoff had to say about the class prefix selector.

Bramus (who is kind of on a roll here, to be honest), also introduced the @supports named-feature() function. This one’s supported by Chrome though, and basically queries support for very specific things that would otherwise be impossible to query, such as whether the browser respects transforms when anchor positioning elements, but hopefully it makes @supports more capable all-round.

What’s also new to Chrome is flex-wrap: balance, which is basically text-wrap: balance but for flex items. Ahmad Shadeed explained what flex-wrap: balance does and what we can use it for.

#10122. What is Pathways? Single-controller training for large models

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Today, we announce the General Availability of Pathways on Google Cloud Platform. In this post, you will learn about Pathways, the distributed system Google built to train its own large models, including Gemini. We discuss how Pathways addresses the coordination challenge by introducing one client that describes the whole computation as a graph, and how the distributed runtime places it, moves the tensors, and keeps the accelerators busy.

Most large-model training runs as Single Program, Multiple Data (SPMD) programs. Every accelerator runs the same program in lockstep and coordinates through collectives like AllReduce.

It works well when your job is the same program running on every device. It struggles when the job is no longer uniform, like a pipeline whose stages wait on each other or an RL loop where a sampler, a reward model, and a trainer are different programs that have to overlap.

Large runs also have to survive hardware failures, which interrupted Gemini 2.5 training multiple times per hour. In SPMD, one lost machine stops every process and sends the run back to its last checkpoint. In each case, the challenge is coordination. You have to decide what runs where and in what order, how tensors move between devices, and how the job keeps going when a machine drops out.

In this post of the series “Pathways on Google Cloud”, you will learn about Pathways, the distributed system Google built to train its own large models, including Gemini. The post discusses how Pathways addresses the coordination challenge by introducing one client that describes the whole computation as a graph, and how the distributed runtime places it, moves the tensors, and keeps the accelerators busy.

By the end, you'll know where SPMD is suboptimal, what single-controller execution changes, and how Pathways coordinates a whole training job across a cluster.

If you train PyTorch models, you almost certainly use Distributed Data Parallel (DDP) or Fully Sharded Data Parallel (FSDP). DDP runs a copy of your script on every accelerator and averages gradients with a collective operation such as AllReduce. FSDP shards parameters, gradients, and optimizer states across workers so that the model fits at all.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

DDP and FSDP both follow the Single Program, Multiple Data (SPMD) execution model. Every process runs the same copy of your script, owns its accelerators for the whole run, and moves in lockstep with the others, because the only way they coordinate is through collectives like AllReduce. Inside one tightly connected group of accelerators those collectives run over a fast interconnect and are cheap. Scale the job across groups and the same collectives cross the datacenter network, which is far slower. On a TPU v5p, for example, each chip gets 1,200 GB/s of bidirectional chip-to-chip bandwidth inside its group, but only 50 Gb/s (gigabits, about 6 GB/s) to the datacenter network which is roughly two orders of magnitude less.

And because every step blocks on its collectives, that communication lands directly in step time. The more groups you span, the larger the share of each step spent moving data, so throughput grows slower than the number of accelerators you add.

As long as your training job is one program that fills the hardware you give it, this execution model fits well. You write your model as if there were one device, and the collectives do the rest. But fitting a model into memory is only part of the problem. Keeping every accelerator busy is the harder part, and this is where the SPMD model starts to show its limits.

Take pipeline parallelism as an example. You split a model into blocks across devices, and each device now runs different code over smaller micro-batches. Activations flow forward, activation gradients flow back, and while device 0 works on the first micro-batch, devices 1 and 2 wait. Apart from the idle accelerators you keep paying for, this drops utilization and throughput, even though every device has work in the steady state.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Another example is RL post-training for an LLM. You have a sampler that generates rollouts, a reward model that scores them, and a trainer that updates the policy and sends the new weights back to the sampler. These are three different programs that should overlap; otherwise the sampler is idle while the trainer runs, and the trainer is idle while the sampler generates.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

In both scenarios, the job is now several different programs running on different devices, and their dependencies can no longer be managed through a single collective. You now have to coordinate which devices share which tensors, which processes run while others are still finishing, and how early to enqueue the next buffer so an accelerator never waits for input. Because each worker only has visibility into its local devices and data, no individual process can manage all of these dependencies itself. That has several consequences.

First, because each process owns its accelerators for the whole run and every collective blocks until all devices finish, one failed machine prevents the collective from completing and stops the entire job, forcing a restart from the last checkpoint. You cannot add devices mid-run, and you cannot give devices back.

Second, because each process holds its accelerators from start to finish, capacity can't move to where it's needed. Within one job, a stage that needs fewer chips than another still keeps the extra ones. They sit idle, you keep paying for them, and no other part of the job can use them until the run ends. Across a team, the same happens at cluster scale. Every job owns its accelerators for as long as it runs, so sharing a cluster means splitting it into per-job allocations, and each new job brings up its own processes on its own hosts.

Finally, the development process itself gets harder. On one host you iterate fast because you run the script, drop into a Python shell, set a breakpoint, and debug. This is not true once the job spans many hosts. You need a launcher to push your script to every host, you hit coordination bugs that never showed up on one machine, and there's no single process to break into. When something hangs, each worker is stuck at a collective waiting for the others, with no one place that shows the whole job.

So the common challenge here is the coordination itself. As soon as the job stops being one program in lockstep, you have to decide what runs where, in what order, and how tensors get from one device to the next.

One way to describe this is as a graph, where each block is a node, each tensor that moves between blocks is an edge, and the devices are where the nodes are placed. You can wire that graph up by hand in SPMD, one process at a time. But what if a single client declared that graph, including what each block does, what it needs, and where it runs, and a distributed runtime handled the transfers, dependencies, and ordering along its edges?

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

That is the idea behind Pathways, the distributed system Google built to train its own largest models, including Gemini. Pathways runs JAX programs on TPUs and it is available on Google Cloud.

With Pathways, you define the end-to-end computation from a single Python client, without writing custom coordination code, and a distributed runtime executes it. That client is decoupled from the hardware. It runs on an ordinary CPU machine and you write your program against virtual devices; a resource manager maps them onto real chips, and a worker on each accelerator host runs the compiled programs it receives.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Because your code refers to virtual devices rather than physical ones, the runtime is flexible to allow users to change the devices used for a given computation. This allows you to stay in control while the runtime handles the mapping to hardware. In an RL loop, for example, the trainer and the samplers rarely want the same number of chips, so you can give the sampler stage more chips by asking for more virtual devices.

Also, the same capability helps when a host fails or the hardware isn't ready yet. If a host dies, the resource manager remaps the work onto healthy chips and the run keeps going instead of relaunching the whole job, which is the basis for elastic training. And because the program targets virtual devices, compilation can start before the physical chips are provisioned, so setup overlaps with provisioning instead of waiting on it.

The resource manager owning the chips also means one Pathways cluster can serve several clients, so researchers on a team can share its capacity. With the Shared Pathways Service, the cluster stays up as a long running service, which lets you develop interactively on TPUs hosted on GKE. Each client connects on demand from a laptop or VM, gets its own slices (groups of tightly connected TPU chips) from the shared pool, and hands them back when it disconnects. When every chip is busy, the next workload waits until capacity frees up.

Take the three-block model from Figure 2. Block A runs on one group of devices, block B on a second, block C on a third. You declare what each block computes, its target devices, and where its outputs go. Pathways expresses those dependencies as a distributed dataflow graph, in which each block is a compiled SPMD program and each edge is a tensor the runtime has to deliver.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

In a single SPMD program, the framework handles the collectives that keep the devices in sync within a group, with or without Pathways, and your code doesn't change. The difference shows up once the job runs several programs on different groups (Multiple Programs, Multiple Data, MPMD), like these three blocks or the sampler, reward model, and trainer of an RL loop. Then no single process sees both sides (the same limit from before, where each worker only knows its own devices), so getting a tensor from block A to block B is a hand-off you have to coordinate yourself.

Pathways closes that gap. You declare the dependency once, and the runtime moves and reshards the tensor for you, over the fast interconnect within a group and over the datacenter network between groups.

To keep the accelerators busy, Pathways prepares launches ahead of time with futures and parallel asynchronous dispatch. While block A is still computing, block B can't run yet, but its input shapes and dtypes are already known, so the runtime allocates B's buffers and schedules the transfer before A's output even exists. Host-side setup for every block happens up front, and each block launches when its inputs arrive.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

This is also what keeps the single client from becoming a bottleneck. Because dispatch is asynchronous, the coordination work overlaps with the compute already running on the chips, so the client stays off the critical path instead of stalling the accelerators between steps.

In Pathways, the runtime decides execution order when a group has more than one operation ready. In pipelined training, B's devices often hold both the forward pass for an upcoming micro-batch and the backward pass for an earlier one. If each device picked on its own, their collectives would conflict and the group would stall.

Pathways has a central scheduler that puts every program's operations into one consistent order, so operations that must communicate start together instead of deadlocking.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

And because it's still one client, the development loop we mentioned before comes back. It's the same single Python process whether you drive one host or thousands of hosts, so you can drop into a shell, set a breakpoint, and inspect the run as it goes. When something stalls, there's one place that knows what's queued on every device and which dependency hasn't been met, instead of a hang spread across separate processes.

Put together, Pathways separates model building from execution. You choose how the model partitions across hardware using the client. And the runtime handles the transfers, ordering, and scheduling that keep accelerators busy, across the whole cluster rather than just your slice of it.

Described this way, Pathways can start to sound like an orchestrator you already know. If scheduling work, moving tensors, and keeping a whole cluster busy brings Ray to mind, it should. But the two work at different layers.

Ray is an open-source framework for orchestrating Python-native AI workloads across large clusters. It provisions the cluster, schedules your Python code as tasks and actors, and launches your workload across hosts, holding data loading, training, serving, and tuning under one control plane. To Ray, an accelerator is a resource to schedule.

What Ray doesn't change is how your training runs on the chips. The job inside each worker still runs as SPMD. The framework inserts the collectives, and each process sees only its own devices. Ray places and starts that job but it doesn't turn it into a single program that drives the whole cluster.

That is the layer Pathways works at. It changes the execution model underneath, so one client coordinates every chip. Because they sit at different layers, the two are complementary. In principle, Ray can orchestrate the cluster and the pipeline while Pathways handles how the training step executes.

So concretely, Ray's job is about orchestration while Pathways' job is about execution. With Ray, you tie your data, training, serving, and tuning together and spread heterogeneous work across accelerators and clouds. With Pathways, you run one model across many groups of chips, express patterns beyond SPMD, and recover from a failed host instead of relaunching the whole job.

When a training job stops being one uniform program, the challenge becomes coordinating the dependencies between stages, the tensors that cross the network, and the ordering that keeps every accelerator fed.

SPMD requires you to address these dependencies. With Pathways, you describe the computation once, from a single client, as a graph of blocks and the tensors between them. And you have a runtime that places the blocks on virtual devices, moves and reshards the tensors, orders the work across the cluster, and remaps around a failed host.

With Pathways, model building and execution are separated and you reason about the whole cluster instead of one slice of it.

If you are curious to learn more about Pathways, you can take a look at the following resources:

TPU Model Design Considerations and Optimization Guide

Agents are sessions, not requests: size GKE node pools for AI agent sessions

Securing Your Agents: A Hands-On Guide to Agent Anomaly Detection

#10121. Let’s Use the Emergent CSS random() Function in all the Browsers

The creator of the TV show The Good Place wrote a tie-in book about moral philosophy which includes a chapter called “The Luck of the Draw,” discussing how the myth of meritocracy leads people to “underestimate the role that luck has played in their lives.” Given how God seems to play dice with the universe, there is something compelling in the way art imitates life when websites embrace controlled chaos in their designs. The jury is out on whether extreme versions of this nondeterminism such as generative UI are a helpful usage of unpredictable UX. Indeed, when I see the YouTube comments reacting to Google’s upcoming usage of GenUI in search, maybe it’s taking the idea too far down a bad path. But there is still something about the idea of a webpage that exists in a state of subtle flux each time you land on it, the same way you can’t step into the same river twice.

I’m a consultant who often works on short-term, greenfield projects, which provide me with a window into the zeitgeist and the trends companies think are the future. It’s no coincidence that the idea of randomness permeated one of my recent projects. That’s epitomized by a burst of confetti to give the user a sense of excitement when they run a random draw they configured. And like many a UI feature in the corporate world, the simple idea of confetti was subject to several revisions to make every randomized particle align with the client’s brand.

In fact, the requirements became custom enough that we ended up ditching the JavaScript plugin we were using and rolled our own confetti implementation! This illustrates the tension between the conflicting needs for chaos and control in UX, even in a fun feature like random confetti.

Wouldn’t it be nice if we could wield controlled presentational randomness in the presentation layer without leaving CSS?

If unpredictable user experiences are having a moment, it follows that CSS will do its part to make randomized layouts easy to implement. The creators of CSS have always been on a mission to harvest common UI patterns into declarative CSS standards. In keeping with that spirit, we see that in late 2025, Safari became the first browser to support the CSS random() spec, as part of an update that emphasized “letting you solve common use cases with HTML and CSS alone, paving the cowpaths, and reducing the need for JavaScript or third-party frameworks.”

Since then, cool demos and discussions of random() keep popping up. For instance, Schalk Neethling showed us how CSS random() can give us fine-grained control over the infamous confetti effect, and Alvaro Montoro made a strong argument that CSS turns out to be the most suitable language for such tasks. He points out this approach is in line with the Rule of Least Power, which encourages “solving a problem using the least powerful language capable of expressing and solving it.”

Now the bad news: half a year after Safari introduced CSS random(), there isn’t clarity on when it will land in the other browsers. At time of writing, there are signs of life that both Chrome and Firefox have been working on it, but no guarantees about when we will be able to use it outside of the Apple world, even behind a browser flag.

So, it seems currently I can only try the online demos of CSS random() on my work MacBook and not on my PC where I do my personal projects. I am tempted to write my own implementation, but the syntax is surprisingly intricate, mostly because of elaborate random caching and keying semantics, combined with the options for base values and intervals. Even if I could manage to get all those details correct, CSS random() is part of an editor’s draft spec that’s in the “early exploration phase” and “major breaking changes are expected.”

On top of that, from my dive into CSS polyfills in my article on ::nth-letter, we know the whole idea of a CSS polyfill can be a minefield.

With all these obstacles in mind, a person would have to be a special breed of crazy to attempt to polyfill CSS random().

One of the commenters on a neat YouTube demo of the feature marvelled that it’s a “feature that works ONLY IN SAFARI?!? Did the Earth get flipped upside down?” Indeed, I am more accustomed to getting my first opportunity to experience emergent features in Chrome, which means my friends on iPhones often can’t run my experiments.

And yet, in the case of random(), it’s darkly poetic that a feature based on chance appears in an unexpected place where many of us can’t use it. In fact, even Safari users may benefit from my css-random-polyfill package, because Safari updates are tied to the OS, meaning not everyone can upgrade to the latest version of the browser. Besides, we know how much Apple loves it when you hack their stuff to improve compatibility.

Jokes aside, Apple seems serious about the “hackability” and transparency of everything about the open source WebKit engine that powers Safari, and most of the demos I’ve used to test my polyfill are forks of demos from the WebKit blog, in which the Apple Safari team showed off the possibilities for CSS random() back when it was in Safari preview.

Here’s my cross-browser version of the first demo from the Safari team’s article. It’s a randomly scattered field of stars fading in and out at random intervals. The larger, four-pointed stars all tilt at the same randomly selected angle. All stars have subtle, randomly hued shadows around them.

To migrate the Safari-only original to a version that works in Chrome and Firefox, we need to change the HTML to reference my polyfill script and add the randomized marker class to all elements that we want to polyfill.

As for the CSS, unlike my :nth-letter polyfill which uses a nonstandard selector that has to be translated into valid CSS at runtime — and introduces drawbacks in the process — this time we need to support a new function in CSS instead of a new selector. It turns out the CSS we can use in this situation is technically valid, even in browsers that have never heard of CSS random(). More later on why it is valid, but for now, just notice that anywhere we want a random value, we store it in an intermediate custom property, and we always have to follow the convention that the property name starts with the prefix --random.

This starfield demo showcases a few different variations of the supported random() syntax, such as the optional third argument for specifying a step interval which, in this case, is used to randomly select only whole number values within the range:

…and the element-shared base value, which we use here to tilt every four-pointed star by the same randomly selected angle.

Note: In the original starfield demo, most of the random values were used inline, which is admittedly more elegant. The spec that includes random() makes it clear that this kind of function “can be used in place of any part of any property’s value,” just like calc() or min(). So, by requiring extra ceremony and conventions, the polyfill is supporting a subset of what we will get with native random(). To see the glass half-full, it means the CSS stays compatible with the native implementation: we could delete the script reference to the polyfill once native support goes baseline and our code will still work, like it does today when it detects native support in Safari. in this case the polyfill does not process random() calls at all and it lets Safari do all the work. This is a compromise I can live with, especially if the alternative is to press our noses against the glass of Safari-only demos on YouTube and make comments such as one viewer did: “Can’t wait to use this in prod in 4 years.”

Chris Coyier said of the original starfield demo from Apple that he found it “pretty darn compelling!” I agree, and when I was testing my polyfill, that demo was fun to watch randomly twinkling, refresh and see the stars scatter differently using an emergent, declarative CSS standard. By contrast, I can’t say I have ever sat around wishing I could create a 100×100 CSS grid with randomly multicolored cells, so this example from the Safari team feels a bit like a contrived excuse to randomize something. However, it did help me test the polyfill support of a few different variations of the syntax.

The polyfill allows for some flexible syntax. You can see that references to custom properties passed to the random() function get substituted as expected, and you can see that inlining multiple random() calls in the same value works. For example, we can create a grid-area shorthand property value with randomized row-start and column-start values.

This example is from Tim Nguyen from the Safari team. To continue the themes of chance and synchronicity, I’ll mention that I had the good fortune to meet Tim last year when I spoke at Web Directions 2025. My talk came right after his talk, and now that I’m forking his CSS random() demo to create a cross-browser version, he is once again a tough act to follow.

You can see in this example that the final random position of the wheel uses a different unit for its step interval parameter than for the minimum and maximum parameters.

The mix of types is supported because the specs say the values must be “resolvable to the same data type,” so we are able to mix units as long as they are in the same “overall data type,” such as turn and deg, familiar from the way CSS calc() adds values with different units when it makes sense, using CSS typed arithmetic.

Note: To make the demo work with the polyfill, I had to define the variable in a CSS class that will be applied when the polyfill first loads, in contrast to Tim’s original demo which uses the random() function inside a keyframes animation that was applied based on a checkbox hack. That’s because, for now, the polyfill only processes the computed styles that are applied to elements when the page first loads. Since all my tests pass with this implementation, I am leaving it like that for now in the interest of doing the simplest thing that could possibly work. There are ways we could explore to make the polyfill react to dynamic changes to the computed styles and/or the DOM.

Chris Coyier has a knack for writing code that’s either as tricky or as simple as needed to get his point across, and his CodePen “Very basic random() in CSS” is maybe the simplest demo of CSS random() possible, showing three randomly positioned squares with random colors. Below is my cross-browser version, which I also modified to randomize the size of the squares, as a test that my polyfill supports random value sharing using custom keys.

Here is the code I added to make each square have a random height that is equal to its random width:

This reassures that we are supporting the correct syntax. Admittedly, custom keys will be more useful in the real native version, which won’t need the intermediate variables. Since we are using intermediate custom properties, we could just have used one custom property named --side and referenced that for both the height and width values.

Many of the above demos include random colors. That’s achieved by passing random numeric values into CSS color functions such as rgb() or lch(). But if we had a list of specific colors we wanted to randomly choose from, we can’t do that easily, which is why the spec for the CSS values and units module mentions the random-item() function, although no browser currently implements it (except for experimental support in safari preview). If we had this function, we could select a random color or anything else from an arbitrary list of values:

The random-item function takes a mandatory first argument of the type random-caching-options, the same as CSS random(), but then it takes a variable length list of arguments to randomly select from, rather than a minimum and maximum value.

I don’t feel like complicating the polyfill to support a CSS syntax that isn’t implemented in any browser — evidently I only give myself permission to do that once a year. But now that we have a version of CSS random() in Chromium which also supports CSS custom functions and inline conditionals, it’s hard to resist seeing what happens if we combine all these weird and wonderful things into one experiment. It turns out these features together can get us pretty darn close to the functionality we’d get from random-item().

If you’re using a Chromium-based browser, you can see the code in action in this version of the squares demo which sets all three elements to the same color randomly selected from the list.

The implementation of my generic --item custom CSS function takes an --index argument followed by 10 optional arguments. These could be increased to any number of arguments you think will be the realistic maximum size of a collection you would need. Each of the optional arguments is made optional by defaulting it to an empty value, so the caller of the function only needs to pass in the arguments it needs to index. Lastly, the function maps the --index to the argument at that index, because CSS custom functions do not support variable length collections of arguments the way JavaScript functions do.

Sidenote: This generic helper function is interesting, because Temani Afif has demonstrated cool use cases for being able to choose from a list of colors using an --index variable, but the solution he created was specific to the color data type and he freely admits it’s “more of a hack than a CSS feature. So, use it cautiously.” By contrast, the custom function approach will work with a list of any data type, and I wouldn’t describe it as a hack because it’s using CSS standards as intended, albeit emergent standards that aren’t available in all browsers just yet.

Now we have gained confidence in our random() polyfill, you might be curious how it works. Is this a good time to level with you and say I don’t fully know? That’s a very 2026 predicament, but thankfully it’s not because of AI.

As I hinted at the start, my level of eagerness to use new CSS syntax before it’s supported is matched only by my level of laziness to implement and maintain my own version of random(), so I went hunting for an open source JavaScript implementation and was pleasantly surprised it exists!

As you might expect, it’s not designed for the exact purpose I want it for. it’s in an implementation that’s designed to be used at build-time rather than on the client, as a PostCSS plugin. Digging through the source we see that this plugin wraps the MIT-licensed @csstools/css-calc which has no dependencies and isn’t coupled to PostCSS. The Readme for this package says it only implements the older CSS Values and Units Module Level 4, but we see from the commit history that it’s recently had an “update to latest spec” of random() and we see it passing automated tests for the kind of random goodness we have been enjoying in this article.

My main question is how on earth we are going to hook it up to client-side CSS, but it turns out not to be too much custom code:

Let’s translate this code into natural language steps:

Point 4 is a big deal. Interpreting arbitrary custom property values using CSS is the closest we have in present day CSS to an honest-to-goodness documented extension point for the language. Since arbitrary expressions in custom variable values are valid and can be read by JavaScript via the computed styles, this approach has the potential to avoid many of the known downsides of polyfilling CSS such as refetching and rewriting stylesheets, doing our own parsing of CSS, and other fun but dangerous pastimes.

Fittingly, it’s only by good luck that an open source project has already done most of the work we need to be able to run CSS random() in any browser while we wait for native support. A lot of people claim they can’t wait for this feature to be available in more browsers, so it will be interesting to see whether people choose to wait now that a polyfill exists. Seeing Chris Coyier’s reaction to the starfield demo, his enthusiasm was contagious! I had a similar moment when I first got the demo working in other browsers. Let me know if having this polyfill available sparks creativity for your own projects. I definitely have ideas for some more advanced use cases for it, which is what prompted me to polyfill it.

Till next time, happy randomizing from your friendly neighbourhood random guy.

#10119. Securing FastAPI Applications: JWT Authentication and Rate Limiting with Redis

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Building a modern web API is only half the battle. Ensuring it is secure, resilient, and capable of handling malicious traffic is what separates a hobby project from a production-ready service. When building highly scalable applications, we typically lean on stateless architectures. However, features like token revocation and rate limiting require state.

In this guide, we will explore the architectural concepts behind building a secure API using FastAPI, Redis, JSON Web Tokens (JWT), and SlowAPI. We will break down the theory, the tech stack, and the flow of data—without getting bogged down in the code.

Before diving into the architecture, it is crucial to understand the role each tool plays in our security model:

Security starts with how you manage your secrets. The first step in our architectural journey is setting up a configuration file and a local environment file.

Hardcoding secret keys, database URLs, or algorithm types directly into your application is a severe security risk. Instead, we load these from environment variables. The application uses a settings manager to read variables like the Redis host, port, the JWT secret key, the algorithm (typically HS256), and the token expiration time. This ensures that sensitive data stays out of your version control system.

To handle rate limiting and token tracking, we need a connection to Redis. Because FastAPI is inherently asynchronous, we initialize an asynchronous Redis client.

Redis acts as the central state-holder for our API. By using Redis, we ensure that even if our API is running on five different servers behind a load balancer, the rate limits and the token blacklists remain globally synchronized.

Next, we establish the utility functions that handle the cryptographic heavy lifting. This step is divided into two main parts:

Here, we initialize the FastAPI application and attach slowapi.

slowapi needs a way to identify the client making the request. By default, it uses the client's IP address. We attach the limiter to the application state and register an exception handler. This ensures that if a user breaches the rate limit, the API gracefully returns a "429 Too Many Requests" error instead of crashing.

We also set up a dependency that acts as a security guard for our protected routes. This dependency intercepts incoming requests, extracts the token, and decodes it. It then checks Redis to see if the token has been blacklisted (for example, if the user logged out before the token expired). If the token is valid and not blacklisted, the request proceeds; otherwise, a "401 Unauthorized" error is thrown.

The login endpoint is where the magic happens. When a user submits their credentials, the API verifies the username and password. In a production scenario, this involves querying a database; in our example, we verify it against a hardcoded credential.

Once the credentials are validated, the API generates a JWT. To ensure we can invalidate this token later if needed, we store the token in Redis with a time-to-live (TTL) that matches the token's expiration time. Finally, the token is returned to the client, which will store it and send it in the Authorization header of future requests.

Finally, we create a route that requires both authentication and rate limiting.

Before the route's logic even executes, two things happen:

If the client passes both security checks, they are granted access to the highly secure data provided by the route.

To validate this system, you would conceptually walk through several test scenarios. First, you test the login flow with correct and incorrect credentials to ensure tokens are only issued to valid users.

Next, you test the rate limit by rapidly firing requests at the protected route to ensure the "429 Too Many Requests" response triggers. Finally, you test the security guard by trying to access the route with no token, an expired token, and a manually blacklisted token to ensure the API correctly denies access in all scenarios.

Building a secure API requires layering defenses. By combining FastAPI's speed, Redis's fast state management, JWT's stateless authentication, and slowapi's rate-limiting capabilities, you create a system that is both robust and scalable.

While this foundational text explains the core concepts, moving to production requires adding token refresh flows, implementing per-user rate limiting (rather than just IP-based), deploying Redis with high availability, and ensuring your secrets are managed by a secure vault. These practices will ensure your API remains secure as your user base grows.

#10115. CSS-Tricks has a new owner!

This is pretty wild, but let’s get right to it because it’s big news:

CSS-Tricks is an independent site once again!

Just to recap: DigitalOcean purchased CSS-Tricks in 2022 from Chris Coyier, who founded the site in 2007. Chris ran it all the way up to that purchase.

Here we are in 2026, and I’m taking independent ownership of the site.

So, wait, who owns the site? That’d be me. (Wow, never imagined I would get to say that!) Of course, I have a small, awesome team to help with the day-to-day management of the site.

That all said, I do envision that some things will change. In fact, I’m planning that they will change in what I hope are very good ways. Maybe a question-answer thing makes sense here since this is very much an evolving discussion.

Yes, I’ve got the keys to the castle, so to speak. And, even after 12 years of working on the site, I’ll admit I’m a little overwhelmed and nervous about that. I mean, it’s an investment for sure, as you can imagine, and I’m making this my full-time job. At least for a while.

I want to make CSS-Tricks both a real community and a place that’s inclusive of everyone, no matter where they are in their front-end learning journey. That’s going to take some work and time.

My latest IPO launched, and I’m now worth one trillion dollars. Wait, wait. That’s not right.

It’s going to be hard. Advertising will certainly be needed now. But, in order to keep the lights on, I’ll have to approach them differently.

In the pre-DigitalOcean days we sold real ad packages with things like display ads, sponsored posts, newsletter sponsors, and the like. As an independent website, we will need to return to our advertising roots.

So, if you’re in a place looking to advertise, please do reach out!

Right?! Ad revenue can’t be the only thing paying the bills. I have lots of ideas and several plates already spinning. Like, I’ve been neck deep in developing an HTML/CSS course for beginners.

(And you can now support the site by becoming a member — thanks in advance!)

Educational content has been in the dumps lately, right? Again, I’m hoping that CSS-Tricks will be more inclusive, no matter where you or anyone else is at in the learning journey. This place has traditionally been for folks who know what they’re doing. But I’m truly committed to creating more entry points for those getting started, particularly as our field is in the early throes of a growing skills gap.

And yes, I do believe that learning the basics is more important now than ever. But what learning actually looks like is what’s key. Front-end courses are down across the board. It has to be done differently.

There are other things in the works, but I think a course is one of the first things you’ll see.

Oh, and the newsletter. That’s coming back after a way-too-long hiatus.

That’s still very much a thing! We’ll keep our regularly scheduled broadcast going.

But maybe a little less than you’re used to. Those articles are part of the investment I’m making. Expect maybe one or two articles most weeks, at least as the ball starts rolling.

That also means the guest writing form is back in business! We have a nice backlog of articles through the rest of the year, which is great, but also means new submissions will have to wait a bit.

I won’t be able to accept everything that comes in, but I will absolutely read them and follow-up as best I can. Unless it’s a slop submission, of course.

Nah! My goal is to make CSS-Tricks a platform that gives folks a voice to share great ideas. I think we all prefer that over me just using it as my own soapbox. I’ve written plenty for this site the past 12 years. I’m happier (and more comfortable) being behind the curtain and giving others the full spotlight.

I may be writing a bit, developing the site here and there, editing a small stack of article drafts, or helping with sponsorships. I gag at the thought of titles, but publisher hits best for me. My fingers are in all the bowls, but my most important responsibilities are:

The best thing I can possibly do is help folks develop a voice and give them a stage to use it. It’s about community and culture building. Publisher. That’s more my jam.

You totally can. I put a little membership deal together where you can join a members-only community on Discord along with some perks. Or check out the new shop. Or purchase advertising for your company. In the near-future you will be able to purchase the aforementioned course as well. Any little bit helps, of course. I squirm at the thought of asking for money, so there’s no pressure.

Support doesn’t even have to be financial! We all know that content publishers need all the eyeballs we can get in these prompt-filled days. Things like subscribing to the newsletter, sharing articles you like with others, clicking through from your RSS app, and checking out our sponsors are all very, very great ways to support us.

Yeah, me too. This post could go on and on. I’m an open book, though, and I’m happy to answer anything you might have in the comments. I know, so 2015, right?

We've got lots of ways to help you connect with the greatest front-end community in all the land.

#10109. How AI Image-to-Image and Image-to-Video Tools Work, and How to Build a Reliable Workflow

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Creating polished visual content used to require professional equipment, editing skills and hours of manual work. Generative AI has changed that for photographers, designers, marketers and developers who build creative tools, but the results depend heavily on understanding what these systems actually do. Two techniques sit at the center of most workflows: image-to-image generation, which transforms an existing picture, and image-to-video generation, which adds motion to a still.

This article explains how both techniques work, which settings control the outcome, how several kinds of tools differ, and how to assemble a workflow with review steps, including a short code example you can run yourself. It also covers rights, authenticity and the limits of the technology. Feature details come from each vendor’s own pages at the time of writing and change often, so confirm current terms before you plan a production.

Image-to-image resembles text-to-image, but it starts from your picture instead of pure noise. According to the Hugging Face Diffusers image-to-image guide, the initial image is encoded into latent space and noise is added to it. The model then uses the prompt and the noisy latent to predict and remove that noise, and a decoder converts the result back into an image.

A single setting, usually called strength or creativity, decides how much of the original remains. In Diffusers, a higher strength gives the model more freedom, and a value of 1.0 means the starting image is largely ignored. Strength is linked to the number of denoising steps: with 50 inference steps and a strength of 0.8, the pipeline adds noise for 40 steps and then denoises for 40.

The ranges above are rules of thumb, not guarantees. Models and tools respond differently, so test on your own images. The script below, adapted from the Diffusers documentation, sweeps four strength values on one source image so you can compare them side by side.

Running this requires a compatible GPU setup and model downloads, and the checkpoint name and defaults can change between library versions, so check the current documentation.

Plain image-to-image can drift from the layout you wanted. Research such as ControlNet adds spatial control to pretrained text-to-image diffusion models, with conditions such as edges, depth, segmentation and human pose. In practice, this is why many tools offer reference images, sketches, masks and pose guides on top of a prompt: they supply structure that a text prompt cannot describe precisely.

The key advantage is speed of iteration. The main limits are consistency between variations, small details such as hands and text, and the gap between a convincing concept and a usable final asset.

The right tool depends on what you need to control and where the output will be used. The table summarizes what each vendor’s own page states, and “not stated” means the page did not say, not that the feature is absent.

OpenArt’s own page describes an AI image to image generator that accepts JPEG, PNG, WEBP or HEIC files, offers a creativity slider and an optional reference image, and lets you switch between several models. Its FAQ advises uploading only images you own or have rights to use. Adobe’s Firefly page describes generative editing and video tools and states that outputs include Content Credentials, while saying its own models were trained on licensed and public-domain content. These are vendor statements, so read the current terms for the plan you use.

Image-to-video uses a still as the starting point for an animated scene. Most current systems extend diffusion models across time, so that a whole sequence of frames is denoised together. The research paper Video Diffusion Models describes this extension from images to video. The practical consequence is that the first frame strongly shapes what follows, so a clean composition and a clear subject make better clips.

For example, a still of a person walking through a rainy city can be animated with falling rain, reflections and a slow camera move. The motion instruction should describe one clear action and one camera movement rather than a long list.

Kling’s main site announces Kling 4.0 and describes stable dynamic motion with an audio-visual experience, with image-to-video features and an API platform listed. OpenArt’s Kling 4.0 page describes how the model is offered on that platform. According to that page, videos run from 3 to 30 seconds at 720p to 1080p, you can use up to 10 keyframes and up to 15 references, and you can save a character for reuse. The page does not state pricing or generation quotas for the model. Capabilities depend on the platform that provides access and on the settings available there, so check supported inputs, output limits and cost before planning a production.

Generated motion needs checking. Warped limbs, drifting faces, flickering textures and unwanted text are common. A quick review script samples frames and reports basic file properties:

Consider a small business preparing a campaign for a new skincare product. The same steps apply to any visual project.

This process reduces the effort of exploring concepts and makes it easier to test several versions of an advertisement before choosing a direction. It does not remove the need for human review.

They can be, with different priorities. Photographers can explore creative treatments, mood boards and alternative backgrounds without replacing traditional photography. Marketers can develop campaign concepts, create variations of social graphics and test promotional videos. In both cases the value comes from supporting a clear creative objective rather than adopting a tool because it is new.

A technique that transforms an existing image using a prompt, by adding noise to the image and then regenerating it under guidance. A strength or creativity setting controls how much of the original is kept.

Use a lower strength, a reference image or a structure condition such as edges or depth where the tool supports it.

Yes. Image-to-video tools animate a still from a motion description, though complex motion can introduce inconsistencies, so review every clip.

No. Several vendors and platforms offer video generation. Compare supported inputs, length, resolution, cost and licensing against your needs.

It depends on the tool, the plan and the rights you hold in the inputs. Read the current terms and keep records of your sources.

Image-to-image and image-to-video tools give creators a faster way to explore ideas, but they work best when you understand their controls. Strength settings decide how much of the original survives, conditioning methods preserve structure, and the starting frame shapes video. Compare hosted platforms, vendor apps and open-source libraries against your needs, build review and provenance into the workflow, and treat the output as a draft that a person checks before it ships.

#10108. What’s !important #19: CSS Refraction Effects, Scroll State Queries, @custom-media, and More

We’re back! Don’t worry, this isn’t a trick (despite what our name suggests!). However, due to the hiatus, we’re also a little behind. That’s why this issue of What’s !important is absolutely stacked. All killer, no filler. Here’s what you missed.

By the way, before we dive in, Mat Marquis’ highly recommended JavaScript for Everyone course is currently -30% off (code: CSSTRICKSFRIENDS). You don’t want to miss this.

Preethi Sam demonstrated how to create background image refraction effects with CSS. The effects are stunning, can be animated, don’t need very much CSS (no JavaScript), and we don’t need to duplicate the image either.

Brecht De Ruyte demonstrated how to build customizable components using container style queries and advanced attr(), showcasing some incredible interactive demos, including the two below:

Dan Holloran showed us how to use the different scroll-state() queries (scrollable, scrolled, snapped, and stuck). It’s not a long read, which is exactly what you want for a new but simple feature like this.

A quick, no-context CSS snippet to entice you:

Declan Chidlow shared some of the craziest CSS to ever exist, and I mean craziest. Most of it comes from Internet Explorer, but let’s face it, you knew that already. Thankfully, they don’t build them like they used to.

Bramus demonstrated how to animate CSS grids using anchor positioning. The idea is that we anchor content to its corresponding cell, and then as the grid reflows, it’s the content (not the cell) that animates with it.

(But it doesn’t work in Firefox due to what sounds like a bug.)

Chris Coyier talked about a bunch of new HTML features, and honestly, there’s a whole lot in there that you definitely haven’t heard of. Like, I’m certain of it.

Victor Ayomipo contemplated why container query adoption has been so terrible despite them topping CSS wishlists for years. Victor thinks that we’re using them wrong, so he’s here to set the record straight.

Cory LaViska demonstrated how to use @custom-media in addition to container style queries as a fallback.

Also, don’t forget that Declan has an extremely thorough @custom-media deep-dive in our Almanac.

Ahmad Shadeed tackled an interesting topic — detecting overlapping elements (with CSS, of course). If you’re keen on anchor positioning, scroll-driven animations, and again (!), container style queries, then this walkthrough is for you.

I want to know when the decorative element hits the section from the left edge, taking into consideration that the element might have a fluid position or size that changes with the viewport size or container size. Basically, it’s a dynamic problem. It’s not about using a media query to hide the decorative element, I want to hide it upon overlapping.

Maybe container queries are finally picking up?

Patrick Brosset walked us through a proposal for the previewsrc HTML attribute, which would enable us to supply preview images for images that haven’t yet loaded using only HTML. For example, we could supply a blurry preview image. However, I’d rather have something like :loaded/:not(:loaded) so that we can style/transition unloaded images with CSS rather than needing to create extra images.

Temani Afif shared a custom borders demo, where the trick is to declare a transparent border, use border-shape to define its shape, then declare a background in addition to background-clip: border-area so that it only shows up within the border, like this (requires Chrome at the moment):

In a similar manner, Leon Arnott shared this awesome trick for animating the background color of ::selection, something that’s actually forbidden (or so we thought, anyway…):

Today in CSS: We all know you can change the highlight colour of selected text, but it's impossible to animate that colour. Unless… the colour was just a variable inherited from the parent element, using @​property to make it animatable… Hahahahaha… [image or embed]

And that’s it, really. Simply use var(--bg-color) on ::selection or some other visually restrictive element.

Chris Coyier demonstrated how to random()ly animate an element along the edge of another element. There’s a cute doggo, by the way. Probably should’ve led with that.

Polypane explained how we might disable the root scroller accidentally (which is surprisingly easy to do — I’ve definitely done it), what we lose in doing so, and what to do instead.

Also, have you ever completely misunderstood how the root (:root/html) works? This is a solid explainer for that, too.

Speaking of accidents, have you ever tapped on a target twice, apparently in quick succession, and ended up zooming in? Andy Bell revealed that touch-action: manipulation disables the double-tap-to-zoom-in functionality. It isn’t new, but I don’t see many web developers using it. One for the CSS reset, I think?

Chrome and Firefox now ship every two weeks. The inaugural releases were fairly exciting (not smaller, as I assumed they’d be). In addition, Safari 27 (along with iOS, iPadOS, and macOS 27) shipped with some of the same features as Firefox, making them Baseline in the same release window. High fives all around!
